## Preface

---

Mr. Court Councilor Buhle <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle (1763–1821) was a philosopher and historian who famously argued that Freemasonry originated from Rosicrucianism.</note> published a specific work in Göttingen as early as the autumn of 1804 under the title: *Historical-Critical Investigation into the Origin and Principal Fates of the Orders of the Rosicrucians and the Freemasons* <note>original title: "Historisch=kritische Untersuchung über den Ursprung und die vornehmsten Schicksale der Orden der Rosenkreuzer und der Freymaurer"</note>, after having previously—according to the *Göttingen Scholarly Review*—read a Latin treatise <note>Buhle's 1803 Latin lecture to the Royal Society of Sciences in Göttingen was titled "De ortu et antiquitate ordinis masonicis," which translates to "On the Origin and Antiquity of the Masonic Order."</note> on this same subject before the Royal Society there. One should not be surprised that I am only now defending myself against this German publication. I truly only read it a few months ago.

The review of the same in the *Göttingen Review* <note>The "Göttingische gelehrte Anzeigen," founded in 1733, is one of the oldest academic journals in the German-speaking world.</note> from the year 1804, issue no. 141, gave me no desire to even look into it. I am with the ge- <note>The text breaks off mid-word; likely "history" (Geschichte) or "society" (Gesellschaft).</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author justifies their delayed response to a work by scholar Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle concerning the origins of the Rosicrucians and Freemasons. The author explains they only recently encountered the full text of Buhle's 1804 publication despite its earlier academic reviews.</summary>

<keywords>Rosicrucians, Freemasons, Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle, Göttingen Scholarly Review, Royal Society of Sciences, 19th-century historiography</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 10, completing the word "history" (Geschichte). The author is asserting their long-standing expertise in the history of secret societies to contrast it with the work of Mr. Buhle.</meta>

-history of Rosicrucianism and Freemasonry is not something I have known only since yesterday. I have—I may say so without boastfulness—perhaps read and investigated more on these subjects for a long time than anyone else. Since I had occupied myself with research of this kind so long and so extensively that almost everything achieved in it until now must have been known to me, I could see from the aforementioned review, which appeared in September of the year 1804, that there would probably be nothing new for me to find in Mr. Buhle’s book; thus, I felt no desire to read it. To be sure, I did notice from this review that Mr. Court Councilor Buhle <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle</note> had, in his book, claimed as his own discovery certain things that were first discovered by me in the two volumes of my *Attempt at a History of the Knights Templar* <note>original: "Versuchs über die Geschichte der Tempelherren"; this work by Friedrich Nicolai was published in 1782, exactly 23 years before the 1805 date mentioned here.</note>, which was printed 23 years ago, and in doing so, he had polemicized against me in an ill-mannered way (which the reviewer disapproved of). But even this did not seem worth the effort of spending time reading a book so lacking in instruction. There are so many naughty children in the literary world who beat their wet nurse after they have sucked her milk! Therefore, when in the month of August of the year 1805, Mr. Court Councilor Buh- <note>The text breaks off mid-name; it refers to Buhle.</note>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page II, completing the name of Mr. Court Councilor Buhle. The author, Friedrich Nicolai, continues to explain his refusal to engage with Buhle’s recent publication on Freemasonry.</meta>

-le <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle</note> several times in person on his journey to Moscow, I did not speak a word with him about this: not about his book, because I had not read it; nor about the rudeness of his attack on me, because it gives me the most unpleasant sensation to see a scholar show himself unworthy of his station.

However, a contributor to the *General German Library* <term>Allgemeine Deutsche Bibliothek; a leading literary journal of the German Enlightenment, edited by Nicolai</term> later wrote to me, asking whether Mr. Buhle’s *Historico-Critical Investigation*, which had been announced as so important, would not be reviewed therein? I replied that from the very beginning, I had allowed almost none of the writings on Freemasonry to be reviewed in the *A.D.B.*, being convinced that all such books had no value for the sciences. He replied to this: “An exception was recently made with Abbot Velthusen’s *Pokeach Iwrim* <note>original: "Pokeach Iwrim" (Hebrew: פקח עורים, "Opening of the Blind"); a 1786 work by Johann Kaspar Velthusen regarding the origins of Freemasonry</note>.” He

believed "Mr. Court Councilor <term>Hofrath; an honorary title for high-ranking officials or distinguished scholars</term> Buhle’s book must also be reviewed, because he after all wanted the history of Freemasonry to be regarded as a scholarly subject." And since my friend had seen from my reply that I had not yet read the book, he added: "I must necessarily read it, for Mr. Buhle has allowed himself attacks against me which I could not aptly leave unanswered." He cited some data.

By this I was moved to read Mr. Court Councilor Buhle’s strange book toward the end of October 1805. I certainly found therein various very ill-mannered jibes against me, which I could, however, easily regard with the contempt they deserved. Only **one passage** (page 313) put me under the necessity not to remain silent regarding Mr. Court Councilor Buhle’s attacks, because he dares to attack my **moral character**.

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai's defense against the scholarly and personal attacks made by Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle. Nicolai is explaining a specific linguistic argument regarding the English word "Mystery" which he had previously published in his essay on the Knights Templar, and which Buhle used as a basis for his accusations.</meta>

I had, in fact, remarked in passing in my essay on the Knights Templar <note>original: *Tempelherren*</note> that in the English language the word *Mystery* means not only secret, but also trade and guild; and that one must therefore carefully consider, in many obscure allusions, which meaning might be intended in each case. Especially when an English text says: Freemasonry <note>original: *Freymaurerey*</note> has been altered by an *occult Mystery* <note>original: "occult Mystery"</note>, these words could not properly be translated into German as "secret secret" <note>original: *geheimes Geheimniß*; Nicolai is pointing out the redundancy of such a translation</note>; rather, one should likely suspect an allusion here to the other meaning of the word <note>Nicolai refers to the distinction between "mystery" (a secret) and the archaic "mistery" (a craft or trade guild), which are spelled identically in many older English texts</note>. Regarding this, Mr. Court Councilor <term>Hofrath; an honorary title for a high-ranking official or advisor</term> Buhle allows himself, on the cited page 313, an accusation against me that is brought in in a completely incomprehensible way, but is in fact outrageous. He wishes to refute my aforementioned remark—certainly as awkwardly as it is in vain, since the double meaning of the word *Mystery* is simply a fact of the English language and was not invented by me. And then he adds:

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s rebuttal of Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle. It concludes a block quote from Buhle’s work begun on the previous page, where Buhle accused Nicolai of "fabricating" a secret history for Freemasonry.</meta>

> “...factory, so that he could gift it with [a secret] which it was supposedly to
> “have had later on.”

I do not know whether Mr. Court Councilor <term>Hofrath; an honorary title for a high-ranking official or advisor</term> Buhle—who reveals himself throughout this book, almost countless times, as a most frivolous and reckless writer—truly reflected on what he was writing here. But what these words must mean to me, and to every honorable man, is plain as day. A man who intends to knowingly argue away the truth in order to substitute it with a falsehood from his own making <note>original: *Fabrik*, literally "factory"; Buhle is accusing Nicolai of manufacturing history like a product</note> is a most contemptible person. One may wish to argue away my knowledge, my scholarship, and my insights; one may pretend that I have erred; to this I can certainly remain silent, especially since a reader who investigates the matter thoroughly can easily discover for himself where the error or the lack of knowledge lies. It is particularly a matter of indifference to me if someone believes they know the history of the origins of Rosicrucianism <note>original: *Rosenkreuzerey*</note> and Freemasonry <note>original: *Freymaurerey*</note> better than I; even many Freemasons, who know only a very little about their own society, believe they know a great deal, even everything. In this matter, I grant everyone, whoever they may be, their own belief—especially those for whom it can be foreseen that they [will have] no...

<summary>Nicolai expresses deep offense at Buhle’s suggestion that he intentionally falsified history, defending his own scholarship on the origins of the Rosicrucians and Freemasons against what he views as Buhle's reckless accusations.</summary>

<keywords>Friedrich Nicolai, Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle, academic integrity, Rosicrucianism, Freemasonry, historical methodology, polemic</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense against the accusations of Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle. Nicolai shifts from defending his scholarship to defending his personal integrity and moral character, specifically addressing Buhle's claim that Nicolai "manufactured" historical secrets.</meta>

...are capable of no further conviction. However, when someone publicly accuses me of having intentionally falsified the history of Masonry <note>original: *Maurerey*; Nicolai uses this and *Freymaurerey* interchangeably</note> in order to introduce a secret of my own fabrication into it; when someone thus directly challenges my love of truth and my sincerity, he attacks me on the basis of my moral character and denies my integrity. This accusation from Mr. Court Councilor <term>Hofrath; an honorary title for a high-ranking official or advisor</term> Buhle is malicious; for he does not say that the secret which I believe to have found in Masonry through my investigations is not the true one—implying that I had simply erred; rather, he explicitly says that I had to argue away the secret which he considers to be the true one, or which is even supposed to be the only true one: specifically to carry out my intention of pushing a secret from my own factory <note>original: *Fabrik*; a repeating metaphor in this text for "manufacturing" or "inventing" history like a commercial product</note> into its place, and indeed not merely to put it into my history of Freemasonry, but to bestow it upon Freemasonry itself. This accusation is doubly malicious, since on the one hand there are so many writers on Freemasonry who concern themselves solely with bestowing upon Freemasonry invented secrets from their own factories, and on the other

<summary>Friedrich Nicolai defends his moral character against Buhle’s accusation that he intentionally falsified the history of Freemasonry to promote his own "manufactured" theories. Nicolai argues that while intellectual errors are acceptable, an attack on his integrity and honesty is a malicious personal strike.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Nicolai, Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle, Freemasonry, historical integrity, polemics, academic honesty, fabrication of history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s rebuttal against Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle. Nicolai is explaining that his previous work was actually intended to purge Freemasonry of the very "manufactured" secrets he is now being accused of inventing.</meta>

...side, especially since I explicitly directed my work, which appeared twenty-three years ago, toward banishing those fraudulently and deceitfully inserted secrets of recent manufacture <note>original: *Fabrik*; continuing Nicolai’s metaphor for "invented" or "mass-produced" historical falsehoods</note>.

Due to this rude behavior of Mr. Buhle toward me, I am compelled to examine his writing more closely. To be sure, I do not undertake to separate the truth from the falsehood within it step by step. Even if one did not already consider the particular nature of a writing on Freemasonry—where the nature of the subject dictates that one cannot and may not express oneself clearly about many things before the general public—the loss of time and the disgust necessarily associated with such work would already deter me. I have already experienced both evils sufficiently on three occasions when, out of love for truth, I exposed the poor quality of the notorious fragments by Knight Zimmermann <term>Ritter Zimmermann; Johann Georg Ritter von Zimmermann (1728–1795), a Swiss physician and philosopher who became a critic of the Enlightenment</term> concerning Frederick II <note>Frederick the Great of Prussia</note> \*), and of the Mirabeau-Mauvillon work on the Prussian monarchy \*\*), as well as the rude attack against

IX

my essay on the Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren; the medieval military order which many 18th-century "fringe" historians claimed were the secret ancestors of Freemasons</term> and its appendix in the *German Mercury* <note>original: *Deutscher Merkur*; one of the most influential literary journals of the Enlightenment, edited by C.M. Wieland</note> from the year 1783 *), [which I] dissected with great labor and care. In these three writings, truth and falsehood are as colorfully tossed together as in Mr. Buhle’s work on Rosicrucianism <term>Rosenkreuzerey; a mysterious esoteric movement which Buhle claimed was the true parent of Freemasonry</term> and Freemasonry. In shedding light on these, I could not escape the disgust felt at the sight of the folly of so many perverse assertions; truly, I could almost regret the time spent on the refutation of even a few of them.

Yet, this was unavoidable if I wished to reject the malicious accusation of Mr. Court Councilor <term>Hofrath; a prestigious title for a high-ranking advisor</term> Buhle. I believe I shall be sufficiently justified in the eyes of reasonable readers if I first lay out the occasion, the intention, and the ultimate fate of my so often misunderstood and unfairly judged essay on the Order of the Knights Templar—along with the two appendices on Freemasonry attached to it—before the impartial public.

VIII

page, since I specifically directed my work, published twenty-three years ago, toward banishing those fraudulently and deceitfully inserted "secrets" of modern manufacture.

Due to this rude <note>original: *unartige*; implying a lack of professional decorum or "naughty" behavior in a scholarly context</note> conduct by Mr. Buhle toward me, I am compelled to examine his writing more closely. I do not, however, intend to separate the truth from the falsehood within it step-by-step. Even if we were not already considering the peculiar nature of a text on Freemasonry <term>Freymaurerey; the fraternal organization which was the subject of intense historical debate and conspiracy theories during the Enlightenment</term>—where the nature of the subject dictates that one cannot and must not speak clearly to the general public about many matters—the loss of time and the disgust necessarily tied to such a task would already hold me back. I have already experienced both of these evils quite enough on three occasions when, out of a love for the truth, [I exposed] the poor quality of the notorious fragments by Knight Zimmermann regarding Frederick II <note>Frederick the Great, King of Prussia (reigned 1740–1786)</note> \*), and the work by Mirabeau and Mauvillon on the Prussian Monarchy \*\*), as well as the rude attack against

IX

my essay on the Knights Templar <term>Tempelherren; the Poor Fellow-Soldiers of Christ and of the Temple of Solomon, a medieval military order that became the subject of numerous 18th-century origin myths regarding Freemasonry</term> and its appendix in the *German Mercury* <note>original: *Deutschen Merkur*; a highly influential literary journal of the German Enlightenment, edited by Christoph Martin Wieland</note> from the year 1783 \*), I analyzed with great effort and care; in which three writings truth and falsehood are thrown together just as chaotically as in Buhle's <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle (1763–1821), a philosopher and historian who argued that Freemasonry evolved from Rosicrucianism</note> writing on Rosicrucianism <term>Rosenkreuzerey; a spiritual and cultural movement that arose in the early 17th century, claiming to possess secret wisdom</term> and Freemasonry, while examining which I also could not escape the disgust felt at the sight of the folly of so many perverse assertions; I truly regret the time spent on the refutation of even a few of them.

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s introductory explanation for his work. He is transitioning from justifying his response to Buhle to explaining his broader goal: purifying the history of Freemasonry from the myths and "Unknown Superiors" that had clouded it since the 1780s.</meta>

X

narrate candidly, and point out the difficulties that are necessarily associated with writing publicly about Freemasonry <term>Freymaurerey; a fraternal organization that became a subject of intense public debate during the Enlightenment regarding its alleged secret origins</term>, such that even I, in my two appendices on the origin of Freemasonry, could not speak clearly enough, and therefore could satisfy only the fewest of readers. If I then, merely in passing, provide some more detailed explanations from my considerable collection of research notes <term>Kollektaneen; from the Latin *collectanea*, meaning a gathered collection of excerpts, notes, and documents used as raw material for a study</term> on the history of Rosicrucianism <term>Rosenkreuzerey; a spiritual and cultural movement that claimed to possess secret esoteric wisdom, which Nicolai viewed as a source of superstition</term> and Freemasonry—a collection I have built since 1783—as much as my current leisure allows, then this will presumably not be unpleasant to sensible readers. 

This is especially true for those sensible Freemasons who would like to see the ugly darnel and weeds—sown among their good seed by worthless Unknown Superiors <term>Unbekannten Obern; a controversial concept in 18th-century "Strict Observance" masonry, referring to secret, high-ranking leaders who supposedly directed the order from the shadows</term>—weeded out. These well-meaning Freemasons will, I hope, thank me for taking the opportunity to interject, as much as I can, a frank word toward uncovering the falsehood of childish legends with which those among the Freemasons have allowed themselves to be deceived for so long. I further aim to strengthen the abhorrence of the deception for which this honorable society so often had to serve, and to speak generally in defense of sound reason <term>Vernunft; the faculty of the mind that allows for logical and empirical understanding, a core value of the Enlightenment</term>; for only sound reason and truth <note>original: "gesunde Vernunft und Wahrheit"</note> can provide us with wisdom, beauty,

<summary>In this page, Nicolai expresses his desire to purge Freemasonry of "childish legends" and the influence of "Unknown Superiors," using his private research to defend Enlightenment reason against deception.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Rosicrucianism, Unknown Superiors, Collectanea, Enlightenment reason, historical truth, Friedrich Nicolai</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s preface, following his previous page where he expressed a desire to weed out "childish legends" and "Unknown Superiors" from the history of Freemasonry. Here, he defends his motivations against critics like Buhle, arguing that his goal is historical clarity, not the creation of new myths.</meta>

XI

...and give strength. Those who might take offense at what I have said from an honest heart regarding legends and abuses, I leave to their own beliefs, with the conviction that the offense was not given, but taken. In all matters that do not disturb the order of civil society, everyone may live according to their own faith. Moreover, I am familiar with the delicate, easily wounded skin of the love of secrecy <term>Geheimnißliebe; a late 18th-century term for a perceived obsession with mysticism, occultism, and the hoarding of "secret" knowledge</term>, which I also did not wish to injure, except where I noticed that a festering mass lay hidden beneath it, which could have become harmful to the nobler parts if the covering had not been opened. <note>Nicolai uses this medical metaphor to justify his "surgical" critique of Masonic myths, suggesting that exposing secrets is a necessary act of healing for the organization.</note>

Meanwhile, I readily admit that through what I note here in passing, the subject is exhausted neither in a literary nor a masonic respect, which could not have been my intention here at all. In any case, I hope that one will be able to judge whether, in what I wrote 23 years ago regarding the history of **Freemasonry** <term>Freymaurerey</term>, it could have been my intention to fabricate a secret, and to present Freemasonry with such a secret from my own factory. <note>Nicolai is defending his 1782 work "Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of Knights Templar," where he argued Freemasonry originated from 17th-century English philosophers rather than ancient mysteries.</note> To bring this to light is actually now my main intention.

As for Mr. Court Councilor Buhle <term>Hofrath Buhle; Johann Gottfried Buhle (1763–1821), a scholar who published a rival theory claiming Freemasonry was merely a branch of Rosicrucianism</term>, it was necessary...

XII

...if the truth were to come to light, it was necessary for me to show, at least through some striking examples, how he goes about his work, and how his book relates to mine—and indeed, how his way of viewing the history of **Rosicrucianism** <term>Rosenkreuzerey; a mystical secret society supposedly founded in the late medieval period, which became a subject of intense historical debate in the 18th century</term> relates to my own. Without such an evaluation of Buhle's work, it would not have become obvious how little trust it deserves, and what liberties Mr. Buhle <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821) was a philosopher and historian who argued that the Rosicrucians were the direct precursors to Freemasonry, a theory Nicolai contested.</note> allows himself. Even if my indignation at his unpardonable carelessness and arrogance first forced the pen into my hand, I hope the attentive reader will nonetheless find that I have never allowed myself to be led beyond the boundaries of impartiality and truth, but have only criticized in his book—(and by no means everything)—that which can be most strictly proven.

I was necessarily obliged to speak at length about several things which may seem like trifles, but in this investigation are not, much as I would have liked to be more concise. It was all the more necessary to present everything here in detail, the more Mr. B. confuses and obscures matters, and the more boldly he wrote whatever he pleased without proof. There are readers who consider precision in literary investigations to be pedantry <term>Pedanterey; here meaning an excessive concern with minor details or rules at the expense of the "big picture"</term>, and cannot comprehend how someone...

<summary>Nicolai defends his lengthy and sharp critique of Johann Gottlieb Buhle's history of the Rosicrucians, arguing that Buhle's lack of evidence and "arrogance" required a detailed, factual correction to preserve historical truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of his rigorous historical method. He is responding to the perceived "pedantry" of his detailed investigation into the Rosicrucians, arguing that even small details are essential for establishing historical truth, especially when correcting the sloppy scholarship of his opponent, Buhle.</meta>

XIII

<sig>* *</sig>

on account of the correction of a small circumstance, which might seem entirely insignificant to them, one can consult twenty books and, for the most part, in vain. After all, people who root through books merely to collect reports without selection might be considered **pedants** <term>Pedanten; a derogatory term for scholars who focus on trivial details without understanding the larger context</term>; but even these, if they combine a **love of truth** <term>Wahrheitsliebe; a central Enlightenment virtue emphasizing intellectual honesty</term> with precision, can sometimes be useful, because by means of their laborious diligence they provide the opportunity for **historical truth** to be brought forth. 

If, however, **historical researchers**—who do not merely collect but seek to evaluate the worth of every historical circumstance with **critical judgment** <term>Beurtheilungskraft; the faculty of discernment or analytical power</term>, yet do not disdain investigating even the smallest circumstances very precisely in a history that has not been sufficiently developed—are convinced that the punctual determination of the truth regarding small details can also often be of great importance for the whole; then these will, one hopes, deserve a better name, especially if the **precision** of their investigations arises only from a pure love of truth. 

The opposite of both is the frivolous chatterer, who, without paying heed to the high value of historical truth and the great difficulty of distinguishing it from false reports—just as one must separate noble metal <note>Nicolai is likely using a metallurgical metaphor here, common in Enlightenment prose, comparing the extraction of historical facts from unreliable sources to the refining of gold or silver from common ore.</note>...

<summary>Nicolai defends the necessity of meticulous detail in historical research, arguing that even "pedantic" accuracy is vital for uncovering the truth and distinguishing it from the "frivolous" claims of less careful writers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s preface. He is concluding a metaphor from the previous page, comparing a poor historian to someone who fails to separate precious metal from worthless rock. XIV

from the barren rock, who mixes falsehood with truth; and he becomes utterly contemptible when he knowingly writes down incorrect reports and arbitrarily devised circumstances as if they were **historical truths** <term>historische Wahrheiten; the Enlightenment ideal of factual accuracy based on evidence rather than tradition or myth</term>, taken from reliable **documents** <term>Urkunden; primary sources or official archival records</term>.

I hold it against no one if they do not wish to read detailed presentations which, through complex **literary investigations** <term>literarische Untersuchungen; in the 18th century, this referred to scholarly research involving the critical analysis of books and manuscripts</term>, lead solely and exclusively to precise determination, and through this to the truth, or at least to the highest possible probability. Therefore, I ask anyone in this position to set this writing of mine aside, for it would only cause them boredom. 

To those, however, who wish to read the following work, I still have this to say. Whoever writes with **frankness** <term>Freymüthigkeit; a core value of the Berlin Enlightenment, signifying the courage to speak one's mind openly</term> and at the same time **impartially**, always exposes themselves to a stricter judgment; they can expect no mercy from those who have already taken sides. For this reason, I have often been treated unfairly because of my frankness. Particularly those who were obstructed by my frank remarks about **Freemasonry** and **secret societies** in general <note>Nicolai was a famous critic of the Rosicrucians and certain mystical branches of Freemasonry, which he believed promoted superstition.</note>, have sought to depict me as an ignorant simpleton and a loathsome person for

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of his historical research into secret societies. He is finishing a thought from the previous page regarding those who criticized his work without understanding his methodology.</meta>

XV

...found it good, even if they themselves often did not understand what I was talking about. Whether I deserve their reproaches, let those judge who are knowledgeable and at the same time impartial. Of course, there are perhaps only a few who are both at once. However, even if I am misunderstood, there remains for me at the end of my **literary career** <term>literarische Laufbahn; Nicolai’s long and influential career as a leading publisher, author, and central figure of the Berlin Enlightenment</term> the consciousness that I never treated anyone unfairly, that I never passed myself off as something I am not, and never pretended to know something that I had not investigated to the best of my abilities.

If only justice is done to my **sincerity** and **love of truth**, then it matters little to me whether **Titius or Sempronius** <note>original: "Titius oder Sempronius"; these are traditional Latin placeholder names used in scholarly German texts to refer to "any person" or "so-and-so," similar to "John Doe"</note> believes he knows everything about the history, origin, and progress of **Rosicrucianism** <term>Rosenkreuzerey; a mystical movement that claimed to have secret ancient knowledge, which Nicolai viewed with Enlightenment skepticism</term> and **Freemasonry** better than I, or whether people who actually know next to nothing about it imagine they have won the prize. In the two parts of my **Essay** <note>Refers to Nicolai's 1782 work, *Versuch über die Beschaffenheit der eigentlichen Freymaurergesellschaft* (Essay on the Nature of the Actual Society of Freemasons)</note> published in the year 1782, I broke a new path toward a serious investigation of the subject. It would therefore be no wonder if I had stumbled here and there in such a bold undertaking; and yet, as far as I can see, thus far...

<summary>Friedrich Nicolai concludes his preface by defending his integrity and scholarly rigor against critics of his historical research on Freemasonry and Rosicrucianism. He reflects on his 1782 work as a pioneering effort in the serious, evidence-based study of secret societies.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Nicolai, Freemasonry, Rosicrucianism, Enlightenment scholarship, historiography, sincerity, 1782 Essay</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface from page XV. Friedrich Nicolai is finishing his defense of his research methodology before moving into a specific critique of a rival scholar, Johann Gottlieb Buhle.</meta>

...[stumbled here and there; yet I find the] essential things I researched on this path increasingly confirmed.

But whoever, after having read what I shall remark concerning Mr. Buhle’s writing, can still believe that this author thoroughly studied the subjects he undertook to write about; that he examined them with **historical criticism** <term>historische Kritik; the rigorous academic method of evaluating the authenticity and context of historical documents</term>, a love of truth, a study of the sources, and impartiality; and even, as he boasts, that he has penetrated into the inner heart of **Freemasonry** <term>Freymaurerey; a fraternal organization that was the subject of intense historical debate and conspiracy theories during the Enlightenment</term>; whoever can still believe that Mr. Buhle did not, without knowledge, very boldly pass judgment; that he did not almost constantly only copy from other writings, and even my own, with obvious **plagiarism** <note>original Latin: "Plagium"</note>, and with unforgivable conceit audaciously asserted all sorts of things without rightly considering what he was asserting—such a person I do not envy for their faith.

->Berlin, the 12th of February, 1806.<-

<meta>catchword: Nicolai's Remarks</meta>

Before I proceed to the remarks on the book by Mr. **Court Councilor** <term>Hofrath; a prestigious title for a high-level administrative or legal advisor in the German states</term> Buhle, it will be necessary to say something beforehand about the occasion and purpose of my book on the **Knights Templar** <term>Tempelherren; a wealthy and powerful medieval Catholic military order suppressed in the 14th century</term>, which appeared twenty-three years ago, and about everything connected with it.

Already more than thirty years ago, among many other historical studies, I had come upon the investigation of the history of the Knights Templar and their condemnation. The latter seemed to me a historical problem that was well worth the effort of solving. I read the sources themselves, in every moment of leisure I was granted and as many as I could find, and finally believed I saw light and order in the dark chaos. During this occupation, my astonishment was not small to find that not a single one of all the writers on the Templars had even read the original documents <note>original: "Urkunden"</note>, more of which have been added to every new edition of **Dupuy’s** <note>Pierre Dupuy (1582–1651), a French scholar whose "History of the Condemnation of the Templars" included vital primary source transcripts</note> history, and yet that in these documents there was no small amount contained for the explanation of many facts reported by the old historians.

However, the results of my several years of occasional investigations into the history of the Templars would presumably have appeared much later, or perhaps not publicly at all; for since my youth I have only...

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the introduction where Nicolai explains how he moved from private study to publishing his findings on the Knights Templar and Freemasonry. ...read and studied solely to educate myself, and not for the sake of publishing, as so many do—especially when they believe they have hunted down something that appears new and might grant them prestige. It was only by a very accidental occasion that I came to make my intended essay public; and just as accidentally, an appendix on the origin of the **Freemason society** <term>Freymaurergesellschaft; a fraternal organization whose origins and secret rituals were a subject of intense historical and conspiratorial debate during the Enlightenment</term> was attached to this essay.

During my journey through Germany in the year 1781, I attended a meeting of the **Academy of Sciences** <term>Akademie der Wissenschaften; the Bavarian scholarly society in Munich</term> in that city. This body quite unexpectedly did me the honor of admitting me as a member (one may see my *Travel Descriptions* <note>original abbreviated as "R. B.", referring to Nicolai's multi-volume work *Beschreibung einer Reise durch Deutschland und die Schweiz*</note>, Vol. VI, p. 615). Because of this, I believed I should show my gratitude to the Academy by submitting a treatise. Among several subjects I had before me, I chose the historical question of the nature of the accusations made against the **Order of the Knights Templar**. I was prompted to choose this particular topic because of the history of the Templar Order published not long before by my friend and correspondent, Dr. Anton <note>Karl Gottlob von Anton (1752–1818), a jurist and historian from Görlitz who wrote on the Templars in 1781</note> of Görlitz, which did not satisfy me (*).

That I had been contemplating the materials for this investigation for more than just a day—in which, I may appeal to the evidence, I took everything from the primary sources—is already clear from the fact that I wrote and finished the treatise between the 3rd and 22nd of January, 1782, and did so amidst many business affairs of an entirely different nature. But while I was writing, I sensed that several facts from the **history of heresy** <term>Ketzergeschichte; the historical study of religious sects or beliefs deemed deviant by the Catholic Church</term> of the 14th century, and the resulting free-

bold reflections, which I had no intention of suppressing, would not be fittingly suited to be included in the proceedings of the Academy of an entirely Catholic country <note>At this time, Bavaria was a staunchly Catholic Electorate where Enlightenment thinkers like Nicolai, a Protestant from Berlin, often feared their work would be censored or altered to fit religious orthodoxy.</note> and to be publicly announced by it. Just as accidentally, an appendix on the emergence of the **Freemason society** (²) was added to this historical investigation of the **Knights Templar**. For a long time already, a number of base people had known how to secretly gain influence within Freemasonry. They endeavored, through this in itself innocent society, to cripple the intellect of honest, well-meaning men by instilling in them a trust—as vague as it was blind—in the future knowledge of important secrets by every possible means. read and studied to inform myself, but not to have it printed, as so many do—especially when they believe they have hunted down something that appears new and can give them prestige. During my journey through Germany in the year 1781, I attended a meeting of the local **Academy of Sciences** in Munich. Just as accidentally, an appendix on the origin of the **Freemason society** (²) was added to this historical investigation of the **Order of the Knights Templar**. For a long time now, a number of base individuals had known how to secretly gain influence within **Freemasonry**. They were eager to use this inherently innocent society to cripple the intellects of honest, well-meaning men by instilling in them, through every possible means, a trust as vague as it was blind in the future knowledge of important secrets. Thus, they finally reached the point of secretly spreading the rumor that Freemasonry had **Unknown Superiors** <note>The "Unknown Superiors" (*Unbekannte Obern*) were a central concept in the "Strict Observance," a Masonic rite that claimed to be the direct successor of the Knights Templar. --- Page 32 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues Nicolai’s critique of the corruption within 18th-century Freemasonry. He previously mentioned how "base individuals" began to influence the society; he now describes how they used claims of secret authority to demand absolute obedience from members.</meta>

be rendered; and they actually forced such blind obedience upon many otherwise well-meaning people. **Unknown persons** issued written orders, signed with unknown so-called "Order names," to the poor brothers of the Outer Court <note>The "Outer Court" refers to the basic levels or "Blue Lodges" of Freemasonry, as opposed to the "inner" secret high degrees.</note>, of whom they said: "You good, dear brother Freemasons, be **our children!**" And such a gracious decree was followed with the deepest reverence; for it was said: "The Order wills it, the Order says it, it is God's will that we should obey the superiors (3)." One would not believe that nonsense could go so far; and yet for years it went that far and even further.

I had the opportunity to become more closely acquainted with this "mystery of iniquity" <note>original: "Geheimniß der Bosheit," likely a reference to the biblical "mysterium iniquitatis" from 2 Thessalonians 2:7, implying a hidden, growing evil.</note> through various means, and I saw that the evil was noticeably increasing, especially from the year 1775 onward. My love for sound reason and my moral feeling revolted against it, and for a long time the wish had been stirred within me that such harmful nonsense might at least be lessened, assuming that the prejudices were rooted too deeply to be entirely eradicated. In France, around the year 1740, the word *Order* <note>original: "Ordre"</note> was used by the Freemason society unnoticed, as if by chance; not long after, it was also used in England, though more rarely, as there they usually speak only of *Masonry*. In Germany, since the year 1761, talk of an "Order"—and simultaneously of "Unknown Superiors" <note>The "Unknown Superiors" (*Superiores Incogniti*) were the alleged secret leaders of the "Strict Observance," a Masonic rite that claimed descent from the Knights Templar.</note> of the Order—had been quietly spread within Freemasonry. This abominable idea of Unknown Superiors was directly connected to the concept of a secret Order, of which it was then boldly said—albeit only in secret—that ordinary Freemasonry served merely as its curtain,

<summary>Nicolai describes how Enlightenment-era Freemasonry was subverted by "Unknown Superiors" who demanded blind obedience, transforming a fraternity into an authoritarian secret society.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of the "Unknown Superiors" and the shift in Freemasonry toward a secret, hierarchical structure. The previous page ended with the introduction of the term "Order" and the concept of mysterious leaders in Germany around 1761.</meta>

...to conceal the Order; a pretense which clearly indicated that unknown people wanted to use Freemasonry for secret purposes. But at that time, no one thought of this necessary conclusion; so strong were the thirst for mystery and the prejudices!

People soon whispered into each other’s ears: the inner core of the Masonic Order was to revive the old Order of the Knights Templar, which had allegedly continued in secret since the 14th century. It was claimed that at the time of the persecution of the Templars, a Grand Master <note>original: "Heermeister," a high-ranking military commander</note> of the Templars, Aumont, had fled to Scotland with several other Templars disguised as masons. Once this new Templar Order was set in motion, it was to be kept so secret that neither a <term>Novice <note>original Latin: "Novicius"</note></term> nor a <term>Squire <note>original Latin: "Armiger," literally an armor-bearer</note></term> nor a <term>Knight <note>original Latin: "Eques"</note></term>—for such were the Latin names given to these gentlemen, many of whom understood little or no Latin at all; and one man who certainly understood no Latin was the <term>House Steward <note>original Latin: "Provisor Domus"</note></term>—was permitted to let a Freemason of the lower degrees suspect that such an Order existed (exactly as was later required of the Rosicrucians). 

Indeed, it was asserted that this Order itself depended upon an unknown Grand Master, who was unknown even to the <term>Provincial <note>original Latin: "Provincialis"</note></term> or Grand Master of the 7th Province (Germany), Herr von Hund, known within the Order as <term>of the Sword <note>original Latin: "ab Ense"</note></term> (*); at least, the latter never made him known by name, nor would he say where he resided, although it was assured that the Unknown was named the <term>Knight of the Golden Sun <note>original Latin: "Eques a Sole Aureo"</note></term>.

This idea of a secret order and its commanderies <note>original: "Komthureyen," the administrative districts or manors of a military order</note> was, alas, seized upon with all too great an eagerness. Since the year 1764, the majority of Freemasons in Germany recognized the secret Templar—

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the critique of the "Strict Observance" system in Freemasonry, specifically focusing on the psychological control exerted by the "Unknown Superiors" and the historical introduction of the term "Order" to replace "Masonry."</meta>

...to be performed; and they actually forced such blind obedience from many otherwise well-thinking people. **Unknown people** issued written orders, signed with unknown so-called Order-names, to the poor brothers of the outer court <note>original: "Vorhof," referring to the lower, symbolic degrees of Masonry that were kept in the dark about the "higher" secrets</note>, of whom they said: "You good, dear brother Freemasons, be indeed **our children!**" And such a gracious decree was followed with the deepest reverence; for it was said: "the Order wills it, the Order says it, it is God's will that we should obey the superiors (3)." One should not believe that the nonsense could go so far; and yet for years it went that far and even further.

I had had the opportunity to become more closely acquainted with this mystery of wickedness <note>original: "Geheimniß der Bosheit," likely a reference to the biblical "mysterium iniquitatis" (2 Thessalonians 2:7)</note> in various ways, and I saw that the evil was noticeably increasing, especially from the year 1775 on. My love for sound reason and my moral feeling revolted against it, and had long since awakened in me the wish that such harmful nonsense might at least be diminished, even assuming the prejudices were too deeply rooted to be completely eradicated. In France, around the year 1740, the word *Order* <note>original French: "Ordre"</note> was used by the Freemasonic society unnoticed, as if by chance; not long after also in England, though more rarely, since there one usually only speaks of *Masonry*. In Germany, starting in 1761, talk of an Order and simultaneously of Unknown Superiors of the Order had been quietly spread within Freemasonry. This abominable idea of **Unknown Superiors** was directly connected to the concept of a **secret Order**, of which it was then said—secretly, to be sure, but boldly—that ordinary Freemasonry served only as its curtain <note>The author suggests that "regular" Masonry was used as a front or recruitment pool for a more exclusive, hidden agenda.</note>,

<summary>The author describes how anonymous leaders used the concept of "Unknown Superiors" to demand blind obedience from Freemasons, tracing the transition of the society into a "Secret Order" during the mid-18th century.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the author's critique of the "Strict Observance" movement within 18th-century Freemasonry, specifically how the myth of a secret Templar succession was used to manipulate members through a hierarchy of "Unknown Superiors."</meta>

...to hide the Order; a pretext which clearly indicated that unknown people wanted to use Freemasonry for secret purposes. But at that time, no one thought of this necessary conclusion; so strong were the thirst for mystery and prejudice!

It was soon whispered in ears: the inner core of the Freemasonic Order was the revival of the old Order of the Knights Templar, which had supposedly continued in secret ever since the 14th century, because at the time of the Templars' persecution, a Grand Master of the Templars, Aumont, had fled to Scotland with several Templars disguised as masons. Once this new Order of the Templars was set in motion, it was to be kept so secret that neither a <term>Novice</term> <note>original Latin: "Novicius," the introductory rank</note> nor a <term>Squire</term> <note>original Latin: "Armiger," a shield-bearer or candidate for knighthood</note> nor a <term>Knight</term> <note>original Latin: "Eques"</note> — (for these gentlemen were titled in Latin, though many of them understood little or no Latin at all; and one man who certainly understood no Latin was the <term>House Steward</term> <note>original Latin: "Provisor Domus," an officer in charge of the physical lodge or "house"</note>) — was permitted to let a Freemason of the lower degrees notice that such an Order even existed (exactly as this was later imposed upon the Rosicrucians). Indeed, it was claimed that this Order itself depended upon an unknown Grand Master, who was unknown even to the <term>Provincial Master</term> <note>original Latin: "Provincialis"</note> or Army Master of the VIIth Province (Germany), Mr. von Hund, called <term>of the Sword</term> <note>original Latin: "ab Ense"</note> within the Order (*); at the very least, the latter never wished to make him known by name, or say where he resided, although it was asserted that the Unknown was called <term>Knight of the Golden Sun</term> <note>original Latin: "Eques a Sole Aureo"</note>.

This idea of a secret Order and its <term>Commanderies</term> <note>original: "Komthureyen," the administrative districts or estates of a military order</note> was, alas! embraced with all too great a desire. From the year 1764 on, the majority of Freemasons in Germany recognized the secret Templar Order—

Within this Knights Templar Order itself ($^6$), however, things occurred that anyone who has not experienced them personally or knows them from official records would not believe. For example, initially, the knights to be initiated—armed from head to toe <note>original French: "armés de pied en cap"</note>—had to keep a "knightly watch" at night; though this did not take place out of doors, it was nevertheless performed at a window ($^7$). 

I also recall the so-called Clerics of the Knights Templar Order, or <term>Regular Canons of Jerusalem</term> <note>original Latin: "Canonici regulares Hierosol."; these were a specific branch within the Strict Observance that claimed to hold the "magical" and "spiritual" secrets of the old Templars.</note>, who came forward from Wismar starting in 1767. In this agreement, they promised to prove their legitimacy in the future—specifically, that they were the true <term>Canons of the Temple of Jerusalem</term> <note>original Latin: "Canonici Templi Hierosolymitani."</note>. But in the end, they proved their legitimacy just as little as the Army Master of the Sword himself. After several conventions had been held, after much...

This led to the calling of a convention in Wilhelmsbad in the year 1782, where various matters were to be decided, including the question: **What is the true origin of the Order of Freemasons?**

I had always investigated the **history of the Order of the Knights Templar** truly without the slightest regard for Freemasonry; I studied it merely to see if I could solve a historical problem—which this history certainly was for a long time. However, since I now had to consider everything pertaining to the Order of the Knights Templar, and since I also reflected upon the quite remarkable historical traces of the continuation of this Order in various places for some time after its suppression, the recent activities involving a Templar Order within Freemasonry naturally came to my attention as well. I likewise made various observations on this matter, which, however, are not intended for the public. I was guided toward this subject all the more because I had previously debated various related matters with **Lessing**. I was also well aware of what most Freemasons dreamed regarding their ancient origin, which they presumed to derive from Egyptian priests, from **Osiris** (that is, from the sun), from **Pythagoras**, from the **Essenes**, from the **Culdees** <note>original: "Kuldeern"; an early medieval ascetic Christian community in the British Isles often claimed as Masonic ancestors.</note>, from the **Canons of the Holy Sepulchre** <note>original: "Canonicis Sti Sepulcri"</note> in Jerusalem, from the wandering masons of the Middle Ages who were said to have built churches and chapels, from the architecture of **Inigo Jones** <note>1573–1652; the famous English architect often mythologized as a Grand Master in Masonic history.</note> in England, and God knows from where else—and finally, from the **Knights Templar**, who were supposed to have always continued in secret. I saw with sadness that even learned and sensible people were not ashamed to strengthen their gullible brothers in such groundless prejudices, without any regard for historical criticism. This happened especially—and likely still happens—through various published histories of **Freemasonry**, which, as far as they concern ancient times, are entirely fictional in their literal sense, and only then become understandable when one knows a certain **cipher language** <note>original: "Chiffersprache"; referring to allegorical or symbolic codes used by secret societies to hide their true intent.</note> (¹¹). They are intentionally composed in such a way that the actual meaning can only be guessed by a few, whereas ignorance, the craving for secrets, and **fanaticism** can project a meaning into them however they wish (¹²); all of which, whether it happens this way or that, is a matter of great indifference to the Unknown Superiors, provided only that the intellect is crippled and the blind craving for secrets (¹³) is nourished.

In particular, the foolish belief in an ancient origin of the order was propagated.

I was even further from wanting to bring about any reform myself, or to meddle in the various reforms which had all been started for some time in the most perverse way (for they all came from the **Unknown Superiors** <note>original: "Unbekannten Obern"; the secret, high-ranking leaders of the "Strict Observance" branch of Freemasonry who claimed to possess ancient wisdom.</note>); or even to distinguish myself in the slightest way in lodge affairs. <meta>This page continues the author's discussion regarding his historical research into Freemasonry. On the previous page, he began to argue against the common myth that Freemasonry descended directly from the medieval Knights Templar, suggesting instead a different historical lineage linked to English developments and German Rosicrucianism.</meta>

...but rather had its first origin in England, emerging from the Rosicrucian Society which had first arisen **in Germany**. I intended, on another occasion, to explain that the old Rosicrucian Society <note>The "old" Rosicrucians refer to the original 17th-century movement sparked by the "Fama Fraternitatis," which was primarily a literary and philosophical phenomenon.</note> was entirely different from the secret society which, since approximately the year 1777, began to become known in public writings in Germany under the name of the **Gold and Rosicrucians of the Old System** <term>Gold and Rosicrucians of the Old System: "Gold- und Rosenkreuzer alten Systems"; a highly secretive, hierarchical Masonic-Rosicrucian order that practiced alchemy and mystical Christianity, exerting significant political influence in Prussia.</term>. But the more I learned about this latter society, the more I found that it would not only be ill-advised but certainly very dangerous to speak of it publicly.

It gradually became all too well known to me what a **powerful following** their members had in most European countries, and especially everywhere in Germany. Already since the year 1783, the imminent death of **Frederick the Great** <note>Frederick II of Prussia (1712–1786), who was a Freemason but favored Enlightenment rationalism over the mystical and occult leanings of the later Rosicrucians.</note> was suspected by many persons, and quite a variety of projects were built upon that expectation. The Circle Directors <term>Circle Directors: "Zirkeldirektoren"; the administrative heads of the local "Circles" or lodges within the Gold and Rosicrucian order.</term> of certain circles of the Gold and Rosicrucians in Berlin made it quite clear in private company what influence their Order would have under the new government—not only through the destruction of all Freemasonry that did not depend on them, but also regarding many other matters in the state <note>This refers to the transition of power to Frederick William II, Frederick the Great's nephew, who was a devoted member of the Rosicrucians and allowed his advisors, Wöllner and Bischoffwerder, to implement conservative and mystical policies.</note>. To those who knew the state of affairs, the **possibility** of such a success was very easy to see; and subsequent times have indeed shown something of the **reality**, particularly regarding the far-reaching intentions of the **Unknown Superiors**. A prudent man, who seeks to be useful through his public speaking on sensitive subjects, must reflect carefully on what he may talk about and what he must remain silent upon; and no one can be expected to expose himself to a certain danger and [himself]...

<meta>This page continues the author's account of his cautious approach to writing about the history of secret societies. Having previously mentioned the danger of offending powerful members of the "Gold and Rosicrucians" during the transition of power in Prussia, he now explains how he had to temper his language in the second volume of his work to avoid making enemies while still attempting to convey the truth about the origins of Freemasonry.</meta>

...making powerful enemies without hope of achieving a sensible purpose. I therefore stated in the second part of my book, quite explicitly but in the mildest possible way, that when I derived the **Freemason society** from the **Rosicrucians**, I certainly did not mean the **Gold and Rosicrucians** of modern times <note>original: ($^{18}$)</note>. To express oneself so extremely cautiously was very necessary at that time <note>original: ($^{19}$); likely referring to the late 1780s when the Gold and Rosicrucians held significant influence over King Frederick William II of Prussia.</note>.

->I was walking through fires<-
->Placed beneath treacherous ash.<-
<note>original: "Incedebam per ignes / Suppositos cineri doloso." A quote from the Roman poet Horace (Odes 2.1), warning of the dangers of writing about recent civil wars or sensitive political topics.</note>

I believed that with such caution I could carry out my well-intentioned purpose; yet I soon encountered greater difficulties than I had initially imagined. I very soon felt that I could not express myself so definitively on most subjects as to be fully understood by every one of my readers. I was not even allowed to speak clearly about ordinary **Freemasonry**, its activities, degrees, forms, etc., especially then, when the secrecy of basically very insignificant things was still anxiously maintained. Still less was I permitted to talk openly about what concerned the inner workings, especially since everything within it was in a state of great **crisis**, and very distinguished persons would have found themselves offended by even the slightest mention of it <note>original: ($^{20}$); high-ranking nobles and even the Prussian King were members of these orders, making any critique potentially treasonous.</note>. Nonetheless, it would have been very useful for my purpose (and would still be useful now), if it were possible to publicly explain everything that is known to me about the inner circles, and which was partly known even then. If one takes and compares everything together, there is—at least from the year 1682 until now—an unmistakable connection; through older **history** one finds much clarification regarding more recent events, and through recent incidents much is again explained from the...

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his strategic reticence in his historical writings. He discusses his internal conflict regarding the controversial appendix on Freemasonry in his work on the Knights Templar and outlines his goal to steer readers toward a rational, historical understanding of the society's 17th-century origins rather than mystical, ancient legends.</meta>

...explained in ancient times. But I was not permitted to involve myself in this before the public. Dissatisfied with myself, I was more than once inclined to completely omit the appendix on **Freemasonry** from my *Essay on the Knights Templar*, a work which subsequently gained an undeserved celebrity for a time solely because of this appendix. Were I not to consider the benefit that I have at least indirectly provided through it, I might well repent ever having said a word publicly about **Freemasonry**, given the many false interpretations and the displeasure and waste of time it has caused me.

However, the hope of providing a bit more room for **healthy reason** <note>The author uses the term "gesunde Vernunft," a hallmark of the Enlightenment, referring to rational thought over superstition and blind faith.</note> in the state of affairs at that time outweighed my misgivings. I imagined that even if those minds heated by **fanaticism** <note>In the 18th century, "fanaticism" often referred to irrational zeal for mystical, occult, or unproven "secret histories" of societies.</note> could initially only be brought back from the foolish opinion of an ancient secret origin by drawing attention to the more recent origin of **Freemasonry** and its double transformation in the **17th century**, this might for the moment prompt reflection, and perhaps something more detailed could be said about it in the future.

I believed I would find acceptance all the more easily if I let nothing be noticed of all the recent events since the years 1758 (21) and 1763 (22) or 1775 (23) <note>These dates correspond to significant shifts in Masonic history, including the Seven Years' War and various conventions (like those of the Strict Observance) that changed the structure of the lodges.</note>, and thus gave the **spirit of partisanship** <term>spirit of partisanship (Parteygeist): the tendency to favor the interests of one's own sect or faction over objective truth.</term> less reason to suspect that I wished to attack any particular Masonic system; which was not my intention in the slightest. Therefore, I sought to hint at the origin of **Freemasonry** and the most important points of its transformation rather than to fully develop them. I spoke definitively only of the origin in the first half of the **17th century**, because this was what was mainly close to my heart; regarding the rest, I gave

<meta>This page continues the author’s account of the backlash he faced after publishing his "Essay on the Knights Templar." He explains how his attempt to use subtle, rationalist hints to correct mystical misconceptions about Freemasonry was met with hostility from various factions within the order, leading to personal professional risks during the late 18th century.</meta>

...scattered hints, and hoped to be understood by those who were meant to understand me. In vain! The more cautiously I expressed myself, the more I was misunderstood. Partly, I freely admit, through my own fault, since I simply had not expressed myself clearly because I was unable and not permitted to do so; but for the most part, and even more so, through the fault of others. One who stands alone, and without <term>partisan bias</term> <note>The original term "Parteysucht" refers to a blind, obsessive loyalty to a specific faction or sect, which the author contrasts with the objective search for truth.</note> seeks the truth—which is to be found in no party—will be attacked by all parties, especially where obscure concepts and passions are at play. Only a few people have a sense for the fact that someone can write something purely out of a love for **truth**, even though he thereby exposes himself to disparagement and unfavorable judgments. 

Several readers of the appendices to my *Essay* did not want **Freemasonry** to be spoken of publicly at all, and believed they would lose far too much of the holiness of their association if it were shown to have had such a recent, and moreover merely accidental origin, and had even undergone transformations of which Brother Orator <note>In 18th-century Masonic lodges, the "Brother Orator" was an officer responsible for delivering speeches and interpreting the history and morals of the order to the members.</note>, who often knows so much to say, had never told them anything. These people zealously protested with lack of understanding. Others, who either knew the inner workings exactly or were close to them, and likely understood me quite well, proceeded very quietly but harbored a silent resentment, the **effects** of which I felt for a long time; and had not some particularly fortunate circumstances intervened, I might well have personally become a victim of my love for truth between the years 1786 and 1796. This kind of person never forgives, but never strikes until it is certain that the blow will land! Furthermore, there were a number of people who [hoped for] all sorts of fine, obscure discoveries about

<meta>This page continues the author’s narrative about the fierce opposition he encountered from within the Freemasonic community following the publication of his research. ...claimed to represent **Freemasonry**, and who had managed to acquire a reputation for great scholarly wisdom within a certain circle. They fell upon me, both secretly and publicly, with fury. **Fury** is the right word; I refer here to the assault by my most well-known public opponent, which compelled me to write the second part of my *Essay* <note>The author refers to his work *Versuch über die Beschuldigungen, welche dem Tempelherrenorden gemacht worden* (Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of the Knights Templar).</note>. For a long time, as the editor of the *General German Library* <note>The *Allgemeine Deutsche Bibliothek*, a leading journal of the German Enlightenment edited by Friedrich Nicolai, which reviewed almost all contemporary German publications.</note>, I had already come to know the **irritable race of poets** <note>original: "genus irritabile vatum" — a famous expression from Horace referring to the sensitive and often thin-skinned nature of writers.</note> through experience; but I freely confess that, although I believed I knew them well enough, I did not yet sufficiently know the **irritable race of masons** <note>original: "genus irritabile latomorum" — the author cleverly adapts Horace's phrase to describe the sensitivity of Freemasons (latomus is Latin for stone-cutter or mason).</note> at that time. It pains me very much that I must mention the name of one of the most excellent German writers here, who, through later, completely changed sentiments and clear insights, amply made amends for various errors of his younger years, where unregulated vehemence sometimes drove him from the path of calm reflection; and with whom, long before his far-too-early passing, I had no further misunderstanding, as a mutual good understanding instead existed between us. **Herder** <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803), a titan of German literature and philosophy, who was indeed a Freemason and initially a sharp critic of the author’s rationalist take on the Order.</note> was, alas! my heated opponent.

Mr. Buhle has, with such visible mockery and ill will, attempted to place what I have written about **Rosicrucianism** and **Freemasonry** in such a false light that I know no other way to demonstrate the correct perspective than by providing a detailed account of the occasion and history of everything pertaining to it. How could I then refrain from speaking of Herder’s attack and explaining the matter frankly according to its true nature, much as I regret it!

I had been on very friendly terms with Herder from 1767 to 1774; he also had a not inconsiderable share in the *General German Library* <note>The *Allgemeine Deutsche Bibliothek*, the influential Enlightenment journal founded and edited by Nicolai.</note>. Following this, a misunderstanding arose between us. If some blame should lie on my side, I can say with a good conscience that it would only be attributed to my love of truth and all-too-candid frankness. Through the gossip of others, the misunderstanding was magnified, and all correspondence and connection between us ceased. In those years, Herder followed the influences of imagination and passion all too easily; in general, that time was the era of the **men of genius** <note>original: "Kraftgenies" — a term for the "strong geniuses" of the *Sturm und Drang* (Storm and Stress) movement who valued raw emotion and individual intuition over the "cold" reason and systematic critique of the Enlightenment.</note>, who reluctantly endured calm and mature judgment and believed they could strike it down through shown contempt; just as the

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s account of his intellectual falling out with Johann Gottfried von Herder. Nicolai is explaining that the intensity of Herder's public attack on him was likely fueled by their disagreement over the origins of Freemasonry and Herder’s own speculative work on ancient history.</meta>

...of the "geniuses" <note>original: *Kraftgenies*; a contemporary term for the impulsive, hyper-emotional writers of the *Sturm und Drang* (Storm and Stress) movement.</note> of the second rank who, for about ten years, shone like bright meteors in the literary atmosphere of Germany and are now falling one after another like dead stones. Herder believed himself to have been insulted by me. Perhaps this influenced the unheard-of violence of his attack; for my expressed opinion on the modern origin of **Freemasonry** stood in direct opposition to that very book over which the final misunderstanding between us had arisen: the *Oldest Document of the Human Race* <note>original: *Aelteste Urkunde des Menschengeschlechts*, published between 1774 and 1776.</note> (24).

In this book, full of extravagant imagination, Herder had taken far too much account of **Freemasonry**. It was also read with the greatest reverence by many Freemasons, who imagined they had found great secrets in the number 7, in the seven "sounding powers," in the seven "crying spirits," and in the symbols <image-desc>Four circular alchemical or kabbalistic symbols: a circle with a cross inside, a circle with a horizontal line, a circle with an 'X', and a circle with a vertical line.</image-desc> and other miserable kabbalistic <note>The Kabbalah is a tradition of Jewish mysticism; Nicolai, as a rationalist of the Enlightenment, viewed these mystical interpretations as "follies."</note> follies (25) upon which the *Oldest Document* places such great weight. Perhaps there are people still living in Riga <note>The city in modern-day Latvia where Herder lived and worked as a teacher and clergyman from 1764 to 1769.</note> who remember some of the circumstances of this Masonic enthusiasm and fanaticism. It was no wonder that Herder—who at the time so enjoyed wallowing in half-dark, fluctuating ideas (as shown by his previous 1773 dispute with Schlözer <note>August Ludwig von Schlözer (1735–1809), a prominent historian who advocated for a more scientific, data-driven approach to history than Herder's poetic style.</note> over **Universal History**, and even his dispute with me)—allowed his imagination to play in the realm of history without any historical criticism, a field in which he had not yet acquired any thorough knowledge. It was therefore not surprising that Herder seriously imagined that the secrets of **Freemasonry**, in which he had become somewhat deeply entangled, might well have originated from Egyptian mysteries, and generally from the most ancient antiquity. He generally set to work with a passion that exceeded all boundaries that a noble and truth-loving man should set for himself in any scholarly dispute. He did not even take the trouble to properly grasp my assertions; he threw everything into confusion that I had explained regarding the **Knights Templar** as well as the **Rosicrucians** and **Freemasons**, misrepresented almost all my claims, blamed me for despicable intentions, even allowed himself false citations (26), and although he himself [understood] nothing thoroughly <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break.</note>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s rebuttal of Johann Gottfried von Herder. An example of contending against me, which Mr. Court Councilor <term>Court Councilor (Hofrath): a prestigious title granted to high-ranking advisors or distinguished scholars in German states.</term> Buhle also follows, though not with equal steps! <note>original: "passibus aequis"; a Latin reference to Virgil's Aeneid, suggesting Buhle followed Herder’s path but lacked his predecessor's stride or impact.</note>

The name **Herder**, which soon became well-known, made an impression, as no one believed such a man would dismiss something so boldly if he had not thoroughly investigated the matter. I was thereby compelled to write the second part of my **Essay** <note>original: "Versuchs"; referring to Nicolai’s major work *Versuch über die Beschuldigungen welche dem Tempelherrenorden gemacht worden* (Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of the Knights Templar).</note>, in which I demonstrated my opponent’s ignorance and ill will step by step in the clearest possible way. It cannot but cause a painful sensation when, after one has investigated a complex subject with one's best efforts, another person, who knows next to nothing about it, speaks of it with such arrogance before a public that has neither the leisure nor the inclination to seriously examine the subjects, or even to check the citations <note>original: "Citationen"</note> to see where the opponent falsified them. For the one who is treated so unworthily and unfairly, it is quite impossible to suppress his resentment. I did not entirely suppress it in my defense <note>original: "Vertheidigung"</note>, as I was forced to defend myself. But I still regret the time I had to spend on it, and just as much being forced anew to speak at length, after his death, about this lapse by an otherwise estimable, learned man who subsequently proved himself in such an excellent manner.

While writing the second part of my Essay, I felt on almost every page of its appendix how difficult, indeed impossible, it is to speak with certainty about Masonic matters <note>original: "freymaurerische Angelegenheiten"</note>, and even about the origin of this society—especially when one's only intention is to be useful, rather than to cause strife and
