Following his defense against Herder on the previous page, he now turns to the broader difficulty of discussing secret societies like the Freemasons and the Knights Templar without causing offense or being misunderstood.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

...to cause displeasure. One cannot and must not clearly express a multitude of subjects, which nonetheless belong to the matter, without being guilty of a highly reprehensible indiscretion. One is not understood, and yet is judged, not only by non-Freemasons but even by the greater part of the **Freemasons** themselves; most readers who take up a book of this kind are either merely curious or have already taken a side beforehand. These latter ones especially expect to see their preconceived opinions confirmed and flattered; they are therefore not even inclined to be taught, but rather become embarrassed or indignant when they find things that are incompatible with their prejudices. I know this partisan spirit from long experience. People who are either afflicted with gross prejudices, or who even secretly seek to favor the cause of the **Unknown Fathers** <term>Unknown Fathers (Unbekannte Väter): a term used in 18th-century "High Degree" Freemasonry to refer to secret, hidden superiors or masters who allegedly directed the order from behind the scenes.</term>, knowingly refuse to let the truth emerge. They deny with the greatest audacity what others assert on the basis of very secure documents, which simply cannot be made public. They distort the facts, mix truth with falsehood, and thereby seek to make the former doubtful. I have also experienced striking examples of these clever arts, for instance, in the dispute over the **Clericate of the Templars** <term>Clericate of the Templars (Klerikat der Tempelherren): A controversial branch of Freemasonry (the Strict Observance) which claimed to have inherited secret priestly knowledge from the historical Knights Templar.</term>. I note here in passing that Dr. **H. Pr. Starck** <note>Johann August von Starck (1741–1812), a theologian and controversial Masonic figure accused of trying to lead Freemasonry back to Catholicism.</note>, because of his indeed very careless remarks, was wrongly blamed for the whole matter, since he was only a part of the whole; but, as a Protestant scholar, he must certainly be regarded as a very thoughtlessly active participant. Everything else in this adverse history, which caused such a dirty dispute, may remain in its place.

<summary>Nicolai explains the frustration of writing about secret societies for a public blinded by prejudice and the strategic denials of those protecting the "Unknown Fathers." He specifically addresses the controversy surrounding Johann August von Starck and the "Clericate" of the Knights Templar.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Unknown Fathers, Johann August von Starck, Knights Templar, Clericate, public prejudice, historical documents, partisan spirit</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s narrative regarding his decision to withdraw from public debates about Freemasonry. Having established the historical inaccuracies of the "clerical" branch of the Order on the previous page, he now describes his broader strategy: to undermine superstition by promoting historical Enlightenment rather than through direct confrontation.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

Convinced, therefore, that the nature of a secret society does not permit one to express oneself clearly about its affairs—even with the best of intentions—I formed the resolution, following the completion of the second part of my **Essay** <note>This refers to Nicolai’s *Versuch über die Beschuldigungen, welche dem Tempelherrenorden gemacht worden* (Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of the Knights Templar), published in 1782.</note>, to lose no further word publicly about **Freemasonry**, its origin, and its progress. My main purpose was achieved to some extent by the fact that I was the first to publicly point out the recent origin of Freemasonry, thereby greatly shaking the belief in the fantasies of an ancient, mysterious origin.

If reflection and attention, along with a tendency toward definite concepts, are first aroused in upright men, then prejudices and a foolish **obsession with secrets** will gradually fall away of their own accord. Furthermore, the game played by the **Unknown Superiors** <term>Unknown Superiors (Unbekannte Oberen): The hidden leaders of the "Strict Observance" branch of Freemasonry, to whom members owed absolute obedience without knowing their identities.</term>—which is so harmful to common sense and the welfare of humanity (and which secretly, alas, still continues even now!)—will be at least somewhat spoiled. One begins to reflect, after all, whether **Unknown Men** could really have a right to command, and whether their intentions might actually be rational and good. 

On this subject, I often had the opportunity to return during the disputes stirred up against me, through no fault of my own, by the Senior Court Chaplain **Stark** <note>Johann August von Starck (1741–1812), a theologian and high-ranking Freemason who was Nicolai’s primary intellectual antagonist; Nicolai accused him of trying to lead Freemasonry back toward Catholicism and mysticism.</note>. In doing so, I perhaps accidentally caused some good within such a numerous and, at that time, very misled society; for without mixing myself directly into their internal affairs, I provided the opportunity for the expulsion of **superstition** through the **Enlightenment** of their history. Since I saw myself unable, by the very nature of the matter, to do more in this regard—and since so many incidents clearly showed me how necessary it always is, and was then even more necessary, to combine caution with frankness...

<summary>Nicolai explains his decision to stop writing publicly about Freemasonry, believing he has already successfully challenged the myth of its ancient origins. He reflects on his conflict with Johann August von Starck and his goal of using historical facts to dismantle the influence of "Unknown Superiors."</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Enlightenment, Unknown Superiors, Johann August von Starck, superstition, secret societies, historical criticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s personal reflection on his relationship with Freemasonry. After stating on the previous page that he would no longer write publicly on the matter to avoid futility, he here explains his refusal to seek office or lead internal reforms within the lodges, citing the inherent resistance of secret societies to outside interference.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>

to connect; why should I have written further about **Freemasonry**? I would regret it, indeed I would be ashamed, to have written even a single word publicly about it, had it not been done with the intention of being useful. For myself, I have never sought anything through **Freemasonry** or within **Freemasonry**. I never desired to have influence within it, never sought or accepted an office; even less did it occur to me to ever reform anything in any part of this society. I knew far too well the nature of all societies, and especially all secret ones. If someone, who is not appointed to do so, presumes to directly oppose abuses or harmful prejudices within any human association, it is naturally taken as arrogance, and often those who harbor prejudices only become more stubborn because of it. This applies from the State down to the smallest dinner party. All those who, by their own individual **absolute authority** <term>Machtvollkommenheit: A term implying total or plenary power, often used in a legal or sovereign sense; here, Nicolai uses it to describe the hubris of individual reformers.</term>, presumed to reform **Freemasonry**—even if their reforms appeared ever so fine and moral on the surface—have run aground on this cliff; even when they did not combine them with private motives, as was, alas! only too often the case. 

Therefore, it was indeed interesting to me to get to know all **Masonic systems** <note>During the late 18th century, Freemasonry was fractured into various "systems" or rites, such as the Strict Observance or the Swedish Rite, each with different hierarchies and mystical claims.</note> whenever I had the opportunity; because no subject can be judged thoroughly if one refuses to view it from several sides. But I left all systems or branches as they were; for I found myself neither called nor capable of intruding into their business and administration, or of improving anything about them. Regarding abuses and prejudices within it, I spoke orally only to those who were previously informed on the matter and who simultaneously had confidence in me, to

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of his public and private writings on Freemasonry. Having established that he never sought power within the lodges, he now explains that his goal was to promote Enlightenment values—clear thinking and reason—over the mystical jargon and "fanaticism" often found in secret societies during the 18th century.</meta>

...to whom I also could have confidence. I am convinced that through this, I have awakened in many an honest man a leaning toward clear, definite concepts, and a distaste for the trifles of mystically anointed or obscure, high-sounding phrases. In doing so, I have perhaps indirectly helped to prepare the way for many better ideas and for various praiseworthy changes that have occurred here and there. This was enough for me. In my public writings, I have never spoken of **Masonic** matters except in general terms, and only during disputes that arose against my will. But of course, I have continued, as I have always done, to defend the rights of sound reason on every occasion in my writings, and to write most candidly against <term>fanaticism</term> <note>original: *Schwärmerey*; in Nicolai’s time, this specifically referred to a kind of irrational religious enthusiasm or mystical delusion that the Enlightenment sought to counter.</note>, superstition, and everything connected with them, leaving it to everyone to take from it as much as might apply to them. **If it itches, scratch it!** <note>This is a German proverb (*Wen es juckt, der kratze sich!*) similar to the English "If the shoe fits, wear it."</note>

From what has been said before, it is sufficiently clear that it is impossible to precisely discuss the origin, the nature, and the most significant changes of **Freemasonry** (which are constantly so interrelated that none can be clearly understood without the others) in public writings. This is because one would have to write for uninformed readers, yet, by the very nature of the subject, one cannot instruct them thoroughly in a public forum. However, it does not follow at all that one may not successfully attempt—for oneself and for informed men, for careful private use, through continued earnest research—a true and useful history of both **Freemasonry** and the **Masonic Order** (27).

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s personal account of his intellectual engagement with Freemasonry. Having previously stated that he writes against "fanaticism" and for the sake of clear reason, he here describes his private motivation: to apply the historical method to the confusing and often contradictory traditions of the Masonic lodges.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

...for my own satisfaction, quite privately. I love definite concepts; obscure and wavering expressions satisfy me neither in philosophy, nor in history, nor anywhere else. After I had seen and heard no small amount within **Freemasonry**, I was by no means satisfied by the obscure indications. Nevertheless, I could sufficiently perceive that all these things had not been blown together by chance, but that, however disparate in nature some appeared to be, they nevertheless bore traces of a certain agreement and a common origin. To research this connection was my intention. **History** carries the torch before **Enlightenment** <note>original: *Aufklärung*; the 18th-century movement that championed reason and evidence-based knowledge over dogma and superstition.</note>. In theology and philosophy, this is very visible; he who has not earnestly studied the gradual emergence and change of theological dogmas and the opinions of philosophers—he who never bothered with how one gradually arose and was derived from the other (28) <note>This marks the beginning of page 28 in the original printed edition.</note>—will always remain a very one-sided, short-sighted theologian and philosopher. 

So I also sought to get to know **Freemasonry** and its various branches thoroughly, in order to gradually arrive at the origin of the society itself, and the meaning and purpose of that which is prefigured and obscurely taught therein in such manifold ways. This research was not the work of a few weeks or months, and I do not yet believe that I have entirely missed the truth on this path. The path, though it is the longest and certainly not open to everyone, is surely the most rational. To this there belongs, of course, besides a not-entirely superficial knowledge of history, also a not insignificant knowledge of **Freemasonry** in its various branches from the beginning...

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s personal account of his intellectual engagement with Freemasonry. <page-num>28</page-num>

...on, combined with a tireless spirit of inquiry <note>original: *Forschungsgeist*</note> and a not entirely unsuccessful gift for synthesis <note>original: *Kombinationsgabe*; the ability to connect disparate facts into a coherent whole.</note>. This path—I may say so boldly—no one before me, as far as is publicly known, has trodden; at least certainly not with the same care to guard against detours and mere imaginings through the most precise definition, comparison, and distinction of objects and concepts, with no other intention than to find the truth <note>original: *Wahrheit*</note> out of the rubble of the most bizarre falsehoods. If I should have gone completely astray upon it, I will gladly leave the prize to whoever knows how to find it better.

I have come to know two men who attempted this as well—though not publicly—with a considerable part of **Freemasonry** <note>original: *Freymaurerey*</note>. I gladly do justice to their unadulterated love of truth and their multifaceted insights. Nevertheless, I believe that I have considered the subject more in its general context and, especially regarding earlier times, have remained more precisely aligned with **history** <note>original: *Geschichte*</note>. That, despite the different perspectives, both truth-loving men essentially arrived at the same results as I did, is a new proof that these results must indeed have a common foundation.

The path I took was as follows. First, through research and comparisons of all kinds, I examined the legend <note>original: *Sage*</note>, so widely spread among all Freemasons fifty or more years ago, of their society’s origin in the **Egyptian and Greek mysteries**, and the subsequent dreams of an imaginary continuation of these among the first Christians; the **legends** of the wanderers of the masons in the **Middle Ages** to build churches, and other such alleged reports of a...

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s account of his historical investigation into Freemasonry’s origins. gray antiquity ($^{29}$). I found that all of this was historically unfounded; yet I had not undertaken this laborious research entirely in vain. For it became quite clear to me afterward that various of these **legends** <note>original: *Legenden*</note> from the **Middle Ages** <note>original: *Mittelalter*</note> were either intentionally invented or at least utilized to represent certain things symbolically for those to whom the **secret** <note>original: *Geheimniß*</note> was to be made known in the future, and to hide them from all others who had not yet reached that point, or who perhaps might never, or should never, reach it.

I therefore paused at the **Rosicrucians** <note>original: *Rosenkreuzer*; an esoteric movement that emerged in the early 1600s, claiming secret wisdom</note> because, as I soon realized, they clearly shared various outward signs with the **Freemasons** <note>original: *Freymaurer*</note>; and because repeated investigations firmly convinced me that before the time of the Rosicrucians, nothing resembling Freemasonry is to be found. I have been more richly equipped with **materials** <note>original: *Materialien*; historical sources and documents</note> for this **history** <note>original: *Geschichte*</note> than perhaps anyone else, and in terms of the patience and perseverance required to use them—to read through writings that have so little appeal with close attention and to take excerpts as the opportunity arises—I may well say that few will perhaps equal me. A love for **literary investigations** <note>original: *literarischen Untersuchungen*; meaning the scholarly study of historical texts</note> that is not found everywhere, and a desire to endure long in the task, can alone motivate such a sacrifice. This effort is ultimately rewarded by the consciousness that one can oversee subjects in their connection with a certain clarity and recognize them more accurately than those who pretend to be well-read without having read, or who falsely boast of having investigated with **historical criticism** <note>original: *historischer Kritik*; the scientific method of evaluating the authenticity and context of sources</note> without ever having seriously investigated anything. <meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critique of contemporary historians, specifically addressing Johann Gottlieb Buhle. Nicolai contrasts his own evidence-based approach with those who make bold claims without thorough archival research. He introduces his acquisition of the Johann Valentin Andreä archives as a primary source for his history of the Rosicrucians.</meta>

...only after precise and careful examination, or according to the first best imagination or delusion, he who decides quite boldly without special effort—as **Mr. Buhle** <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a scholar who argued that Freemasonry evolved from Rosicrucianism, a view Nicolai partially shared but contested in detail</note> and many of his kind do here—fares better than he who only dares to draw a few results after long investigation and, if necessary, gladly accepts further instruction.

As early as the year 1783, I had the good fortune to purchase the entire collection of posthumous manuscripts of the famous **Johann Valentin Andreä** <note>1586–1654; a German theologian widely identified as the author of the Rosicrucian manifestos</note>, among which are also manuscripts by his son **Gottlieb Andreä** and by **Jakob Andreä** <note>1528–1590; a famous Lutheran theologian and Johann Valentin’s grandfather</note>, together with an almost complete copy of J. V. Andreä’s printed works, which **A. C. Zeller** <note>Andreas Christoph Zeller (1684–1743), an educator and collector of Andreä’s works</note> had assembled with great effort in the first quarter of the 18th century, including various small, rare, and almost entirely unknown **pamphlets** <note>original: *Traktätchen*</note>. These manuscripts had long been preserved by J. V. Andreä’s descendants and were eventually combined with Zeller’s collection of printed writings. In these manuscripts, much of interest regarding Johann Valentin is to be found; in particular, certain individual passages in the correspondence often provided me with clarity regarding various conjectures I had formed from other circumstances, as well as from his **autobiography written in the Latin language** <note>original: *Vita ab ipso conscripta*; Andreä’s self-authored life story, used by Nicolai to prove that the Rosicrucian movement was originally intended as a literary satire or "parody"</note>.

It is, as is well known, quite generally accepted that J. V. Andreä caused all the widespread talk about **Rosicrucians** <note>original: *Rosenkreuzer*</note> through the **Fama** and **Confessio** <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the founding anonymous manifestos of the Rosicrucian myth</note>, which first became known in 1614, as well as through the **Chemical Wedding** <note>original: *Chymische Hochzeit*; an alchemical allegory published in 1616</note> which appeared in 1616. ...then I began to investigate more closely what had first prompted him to announce a **Rosicrucian Society** <note>original: *Gesellschaft des Rosenkreuzes*; the mysterious fraternity whose manifestos appeared in the early 17th century, sparking a massive intellectual controversy</note>, and shortly thereafter to take on the appearance of either knowing nothing about it or having merely played a joke with this legend <note>Andreä later referred to the Rosicrucian movement as a *ludibrium* (a scholarly jest), though scholars still debate whether he intended it as a serious call for reformation or a satire</note>. <meta>This page continues Nicolai's discussion regarding his correspondence with other scholars—specifically Johann Gottlieb Buhle—and his deep dive into the manuscripts of Johann Valentin Andreä. It follows the comparison of different copies of Andreä’s autobiography (the *Vita*).</meta>

In the year 1794, I shared these willingly and received them back from him with some interesting remarks, for which I am indebted to him; among them was naturally also my transcript, supplemented by the **Wolfenbüttel manuscript**, which **Pastor Seybold** <note>Johann Gottlieb Seybold (1743–1821), a theologian and educator</note> received from **Pastor Burk**. This Swabian scholar <note>original: *Wirtembergische Gelehrte*; referring to Burk</note>, who occupied himself for many years with the literature of J. V. Andreä's life and who in the year 1793 had a **valuable catalog** <note>Philipp David Burk's *Verzeichniß der Schriften Joh. Val. Andreä*</note> of his writings printed, confessed that through my collection he had become acquainted with several works previously unknown to him. That I have attentively read through these writings—in which so much refreshing food for the mind and heart is to be found—with the intent of becoming more precisely acquainted with the character and undertakings of this man, is surely something I can be credited with; for I have not shied away from reading through a multitude of other seemingly absurd and tedious books in order to thoroughly study the so-entangled history of the **Rosicrucians** and, where possible, to find one or several threads that might show the way through this labyrinth.

J. V. Andreä’s handwritten **autobiography** <note>original: *Lebensbeschreibung*; his *Vita*</note> is so important in every regard, both for the exploration of his character and for the origins of **Rosicrucianism** <note>original: *Rosenkreuzerey*</note>, that I must treat it in even greater detail. The copy located in the library at **Wolfenbüttel** contains several passages which are not found in my **Zeller copy** <note>The manuscript Nicolai purchased from the estate of Andreas Christoph Zeller</note>, whereas Pastor Burk’s copy is closer to the Wolfenbüttel one. In my collection, there is also a **Brief Account of the Life of Andreä** <note>original: *Breviarium Vitae Andreanae*</note>, **"selecting the most significant parts of his rhapsody"** <note>original: *potiora rhapsodiae ejus carptim libans*; referring to a condensed version of his life story or 'rhapsody'</note>, written very tightly in Andreä's own hand on several octavo pages, also from the one in Wolfenbüt- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword for 'Wolfenbüttel'</note>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s detailed comparison between his own manuscript of Johann Valentin Andreä’s autobiography and the version held in the library at Wolfenbüttel. Nicolai is analyzing discrepancies to understand Andreä’s original intentions.</meta>

[Wolfen]büttel, which were not written by Andreä himself, differ significantly. Already the title of my copy, written by Andreä's own hand, reads exactly as previously cited; in the Wolfenbüttel transcript, it reads: *A Brief Account of Andreä's Life, selecting the most important parts bit by bit* <note>original Latin: *Breviarium Vitae Andreanae potiora carptim libans*</note>. Since Andreä, when composing his own biography, clearly intended—as is easy to see—to present many of his dealings in a favorable light, it seems a little naive that he called it a "rhapsody" <note>In this context, a "rhapsody" refers to a miscellaneous collection of writings or fragments, though Nicolai suggests a double meaning of something "stitched together" or perhaps even fictionalized.</note> in his own handwritten draft, yet omitted this designation in the transcript he sent to his patron, **Duke August of Brunswick**. Could this humorous man—fully aware of why he was drafting his own life story and wishing to share it with others during his lifetime—have thought during his first draft that this biography should be, in the literal sense of the word rhapsody, a "little piece of poetry"? <note>Nicolai is questioning if Andreä was subtly admitting that his autobiography was partly a literary invention or "spun" narrative.</note> Yet, be that as it may; my manuscript of this *Brief Account* goes only until March 2nd of the year 1645, while the Wolfenbüttel version is continued until the year 1654. It is even more obvious, however, that it was continued by another person, since Andreä’s death on June 27th of that year and his subsequent burial are recounted therein. This is also evidenced by the signature: *As a token of owed gratitude, the spirit of Johann Valentin Andreä restores these things with a most sincere heart.* <note>original Latin: *Debitae gratitudinis ergo sincerissimo corde haec reponit Jo. Valent. Andreae genius*</note>. It is remarkable that in the handwritten *Brief Account*, several passages are crossed out which are nonetheless found in the Wolfenbüttel copy; from this, one might almost conclude that Andreä intended to write certain things only for Wolfenbüttel <note>Meaning, for the eyes of his patron, Duke August.</note> with a specific purpose in mind. It is a pity that of the detailed—

<meta>This page continues the quote from an anonymous scholar (possibly Nicolai himself or a contributor to his journal) regarding the motivations behind Johann Valentin Andreä’s autobiography. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing Andreä's tendency to let his imagination outrun his reason.</meta>

„...actually wrote it in order to become known, so that he might thereby be „excused for certain things that were rumored about him. From this point of view, „much is explained that would otherwise remain incoherent.“

These remarks by a most estimable man, who—due to both his extensive erudition and his knowledge of the world—is authorized to judge such matters, I have found thoroughly confirmed during my very earnest study of Andreä’s life and writings. Despite so many excellent qualities and talents, which I certainly revere in Andreä in every way, one cannot fail to recognize in him a certain urge to assert himself and to gain and maintain influence through various means; this becomes very evident, especially when one considers several circumstances of the history of that time. Throughout my life, I have known many brave and excellent men who possessed just such an inclination to assert themselves through closer connections, and thereby to have and maintain influence, even for the promotion of the good. They too brooded over the impossible, and thus often fell into similar embarrassments as Andreä, from which they knew how to extract themselves—as little as he did—through new but unsuitable means; and all the less so, the more good-natured, imaginative, and unfamiliar with the course of the world they were. If one does not lose sight of this perspective, it becomes understandable why Andreä seeks more to cover up than to explain various things in his autobiography <term>Lebensbeschreibung|Andreä's Latin autobiography, titled *Vita*, which was not published until long after his death</term>; why he says nothing at all therein of the *Fama* and the *Confession* <note>original: *Fama und Konfession*; referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and the *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the founding manifestos of the Rosicrucian movement</note>, and speaks of the *Chemical Wedding* <note>original: *Chymische Hochzeit*; referring to the *Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosenkreutz* (1616), an alchemical allegory Andreä later claimed he wrote as a youthful prank</note> only quite incidentally, intending to declare it, as it were, a mere joke, without in the least showing how...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Johann Valentin Andreä’s ambiguous relationship with the Rosicrucian manifestos. The previous page questioned why Andreä remained silent about the *Fama* and *Confessio* in his autobiography while admitting to writing the *Chymical Wedding*.</meta>

...for what prompted such a joke, and what its purpose was; for truly, without a special occasion and intention, one does not joke at such great length! That something hidden lay here is very clearly seen. The *Chymical Wedding*, printed in 1616, obviously refers to the *Fama* and *Confessio* printed in 1614. Why then does Andreä speak only of that first one, of which he confesses to be the author, and does not declare himself clearly regarding the latter ones, as to whether he is the author or not? This must surely have had a cause. In his *Tower of Babel* (1619, p. 69), he has the *Fama*—whom he introduces as a speaking character—say: "Aha, mortals! There is no reason for you to wait for the Brotherhood! The play is over. Rumor built it up; rumor tore it down. Rumor affirmed it; rumor denies it." <note>original: "Ehem, Mortales! nihil est, quod Fraternitatem expectetis! fabula peracta est. Fama exstruxit; fama destruxit. Fama ajebat; fama negat." In this context, Andreä is playing on the double meaning of "Fama" as both the title of the Rosicrucian manifesto and the personification of Rumor.</note> But we learn nothing of what entitled him to say: the play is over! <note>original: "fabula peracta est!"</note> On the following page, he has one of the speakers, Resipiscens <note>Latin: "One who comes to his senses," a character acting as a mouthpiece for Andreä’s own mature views</note>—who, as seen from the previous dialogues, actually voices Andreä’s own thoughts—say: "Therefore, although I indeed dismiss the society of the brotherhood itself [namely the <term>R. C.|Rosicrucians</term>], I shall nevertheless never abandon that true Christian brotherhood which smells of roses under the Cross, and separates itself as far as possible from the world's defilements, confusions, delusions, and vanities; but I aspire to enter into it with any pious, wise, and discerning person." <note>original: "Itaque ut fraternitatis ipsam societatem... quidem mitto, nunquam tamen veram Christianam fraternitatem, quae sub Cruce Rosas olet, et a mundi inquinamentis, confusionibus, deliriis, vanitatibusque se quam longissime segregat, dimisero; sed ad eam cum quovis pio, cordato, et sagace ineundam aspiro."</note> Thus, he speaks of the *Fraternitas R. C.* so ambiguously only to put his own *Fraternitas Christi* <note>The "Brotherhood of Christ," a real-world sociopolitical and religious reform group Andreä attempted to establish</note>, established just at that time, in its place. However, he explicitly calls this one a *Fraternitas Roseae Crucis* <note>Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross</note> as well; if, therefore, the former was only a comedy, then this one would have been nothing better. Since, however, he was certainly very serious about this one, he may also have [been serious] with...

<meta>This page continues the argument that Johann Valentin Andreä’s Rosicrucian writings were more than a literary "prank" and likely had a hidden political and religious purpose involving the Protestant resistance in Austria. It follows a quote from Andreä’s "Tower of Babel" where he distances himself from the movement while affirming the values of a "true Christian brotherhood."</meta>

...that he did not merely intend to play a **joke**. At the end (p. 72), Resipiscens <note>Latin: "One who comes to his senses," a character representing Andreä’s mature viewpoint</note> even wants to leave it uncertain whether the Rosicrucians ever really existed; he says: "Whether they exist, or whether they do not exist." <note>original: "Sive sint, sive non sint."</note>

I have already suggested in the first part of my *Attempt* (p. 168) that Andreä devised the Rosicrucian Society also with political intentions (or helped others to devise it).

Several circumstances strengthened me in the suspicion that the Rosicrucian Society initially related to the unrest in Austria regarding the religious oppression of the Protestants (of which I already gave a small hint in my *R. B.* Vol. VI, p. 485). I believe that the **journey** which Andreä took to Austria in the year 1612—just as **Matthias** <note>Matthias (1557–1619) was Holy Roman Emperor whose reign saw increasing tensions between Catholics and Protestants</note> had become Emperor—was closely connected to this, as was his second journey there in 1619, a few months after the death of this Emperor (who had toward the end been somewhat more mildly disposed toward the Protestants). In his place, the hard-hearted Ferdinand II <note>A staunch Catholic who sought to suppress Protestantism, sparking the Thirty Years' War</note> assumed the government, to whom the (mostly Protestant) Estates of the land above the Enns <note>The "Land ob der Enns" refers to the region of Upper Austria</note> refused to pay homage. Andreä undertook this journey, as he says himself in his own autobiography (see the German translation of this life by Prof. Seybold, pp. 89, 90), at the express invitation of the two Austrian Protestant barons **Hohenfeld von Aistersheim** and **Enenkel von Hoheneck**; here he admits himself to have carried out a secret commission in Linz, and lists a number of Austrian noblemen by name whom he spoke with on this journey, and who, as he explicitly adds, were all adherents of the "pure doctrine" <note>A common contemporary term for Lutheranism</note> (that is, Protestants). In this same autobiography, he says regarding the year 1622, concerning the [undertaking] started by him in 1619 (shortly before...

Thus: In the year 1622, some Austrian nobles were inclined to join the *Societas Christi* <note>Latin: "Society of Christ"</note>, which in any case was not merely "literary" <note>original: *literaria*</note>, and of which Andreä could not rightly say that the idea had been shared with only a few confidants, since he had already printed a public invitation to such a society in 1619. (By whom? Most likely by their government, which was so hostile toward the Protestants at that time!)

If there had been a reason for a warning earlier because of a former similar society in Austria, then such a solemn warning becomes more understandable; otherwise, no occasion for it can be seen at all. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Fraternitas Christi" and the confusion caused by its secrecy. But the initial intention of the **Rosicrucian Society** to support **Protestantism** did not last long. As is clearly evident from many circumstances, it was wrested from the hands of **Andreä** and his friends by cunning **Catholic Brothers**—who had managed to creep in soon after its formation, or perhaps even at its very inception—and was then turned toward the promotion of **Catholic interests**. There are various traces of this. I pointed them out in the second part of my *Essay* <note>original: *Versuchs*</note>, pages 181 and 209; and I will return to this again below, where I must refute Mr. Buhle's <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a scholar who wrote a famous history of the Rosicrucians and Freemasons</note> hollow claim: "that the Catholics were initially excluded from the Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross." 

Among these traces is the existence of certain **Knights of the Blue Cross**, about which I will say more in a specific note (31). Do we not have examples, even in our own times, of how the **Jesuits** very quickly forced their way—through their *familiares* <term>familiares: Lay associates or servants of the Jesuit order who lived in the world while secretly serving the order's goals</term>—into a **secret society** that was directly aimed against them (though admittedly with more weak goodwill than knowledge of the world and humanity)? They overthrew it after having tried, to some extent but without success, to turn it toward their own purposes through a few good-natured Protestant scholars. It is a quite common phenomenon in the affairs of secret societies, as well as in politics, that well-meaning people—especially young people (as **Andreä** was at that time)—who imagine they can create a sphere of influence through well-intentioned and plausible ideas, and perhaps even wish to lead others, are [manipulated] by shrewd, designing...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how secret societies can be manipulated by outside forces. It specifically addresses the vulnerability of idealistic reformers—like Johann Valentin Andreä—who may find their organizations "hijacked" by those with opposing agendas, specifically referring to the Catholic infiltration mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

...[purposes] to be used unnoticed for their own intentions, and they do not realize they are being ridden until they feel the bridle all too clearly; then they do not know whether or how they should throw off the rider, who seeks to trot along with them—not where they wish to go, but where he wills. Fortunate are they if, after being freed from the burdensome rider, another does not once again bridle and saddle them quite unnoticed, galloping away with them before they even realize it! 

In the 18th century, we also have examples of a considerable number of well-meaning people who, without knowing anything about it, were ridden excellently for a very long time by certain **cunning minds**. This happens all too easily, among others, to those people who, with the best of intentions, believe they can completely **reform** their century. Usually, they do not consider the great difficulties that will necessarily oppose their **cosmopolitan plans** <note>original: *weltbürgerlichen Planen*—plans for the betterment of all humanity regardless of borders</note>—obstacles such as deep-rooted **prejudices**, long-standing **abuses**, and the passions and agendas of those who are already in positions of power. Full of their **ideals**, they regard these great difficulties as insignificant things, believing they can cut through thick wooden blocks with their penknives (32) <note>This metaphor suggests that the reformers' tools—words and ideas—are too fragile to dismantle the "heavy timber" of established political and religious systems</note>.

From all this, we can explain why **Andreä** <note>Johann Valentin Andreä (1586–1654), a German theologian often linked to the origins of the Rosicrucian movement, though he later distanced himself from it</note>, as soon as he noticed that his and his friends' intentions for the **Rosicrucian Society** were no longer achievable—and indeed, that a completely opposite intention was being foisted upon them—could and would have nothing more to do with this society. He sought to erase the trail (though always in vague terms) of his ever having been involved with it (*Essay*, Part I, p. 176). By doing so, he wanted to avoid, on one hand, the Austrian...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Johann Valentin Andreä's struggles to navigate the political and religious tensions of the early 17th century. It follows the previous page’s mention of how his reformist plans were often "ridden" or manipulated by outside forces, specifically focusing on his attempt to distance himself from the Rosicrucian movement he helped create.</meta>

powerful House, and on the other hand, of becoming suspicious to the raging Lutheran Orthodox, which latter group heartily hated and persecuted him anyway because of his supposed inclination—they did not know quite whether to Calvinism (33) or to mysticism. Nevertheless, he could not avoid this: for partly his restless activity, and partly his good-natured delusion of achieving something excellent through the closer association of certain persons, drove him—as mentioned—to establish a **Brotherhood of Christ** <note>original: *Fraternitas Christi*</note> immediately after he renounced the **Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross** <note>original: *Fraternitas Roseae Crucis*—the Rosicrucians</note> in the year 1619. By this means, to all appearances, he wanted to wipe out the burdensome rumor that he had taken part in the notorious sect of the Rosicrucians, and to gather the well-meaning Lutheran **theosophists** <note>In this 17th-century context, "theosophists" refers to thinkers like Jacob Böhme or Johann Arndt who sought a direct, mystical knowledge of God through nature and scripture, rather than the 19th-century movement of the same name</note> (such as **Arndt** <note>Johann Arndt (1555–1621), a famous Lutheran theologian whose mystical writings were deeply influential but often viewed with suspicion by strict church authorities</note> and others) under his banner (see *Attempt* <note>original: *Versuch*—likely referring to the author's own work, *Attempt at a History of the Rosicrucians*</note>, Vol. II, pp. 175–176), and perhaps to accomplish something else besides.

However, he accomplished nothing, even though his new **Brotherhood** <note>original: *Fraternitas*</note> came into being. Instead—aside from the suspicion that he also held secret (34) and perhaps even political intentions with this society—he once again voluntarily exposed himself to new slanders and persecutions from the rigid Orthodox, who considered such a theosophical-looking society detrimental to pure doctrine and sectarian. This character of restless activity is just as clearly evident from **Andreä’s** life and writings as is his satirical mood and the noble frankness with which he attacked the follies and abuses of his time—though he also drew upon himself a multitude of adversaries, about which he often complains bitterly enough. On the other hand, it was also quite natural that the Rosicrucian society, once it was no longer in the hands of Andreä and his friends (see my *Attempt*, Part I, p. 176), now [took] very different paths...

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s analysis of Johann Valentin Andreä’s "Brotherhood of Christ" and the broader Rosicrucian phenomenon. The text picks up mid-sentence, describing how Andreä’s efforts were interpreted or "taken up" by various followers and opportunists.</meta>

...took <note>The text begins mid-word, completing a thought from the previous page regarding the "shape" or "direction" Andreä’s efforts took.</note>, depending on whether this or that person found something in the obscure words and allusions that suited their own concepts, or whether someone noticed more or less of the new intentions, or even substituted their own intentions. All these phantoms of **Rosicrucians** <term>Rosenkreuzer: members of a legendary spiritual and cultural movement that arose in early 17th-century Germany</term> were not infrequently reshaped in various ways by the events of the public world, and finally disappeared bit by bit, either through their own insignificance or through mutual hatred and envy—whereby one usually did not quite know what exactly they were envying each other for. And so perhaps only that system lasted the longest which had been established early on with so much cunning and consistency. The history of the **secret societies** of the 18th century provides a parallel to what I believe to have found regarding the Rosicrucians in the first half of the 17th century. One notices that public quarrels over such secret societies are rarely detrimental to their progress; on the contrary, passions are aroused by them, and cold reflection and calm examination are removed, so that now everyone, with a heated imagination, insists all the more zealously on their opinion the more obscurely the object gleams—an object which they imagine they see in a bright light with a believing eye. This is likely how it went with the old Rosicrucians as well, of which clear traces can be found in the printed writings.

I must accept being outright laughed at by **Mr. Buhle** <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a scholar known for his skeptical history of the Rosicrucians and Freemasons, whom the author is here addressing as a critic</note> and those like him as I share here a few results of my investigations into the Rosicrucians; this is because I cannot include any proofs here, and even less can I present these results in detail in connection with a multitude of other observations, through which they [would gain] such a high degree of **probability**...

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his historical methodology and his access to primary sources. He is arguing that his conclusions are not mere speculation but are based on a rigorous comparison of historical events and a vast collection of original Rosicrucian documents.</meta>

Those who know me more personally will be convinced in any case (and I hope my other writings prove it) that I have never boasted of having conducted investigations that I had not actually performed.

I personally possess a fair number of writings concerning the **Rosicrucians** <term>Rosenkreuzer: a legendary esoteric brotherhood said to possess secret wisdom, which became a sensation in the early 17th century</term>, and I sought them out from all the local public and private libraries; especially in the year 1783, when I was writing my second volume, I obtained many more through the friendship of scholars abroad. I may therefore well say that, for at least thirty years, no one has had such a complete collection of such books before them as I did then. This becomes clear to me when I compare the annotated catalog I made at that time against the deficient list in Mr. von Murr's <note>Christoph Gottlieb von Murr (1733–1811) was a prominent historian and polymath who often sparred with contemporary scholars over the origins of secret societies.</note> little work *On the Origin of the Rosicrucians and Freemasons* <term>Freymaurer: members of the fraternal organization of Freemasonry</term> (1803, octavo <note>The "8." in the original text refers to "octavo," a book size where the printed sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note>), a book which is highly incomplete and, like all the writings of this compiler, very wrongly and haphazardly cobbled together without the slightest critical judgment. I have not allowed myself to be discouraged from carefully going through all the writings concerning the Rosicrucians that came to my knowledge, which is already proven by the many reports I have cited from the most prominent among them. Through this, it also became possible for me, in the first part of my *Essay* (p. 177 ff.), to categorize the Rosicrucian writings to some extent into five

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion of his research into the Rosicrucians. Having previously mentioned his extensive collection of books, he now details how he organized these works into specific categories to correct the "peculiar" disorder left by earlier historians.</meta>

...to divide them into classes, since all writers of this kind had been thrown together in the most peculiar way by my predecessors. Afterwards, further investigations into this classification led me to clues of various kinds. In particular, I should have added two more classes: namely, first, the class of the **Seekers**, that is, those who would have liked to know something about the **Rosicrucians** <term>Rosenkreuzer: a legendary esoteric brotherhood that claimed secret wisdom and became a European sensation in the early 17th century</term> without having any knowledge of them (35), or those who had a desire to join them without really understanding the "what" and "how," and without knowing where to turn. Many similar writings about **Freemasonry** <term>Freymaurerey: a fraternal organization that evolved from stonemasons' guilds, often associated with secret rituals and symbols in the 18th century</term> can be found in the 18th century. 

Following this, a seventh class should have been cited: namely those writings in which, under a Rosicrucian cover, political ideas and allusions to the history of the era of the **Thirty Years' War** <note>The Thirty Years' War (1618–1648) was a massive European conflict that began as a religious war in Bohemia and escalated into a continental struggle for power.</note> are found. I did not want to say anything about it at the time, because I was not yet as certain within myself about certain things as I am now. However, in the second volume, page 213, I gave a hint and had Figure II engraved in copper to see if anyone would attempt an explanation. But no one noticed this image, just as little as they noticed the even more remarkable Figure I. 

I will briefly explain the image in Figure II here. There, one beholds the sun and moon, which flow together into a chalice—which is well known as the symbol of the Christian religion. To show that this causes all movement, the figure of **Mercury**, the **vivifying force** <note>original: "vis vivificans"; referring to the life-giving or animating power in alchemical philosophy</note> (precisely what is indicated in **Freemasonry** by the **blazing star**: see my *Essay*, Vol. II, p. 214), is explicitly placed under the chalice. It is, after all, well known that the Bohemian War—and from it, the **Thirty Years' War**—[began] because of the chalice in the Lord's Supper <note>The text ends mid-sentence. The author is referring to the Utraquist controversy, where Bohemian Protestants demanded the "cup" or chalice be given to the laity during communion, a key spark of the conflict.</note>

<meta>This page continues the author’s detailed description of a symbolic engraving related to the Rosicrucians, specifically focusing on the alchemical and religious symbols found within the earth's interior as depicted in the image. It transitions from his analysis of Rosicrucianism to his research into Freemasonry.</meta>

...Lord’s Supper was created. Around this image is written: **Visit the interior of the earth; by rectifying, you will find the hidden stone** <note>original: "Visita Interiora Terrae, Rectificando Invenies Occultum Lapidem." This is a famous alchemical motto whose initials spell "VITRIOL," referring to both a chemical substance and a process of internal purification.</note> (36). Thus, one is meant to investigate the innermost parts of the earth. And what does one find there? The **Imperial Orb** <term>Reichsapfel: a globe-shaped piece of regalia topped with a cross, symbolizing secular power over the Christian world</term>, the double-headed **Imperial Eagle** <term>Reichsadler: the heraldic bird representing the Holy Roman Empire</term>, the **Bohemian Lion** <term>böhmischer Löwe: the heraldic symbol of the Kingdom of Bohemia, traditionally depicted with two tails</term> with its double tail; and between them at the very bottom, a seven-pointed star, which actually sets everything in motion, just like Mercury does above. Now, the number seven is well known to be sacred to the Catholic religion. It has seven sacraments, seven orders of ordination, and so on. Next to the seven-pointed star, one sees small pieces on a ring, which are presumably intended to suggest bread or wafers, and two barrel staves opposite them; this refers to the two components of the **Lord's Supper** <term>Abendmahl: the Christian ritual of the Eucharist or Holy Communion</term>, bread and wine.

If I had another life to live, or even now possessed two healthy eyes and the leisure, I believe I would be capable—by virtue of my previous reading and the many additions and confirmations found over the last 23 years—of publicly explaining the history of the **Rosicrucians** <term>Rosenkreuzer: a secret society of mystics and alchemists whose manifestos in the early 1600s sparked a massive intellectual movement</term>, which is still so obscure, from the manifold contemporary writings with citations of supporting evidence, so that one might at least see more clearly than before; though the question would certainly remain whether many readers would thank the author for such laborious work and follow him with patience and attention through such a detailed and necessarily dry investigation. In any case, I can no longer achieve more in this regard than I have already achieved.

But I move on now. After I had fairly exhausted what was possible for me to investigate regarding Rosicrucianism, as far as my strength allowed, I moved on to **Freemasonry** <term>Freymaurerey: a fraternal organization that evolved from stonemasons' guilds, often associated with secret rituals and esoteric symbolism during the Enlightenment</term>. I had personal knowledge of it in various ways; also the opportunity to [speak] with men...

<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of his research methods and his transition into the study of Freemasonry. It follows his previous mention of having access to unique resources and individuals who assisted his historical investigations.</meta>

...who possessed great experience in various branches of the same, to associate closely for many years. Through these individuals, I obtained a considerable number of important handwritten accounts and original official documents <term>Aktenstücke: formal administrative, legal, or historical records</term>, which are seldom accessible; some were for reading and excerpting, others were copies for my own possession. In this way, it became easier for me to better understand and more accurately judge printed writings, and especially to trace the sources from which each might have originated.

I then read and examined all printed writings on Freemasonry <term>Freymaurerey: an older German spelling of the term</term> in all languages (37) <note>This likely refers to a specific footnote or bibliographic entry in the original manuscript.</note> that I could possibly obtain. Through this, I was enabled to distinguish those which deserve attention—whether for good or ill—from those which are little more than empty scribbling, and to focus primarily on the former. 

I was, and still am, convinced that nothing of significance which appears secret can be entirely without connection to that which is publicly known. A secret society <term>geheime Gesellschaft: a private organization that keeps its rituals, membership, or internal hierarchy hidden from the public</term>, especially when "Unknown Superiors" <term>Unbekannte Obern: a specific term in 18th-century Masonry, particularly within the 'Strict Observance' rite, referring to mysterious leaders whose identities were kept secret even from high-ranking members</term> wish to enforce their intentions, still aims to influence its members, and perhaps occasionally others as well. Yet these members all live in the public, real world and are never separated from it; their public lives and their lives within secret societies can never remain entirely without mutual influence. 

It must therefore be that the origin of such societies, their remarkable changes, their division into various branches, and the like, either have their causes in public human and civil society <term>bürgerliche Gesellschaft: the organized social and political world of the general citizenry</term>, or must exert a noticeable influence upon it. To trace this mutual connection, I consider to be the primary duty of anyone who [investigates] the history of such a society...

<summary>The author describes his methodology for researching Freemasonry, emphasizing the use of rare manuscripts and the necessary connection between secret societies and the public, "civil" world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of his research methodology, specifically how he deciphers the "allusions" and symbolic connections within secret society records to trace their shared origins and historical development.</meta>

aims to investigate; and thus I worked with the secrets I had learned and personally experienced. I further reflected that when subjects are declared secret, and therefore what is supposed to look like an explanation remains obscure, there must nevertheless be some allusions present to which this or that refers. I was all the more diligent in seeking to find the key because, in the multitude of official documents <term>Aktenstücke: formal historical, administrative, or legal records</term> lying before me—however complicated or confused they might seem at first glance—it was nevertheless evident that the principal ones shared a common **origin** <note>original: "Ursprung"</note>, so that in branches of extremely different divisions, one always returns to certain ideas of the same kind.

Already during my first investigations into the German Rosicrucians <term>Rosenkreuzer: a mystical secret society originating in the early 17th century, focusing on spiritual alchemy and esoteric Christianity</term>, and while distinguishing between their various types, I had come upon the trail of cipher characters <term>Chifferzeichen: secret symbols or coded writing systems used to conceal sensitive information</term> in one particular branch of Rosicrucians, through which this or that seemed to be indicated. These ciphers were not found in the other branches. I pursued this further, and lo and behold! Among the Freemasons in England, this very cipher was found from the beginning; but, what is very remarkable, it was not found among the oldest English Rosicrucians. This trace of a notable difference, along with many other reasons, first showed me that while the Freemasons arose from the Rosicrucians, they were by no means entirely identical to them from the start. This contradicts the current claim of Court Councilor Buhle <note>Johann Gottfried Buhle (1763–1821), a German philosopher and historian who famously argued that Freemasonry was a direct offshoot of Rosicrucianism</note>, who is always accustomed to boldly drawing conclusions about the whole from the first available individual idea he finds, without looking left or right to see if other circumstances—which one must not ignore—present difficulties and force us to abandon a premise, or at least to restrict it significantly.

<summary>The author describes his method of using recurring "keys" and ciphers to trace the shared origins of secret societies, specifically arguing that while Freemasonry evolved from Rosicrucianism, the two were distinct groups with different symbolic systems.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s explanation of his research methodology. ...al <note>This completes the word "identical" from the previous page.</note> with people who possessed great experience in various branches of these societies, interacting with them intimately for many years. Through them, I obtained a significant number of important handwritten reports and original official documents <term>Aktenstücke: formal historical, administrative, or legal records</term> that are only rarely accessible—partly for reading and excerpting, and partly as copies for my own collection. In this way, it became easier for me to better understand and more accurately judge even printed writings, and especially to trace the sources <term>Quellen: the original materials, documents, or primary evidence used for historical research</term> from which each might have originated. I then read and examined every printed work on Freemasonry <term>Freymaurerey: the practices and symbols of the fraternal order of Free and Accepted Masons</term> in every language ($^{37}$) <note>The number refers to a citation or footnote in the original manuscript.</note> that I could possibly obtain. Through this, I was enabled to distinguish those works which deserve attention—whether for their merits or their faults—from those which are little more than empty scribbling, and to focus my efforts primarily on the former.

I was, and still am, convinced that no matter of importance that presents itself as secret can be entirely without connection to what is also publicly known. A secret society <term>geheime Gesellschaft: an organization that conceals its rituals, purposes, or membership from the general public</term>, especially when "Unknown Superiors" <note>original: "Unbekannte Obern." This was a controversial concept in 18th-century high-degree Masonry, where members were told they were being guided by hidden leaders whose identities remained a mystery.</note> seek to carry out their intentions, still aims to influence its members, and perhaps occasionally others as well. However, these members all live in the public, real world; they are never separated from it. Their public lives and their lives within secret societies can never remain entirely without reciprocal effect. It cannot be otherwise: the emergence of such societies, their remarkable changes, their division into various branches, and the like, must either have their causes in public human and civil society, or they must exert a noticeable influence upon it. To trace this reciprocal connection is what I consider the first duty of anyone who [wishes to investigate] the history <term>Geschichte: the chronological record and analysis of the development of a specific subject</term> of such a society

I was all the more eager to find the key, since in the mass of documents <term>Aktenstücke: formal historical, administrative, or legal records</term> lying before me, however complex or confused they might seem at first glance, the most important ones clearly had a common origin. This was evident in the way that, even in branches of extremely different divisions, certain ideas of the same kind were always returned to.

Already during my first investigations into the German Rosicrucians <term>Rosenkreuzer: a secret esoteric society said to have been founded in the late medieval period</term>, and while distinguishing between their various types, I had come across the trail of cipher signs <term>Chifferzeichen: secret alphabets or symbolic codes used to conceal messages</term> in one particular branch of Rosicrucians, through which this or that seemed to be hinted at. Among the Freemasons <term>Freymaurer: members of the fraternal order of Freemasonry</term> in England, this very same cipher was found from the beginning; but, what is very remarkable, it was not found among the oldest English Rosicrucians. This trace of a noticeable difference, along with many other reasons, showed me for the first time that while the Freemasons did emerge from the Rosicrucians, they were by no means completely identical to them from the start. --- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s comparative study of secret societies. He is specifically contrasting the symbolic evidence—ciphers and allegorical languages—found in Freemasonry versus those in Rosicrucianism to establish a historical timeline and lineage.</meta>

The aforementioned cipher <term>Chiffer: a secret system of writing or symbolic code used to hide meanings from outsiders</term> is found, however, in all Freemasonic rituals, and in very many writings and documents <term>Aktenstücke: formal historical, administrative, or legal records</term>, as far back as one can go—approximately until the year 1685 or slightly earlier (38)—up to the present time, serving as clear evidence of the uninterrupted continuity of at least one main branch. I also discovered, bit by bit, a second cipher which, together with the one just mentioned, can be traced back to quite old times, and of which just as little is to be found among either the German or English Rosicrucians. Finally, traces of an allegorical language were also found among the Freemasons <note>The author distinguishes between "ciphers" (the alphabet/signs) and "allegorical language" (the symbolic way of speaking or writing about concepts).</note>, which through several comparisons and juxtapositions was finally so clearly developed that no doubt could remain regarding its correct explanation, whereby, of course, much appeared completely clear. These two allegorical languages <note>original: "zwey allegorische Sprachen"; the author likely refers here to the two systems of symbolic communication derived from the ciphers previously mentioned.</note>, along with constant allusions to their secret interpretation, are found once again in all branches of Freemasonry, from the oldest times known up to the present day, quite consistently; whereas in the old Rosicrucianism, there is almost nothing of it: at most in one division, from which I believe Freemasonry gradually emerged, a faint trace of it can be found. This seems to prove that certain ideas, which were generally established much later, were not yet present so purely and consistently in the beginning.

In this way, I conducted my investigations, always paying attention to whether doubts or difficulties arose, and I either sought to clear them away or abandoned my premise. Hereafter, every impartial reader may decide whether this is not the only rational <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the German word "vernünftige" is completed by the catchword below.</note>

<meta>catchword: rational [vernünftige]</meta>

<meta>The previous page discussed the author's discovery of specific ciphers and an allegorical language found within Freemasonry that were absent in Rosicrucianism, suggesting a distinct historical evolution for the former.</meta>

...was the rational way to decipher secret writings <term>geheime Schriften: coded or esoteric texts intended only for initiates</term>, and through the comparison of several parts thereof with publicly known history, to investigate the true connection of a society that, under many various minor changes, has nevertheless always remained fundamentally the same and has now continued for over 160 years <note>Given the author's timeframe, this 160-year period likely dates the society back to the early 17th century, around the time the first Rosicrucian manifestos appeared.</note>.

These investigations finally granted me a conviction in all their parts that was subjectively sufficient for me; yet I have also found that very sensible and experienced men have been convinced by it. Specifically, after the second part of my *Essay* <note>The author likely refers to his work *Versuch über die Beschaffenheit der alten Freimaurergesellschaft* (Essay on the Nature of the Old Masonic Society), published in the early 1780s.</note> was finished, I attempted to set down in a coherent way the sequence of my ideas regarding the origin and nature of the old Rosicrucianism <term>Rosenkreuzerey: an esoteric movement focused on alchemy and mysticism, which some believe preceded Freemasonry</term>, its first transition to Freemasonry, and the primary changes to the latter in both the 17th and 18th centuries, adding the supporting evidence. The essay was only very hastily composed, as I had only a few days to devote to it; its primary purpose was simply to make the sequence of my ideas—which were then vividly present in my mind because I had been required to read and research so much for a time—easier and quicker to retrieve if necessary. After further experience of more than 20 years, and from my notebooks <term>Kollektaneen: a personal collection of notes, excerpts, and research materials</term> compiled only occasionally, I could execute the subjects treated in this manuscript much better, if I had the inclination or leisure to do so. This essay, imperfect as it is, I gradually allowed several sensible men, highly experienced in the higher degrees of quite different branches of Freemasonry, to read in my presence, with the request that they never make use of its content—

...to make it available to them, but to judge it strictly and to share their thoughts with me candidly, so that I might perhaps come even closer to the truth. Everyone fully agreed with the essential points of my discoveries <term>Entdeckungen: the author's specific findings regarding the historical links and shared symbols between secret societies</term> and found the provided evidence <term>Beweise: the historical documents and cipher comparisons mentioned on the previous page</term> convincing; indeed, from some individuals, I received very interesting contributions and confirmations. 

Even the doubts, which experience and a love of truth suggested to some, proved instructive to me. I would have continued to be useful to myself and others through this private, friendly sharing of my—albeit very imperfect—essay, had I not been moved by the irresponsible misuse (39) <note>The number (39) likely refers to a specific footnote or an instance of unauthorized distribution or plagiarism that the author encountered, which soured his willingness to share his work.</note> that someone made of it. This led me to the decision to share neither this essay nor anything similar with anyone else ever again. 

I was further confirmed in this resolution as I realized how necessary it would be to completely rework this manuscript <term>Handschrift: literally "hand-writing," referring here to the unpublished draft</term> if it were to achieve the level of completeness that would match the greater knowledge I had gained through further research and confirmations. However, I found myself less and less able to undertake such a revision <term>Umarbeitung: a thorough rewriting or updating of the text</term>, as I later returned to investigations of this kind almost not at all, or only in passing. The time I had left over from my burdensome business duties was, in any case, scarcely enough for other studies <term>Studien: his broader intellectual pursuits beyond the history of secret societies</term> that were more pleasant and useful to me.

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues from the author’s description of his decision to stop sharing his private research due to misuse. He now begins to explain the motivations behind his previously published work, specifically his "Versuch" (Essay) published twenty-three years prior to this writing, and the specific historical myths he aimed to debunk.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>
<sig>D 2</sig>

Here I have now somewhat indicated the thread by which my investigations were guided. Twenty-three years ago, in the two appendices of my *Essay* <term>Versuch: referring to his published work, likely the "Versuch über die Freimaurerei" (Essay on Freemasonry) published in 1782</term>, I only made a portion of this known—not to write a history of Freemasonry (for, as seen above on page 26, that cannot be done), nor to gain fame, but to be of use. I wished to work against very gross prejudices and delusions <term>Schwärmereyen: a common 18th-century term for religious or mystical fanaticism and irrational beliefs</term> by discovering the true origin in that indirect and gentle manner which is most appropriate for a public announcement. Even if doubts could still be raised against some of my assertions—despite all the care I took to assert nothing of which I had not convinced myself through impartial combination and examination of several reports—it did not matter so much for my purpose at the time, which was to stimulate beneficial reflection within a large society that was then being led astray by deceivers <term>Betrüger: likely referring to charlatans like Cagliostro or leaders of the Strict Observance who claimed secret ancient lineages</term>.

Suffice it to say, I showed that the origin of Freemasonry is not to be sought earlier than the first half of the 17th century. Through this, it must have become clear to the thinking Freemason that the origin was to be found neither in a secretly continued Order of the Temple <note>original: "Tempelherrenorden"; referring to the popular myth that Freemasons were the direct successors of the medieval Knights Templar</note>, nor with the Archbishop of Nicosia in Cyprus, nor among the Canons of the Holy Sepulchre (4°) <note>original: "Canonicis Sti Sepulcri"</note> in Jerusalem, nor among the *Knights of the Holy City* <note>original: "Chevaliers de la Cité Sainte"</note>, nor among the Unknown Superiors <term>Unbekannten Obern: the secret, high-ranking leaders claimed by the "Strict Observance" branch of Freemasonry</term>, those Fathers resting in God. In the second part of my *Essay*, pages 163 to 165, I clearly indicated that this was my intention. Anyone who does not realize that in the year 1782, this was an unheard-of assertion which no one had yet dared to make, and that it certainly [affected] those who did not merely brood in the dark... <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break.</note>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that early Freemasonry was a hybrid of scientific inquiry and existing trade guild structures, specifically highlighting the involvement of figures like Robert Fludd and Elias Ashmole.</meta>

we also know well enough that Fludd <note>Robert Fludd (1574–1637) was an English Paracelsian physician and mystic whose work bridged the gap between early science and occult philosophy.</note>, who wrote so much mystical material, nevertheless also made known several physical and anatomical truths in his writings that were quite important, especially for that time. Such was the nature of that era. In general, people of very different mindsets can very well unite for a single purpose if it is important to them; it is, rather, a very happy coincidence when people who are otherwise burdened with heavy prejudices enter into association with open-minded people. Sound reason develops only gradually, but one is on the best path toward it when one begins to combine reflection with experience through social effort and the exchange of thoughts.

2) I first traced the oldest public historical record of **Freemasonry**—namely, that the well-known antiquary **Elias Ashmole**, who was at the same time a zealous **Rosicrucian**, was admitted as a **Freemason** in the year 1646, specifically in **Warrington** in **Lancashire** (44). In **London**, the lodge was later held in the Guildhall of the Masons. (See my *Essay*, Part I, pp. 191, 193, and Part II, p. 192.) This was a sign that the members of this society were now joining the **Masons' guild**; for in England, as is well known, it is necessary for anyone who wishes to have the rights of a citizen in London to join one of the guilds, without it being necessary to actually practice the trade of that guild. From this arose the name <term>Freemason</term> <note>The author explains that the "Free" in Freemason refers to being "Free of the Company"—having the full legal rights and privileges of a guild member.</note>, for in the English language, "Free" refers to someone who is a member of a guild. Another proof is that the Great Lodge of Freemasons in London has the very same coat of arms as the Masons' Guild there, and that every working mason, when he is admitted as a Freemason, [pays] only half of the

<summary>The author explains how intellectuals and mystics like Robert Fludd and Elias Ashmole integrated into the London Masons' Guild, arguing that the term "Freemason" derives from the legal status of guild membership rather than the trade itself.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Fludd, Elias Ashmole, Rosicrucians, Freemasonry, Warrington, London Guilds, etymology of Freemason, history of Freemasonry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the historical transition of Rosicrucian thought into the early structures of Freemasonry, specifically focusing on the influence of English Royalists and the evolution of Masonic symbolism.</meta>

...pays fees. The fact that most of the first members of the society belonged to the **Royalist Party** <note>Supporters of the monarchy during the English Civil War and the subsequent Restoration period.</note> was, of course, accidental, but it later had a significant influence on the purpose and the manifold transformations of the society. At least, that is how I envisioned the matter.

I assumed for several reasons that this **Rosicrucian** society—which, when it established itself in London, called itself the **Society of Freemasons** after its meeting place there—possessed an outward symbolic form quite similar to that seen in the first two degrees of modern **Freemasonry** (see Part 1 of my *Essay*, pp. 192, 193). However, it was not identical, as at that time it only made use of symbols representing the "order of the works of creation" (see the same, p. 198). It possessed secrets insofar as the Rosicrucians of that era, and even **Bacon** in his *Atlantis* (for his **House of Solomon** <note>A fictional research institution described in Francis Bacon's utopian work *New Atlantis*, dedicated to the study of nature and science.</note>), demanded an oath of secrecy (see the same, p. 202). The symbols that were unique to these **Rosicrucian Freemasons** before they adopted **Solomon’s Temple** as a general symbol around the year 1682 were, in my opinion: the **sun**, the **moon**, the **flaming star**, the **compasses**, the **square**, the **level**, and **Jacob’s ladder** with three steps (45). These belong to the symbols of natural philosophy and are therefore found among the Rosicrucians, unlike the symbols adopted later by the Freemasons which belong exclusively to them.

3) **Ashmole** was a zealous supporter of the **Royalist Party**, as is sufficiently clear from his biography. Now, I asserted that, along with Ashmole’s like-minded...
