<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the political origins of Masonic degrees, specifically how English Royalists used the guise of Rosicrucianism as a cover for secret meetings during the interregnum period following the execution of Charles I.</meta>

...friends, after the year 1649, once King **Charles I** had been executed, a division met privately in secret under the pretext of **Rosicrucian Freemasonry**, in order to deliberate on the loss of the monarchy and their wishes for the restoration of **Charles II** (46). I assume that the symbols of the **Rosicrucians** would have been used as little in this secret committee as the symbols of **Freemasonry**—which was founded later, around 1682, based on the building of **Solomon’s Temple**—for in this secret committee, there was mention of neither Rosicrucianism nor Freemasonry. I am, however, of the opinion that the symbolic expressions they used orally at that time bore some resemblance to the symbols commonly used in the third division <note>original: "Abtheilung," here referring to the "Master Mason" degree of initiation.</note> of modern Freemasonry. But whoever understands what belongs there, and reads page 201 of the first part of my *Essay* attentively, will not find all the current symbols there. Could I explain myself more clearly, this would become even more evident. If one wished—as **Mr. B.** does (who confuses everything by placing the image of Solomon's Temple much earlier than it actually appeared) on page 359 ff.—to seek a degree of Freemasonry in this secret agreement, and to imagine a **slain Hiram** <note>Hiram Abiff, the legendary architect of Solomon's Temple, whose death forms the central myth of the Masonic Master's degree.</note> and the like even then, the difficulties would become insoluble, especially when one carefully compares this with public history.

4) I assumed that the **Royalists** <note>original: "Königlichgesinnte," refers to supporters of the monarchy during the English Civil War.</note>, around the year 1659, for some reason—presumably because one of their associates had become suspicious to them—established yet another second special division

...they formed a division <note>original: "Abtheilung," here referring to a specific group or degree of secret association.</note> in which only the most secret or Scottish affairs (47) occurred, because at that time the eyes of the Royalists were mainly fixed on the army which **General Monck** <note>George Monck (1608–1670), a key military figure whose march from Scotland to London was instrumental in the restoration of Charles II.</note> was leading down from **Scotland**.

5) I maintained, and still maintain, that "after the political business of the former society had ended through the restoration of the royal dignity," and the **Rosicrucian** physical <note>original: "physikalischen," referring to the natural philosophy, alchemy, and "secret sciences" pursued by early Rosicrucians.</note> ideas had become outdated—and therefore neither held any special interest for anyone anymore (48)—a change occurred in the organization of the society, and only then was the **Temple of Solomon** chosen for its outward symbolic representations <note>original: "Sinnbildern."</note>. I placed the time of this change around the year 1685. I have already mentioned above on page 48 that I have since found reasons to believe it may have happened somewhat earlier, perhaps in the year 1682. It cannot be expected of me to explain publicly what primarily caused this change. The reason why this cannot happen is sufficiently developed above on page 26. I said in the first part of my *Essay* <note>original: "Versuch," referring to Nicolai’s work *Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of the Knights Templar and its Secret Content*.</note> on page 213: "To discuss the closer intentions behind this change is not at all my purpose here." I say the same here now.

Naturally, with this change in outward form, the visual signs—which were retained from the previous Rosicrucian symbol of a **"Solomonic House"** <note>A reference to the "House of Solomon" from Francis Bacon's *New Atlantis*, which Nicolai argues influenced early Rosicrucianism before the transition to Masonic Temple imagery.</note>—also had to be given a new interpretation relating to it, now that a general new visual representation of the **Temple of Solomon** had been adopted for the whole. Even new symbols had to be added. Only then did the later symbolic representations arise, for example, the **three windows**. In a

<summary>Nicolai argues that the transition from Rosicrucianism to modern Freemasonry occurred around 1682–1685, shifting from political intrigue and alchemy to the moral allegory of Solomon's Temple.</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicolai's argument regarding the distinct origins of Rosicrucian and Masonic symbolism. He is specifically contrasting the "building" of the Rosicrucians with the Temple of Solomon used by Freemasons.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

In the German Rosicrucian book printed in 1618, it is explicitly stated concerning the construction of the Rosicrucians: "It is a building lacking windows and doors" <note>original Latin: "carens fenestris et foribus." This phrase emphasizes the internal, hermetic nature of Rosicrucian spiritual work.</note>. This was, therefore, a completely different structure than the Masonic Temple of Solomon, in which the three windows (in whose place three lights are now set in many locations) have a very important significance, and in which, as is well known, there is also talk of three gates or entrances. This is further proof that the Rosicrucian building did not take the Temple of Solomon as its model at all. It was only at that time <note>referring to the late 17th-century transition mentioned on the previous page</note> that the actual Masonic symbols <note>original: "Sinnbilder"</note> first emerged; namely, in addition to the three windows, the representations of the rough and hewn stone <note>often called the "ashlar," representing the moral development of a person</note>, the trowel, the drafting board <note>or "tracing board," used by a master to draw designs</note>, the so-called indented tassel (49) <note>a decorative border featuring tassels, often found on Masonic carpets or boards</note>, the skull, and what belongs with it, the cassia <note>likely referring to "Acacia," a plant symbolizing the immortality of the soul in Masonic ritual</note>, and so forth.

Of all these, no mention is ever made regarding the construction of the Rosicrucians; although the greater part of these symbols can be found long before in other writings where figurative representations occur, so that it can be proven from where the founders of Freemasonry took them. Only then were the two pillars also designated with **J** and **B** <note>referring to Jachin and Boaz, the two bronze pillars which stood at the porch of Solomon's Temple</note> (initially as **B** and **J**), which is not found on the pillars seen in Rosicrucian writings (50).

---

These were my assertions. If one wishes to regard them as mere hypotheses, I can accept that, despite the fact that for me they are more than hypotheses; for through manifold investigations I have encountered a great number of traces which could hardly coincide so perfectly by mere chance.

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai's defense of his historical theories. so that one always explains the other more closely. Meanwhile, I gladly leave everyone to their own beliefs regarding Rosicrucianism and Freemasonry, especially since, for the reasons mentioned above, I cannot and will not publicly present my investigations on the matter in their full context. But when a man like Mr. Buhle <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821) was a German philosopher who famously argued that Freemasonry was a direct offshoot of Rosicrucianism, though he and Nicolai disagreed on the specifics of how and when this happened.</note> formally opposes me, and behaves most clumsily <note>original: "links," literally "left-handedly," implying awkwardness or lack of skill</note> in doing so, I shall be allowed to show that at least my assertions are not invalidated at all by his refutation, even if some other objections might still be raised against them. If Mr. B. now puts completely unfounded hypotheses in place of mine, and even passes off his dreams as established **truths**, then, once I show their poor quality, impartial readers may judge whether the results of my own investigations—even as they stand publicly and in isolation—do not gain even more probability.

Mr. Court Councilor Buhle explicitly boasts (in his preface, pages VII and VIII) that he has refuted all my hypotheses; therefore, he must have refuted all the above-mentioned ones as well. We shall see!

---

The first and most essential hypothesis, which I made public as many as 23 years ago <note>Nicolai refers to his 1782 work "Essay on the Accusations against the Order of the Knights Templar," where he first proposed his theory on the origins of Freemasonry.</note>, is:

that the Freemasonic society arose from the Rosicrucian society around the middle of the 17th century, and that the former is not older than the latter.

This hypothesis of mine—if it is indeed to be called a hypothesis—Mr. Buhle has not refuted; rather, he has tried to stealthily give himself the appearance of having made this discovery himself before he even read my book.

<summary>Friedrich Nicolai defends his theory that Freemasonry originated from Rosicrucianism in the mid-17th century, accusing his critic Johann Gottlieb Buhle of failing to refute his claims and essentially plagiarizing the discovery.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Nicolai, Johann Gottlieb Buhle, Rosicrucianism, Freemasonry, 17th century, historical hypothesis, academic dispute</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s polemic against Johann Gottlieb Buhle. Nicolai is further refining his timeline of when specific symbols, particularly Solomon's Temple, entered Masonic ritual, arguing they were not present in the earlier Rosicrucian tradition.</meta>

My second assertion and the most essential qualification of my discovery regarding the origin of the Masonic society <term>Freymaurergesellschaft; the organized body of Freemasons</term> is:

> that the construction of Solomon’s Temple only became the general symbol of the Freemasons around the year 1682.

Without this necessary qualification, which I clearly presented 23 years ago in the first part of my *Essay* <note>Nicolai is referring to his work *Versuch über die Beschuldigungen, welche dem Tempelherrenorden gemacht worden* (1782), where he argued that Freemasonry grew out of 17th-century English intellectual circles.</note> on pages 210 to 212, one misinterprets everything I said about the gradual origin of Freemasonry from Rosicrucianism. If I am to be wrong in this, one must refute the fact that the construction of Solomon’s Temple was adopted only long after the origin of Freemasonry; otherwise, I have not been refuted. I have sought it, and I believe I have found it. I will not concern myself here with the meaning that has also changed since 1682.

He says on page 38: "According to reliable historical data, at the beginning of **Rosicrucianism** <term>Rosicrucianism: An esoteric movement that emerged in early 17th-century Germany, claiming to possess secret wisdom passed down from antiquity.</term> and **Masonry**, the **Catholics** were excluded from the orders." And on page 47 he explicitly repeats: "that according to reliable historical data in the beginning of Rosicrucianism and Masonry, the Catholics were also excluded from them, and only **Protestants** were allowed to take part in them." He adds: "This trait is very striking and characteristic for the history of those institutes."

This is directed straight at me, even though Mr. B. <note>referring to Buhle</note> did not see fit to name me this time.

For it is an essential part of my opinion, founded on very serious investigations into the beginning and progress of Rosicrucianism:

That Rosicrucianism, as it initially arose among the Protestants in Germany, passed over to the Catholics shortly thereafter.

I explained myself on this above on page 39, and already in the second part of my *Essay* <note>Nicolai refers to his work "Versuch über die Beschaffenheit der ehemaligen Klosterbibliothek zu Erfurt," which discussed the origins of Masonry.</note> on page 181, I had noted: "In all editions of the **Fama** <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis*, the first Rosicrucian manifesto published in 1614, which announced the order's existence.</note>, from 1614 onwards, there is a kind of **confession of faith**, and in it: 'We also enjoy **two sacraments**.' <note>A distinctly Protestant view, as the Catholic Church recognizes seven sacraments.</note> But in the edition of 1617 (**Frankfurt am Main**, published by **Bringer**), page 30 says: 'We also enjoy **the sacraments**.' This change, which at first glance appears minor, provides an explanation for a difficulty that often misled me at first. — For either the edition was produced by a Catholic, or the change was made to please the Catholics." I add: "One finds that in the beginning, the Rosicrucians declared themselves strongly against the Catholics. A short time later, one finds,

Mr. B. <note>referring to Johann Gottlieb Buhle</note> pretends that everything true in my book confirms his own discoveries. Why, then, did he not refute my hypothesis, which directly contradicts him? And why did he not refute the quite probable proof drawn from the alterations in later editions of the **Fama** <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis*, the primary Rosicrucian manifesto</note> regarding the **two sacraments**? <note>As noted on the previous page, the original 1614 Protestant text mentions only two sacraments, but Nicolai argues later versions were modified to be more inclusive of Catholic views.</note> He, who boasts with such conceit of having refuted all my hypotheses—why does he not even mention it? Is this meant to imply that no further discussion is possible regarding an opinion simply because a man like **Court Councilor Buhle** twice assures us he has found the opposite in secure historical data?

In this, Mr. Buhle is right: "it would be very striking and characteristic" if what he so dismissively presents as the truth were actually true. For there is no rational reason why **Catholics** would have been excluded from the **Rosicrucian society**, whether one accepts Mr. B.’s opinion or my own regarding the origin of this society. If, according to my view, the **Rosicrucians** in **England** initially established a society—following the model of the **House of Solomon** <term>House of Solomon: A fictional scientific research institution described in Francis Bacon's utopian novel *New Atlantis*, which Nicolai believes influenced the formation of secret societies.</term> proposed by **Bacon** <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), an English philosopher and statesman</note>—to attempt to understand the works of God and

If, as I further believe to have discovered, after the execution of **King Charles I**, a secret committee was formed among the **Royalists** within the **Freemason society** to quietly deliberate on the restoration of the monarchy; then there was once again no reason to exclude the **Catholics**. **King Charles I** was, after all, well-disposed toward **Catholics**, had a **Catholic** queen <note>Henrietta Maria of France</note>, and one could far more readily expect the **Catholics** to cooperate in the restoration of the royal dignity of **Charles II** than the **Puritans** and **Presbyterians**, who were so vehemently republican at the time.

They were indeed Christians! **Taulerus** <note>Johannes Tauler, a 14th-century Dominican mystic</note>, **Trithemius** <note>Johannes Trithemius, a Benedictine abbot and polymath</note>, the author of the book *The Imitation of Christ* <note>original Latin: "de Imitatione Christi"; traditionally attributed to Thomas à Kempis</note>, and the author of the *German Theology* <note>original German: "deutschen Theologie"; a mystical treatise from the 14th century</note> were all **Catholics** and had walked the mystical path with great fame and success long before.

It is well known that **Fludd** <note>Robert Fludd, an English Paracelsian physician</note> argued vigorously with the Franciscan **Mersennus** <note>Marin Mersenne, a French priest and mathematician</note> regarding the **Rosicrucians**, yet he did not reproach him by suggesting that, as a **Catholic** excluded from this brotherhood, he could not judge it at all. And if **Michael Maier**, as Mr. B. says himself on page 201, wanted to found a special **Rosicrucian order**, he too could likely not have conceived of the idea to exclude **Catholics**—he, who was the personal physician to an arch-Catholic Emperor <note>Emperor Rudolf II</note>, and lived among nothing but...

It will be very easy to answer them. For since all Christians of whatever religion tend toward one and the same goal, namely Christ himself, who is the sole truth, in this unanimous agreement they all indeed meet. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of "Mr. B.," specifically addressing the claim that Catholics were excluded from the Rosicrucian Order. ...excluded from the Rosicrucian Brotherhood, and moreover: "because of the internal character of the Order." But lo and behold! From an author whom he considers the primary and almost the only classical author on Rosicrucianism <note>The author is referring to Robert Fludd (1574–1637)</note>, he provides the clearest and most certain proof that Catholics were *not* excluded from the Rosicrucian Brotherhood. For Fludd explicitly says that Christians of all religions move toward the very same goal; that disputes over the forms of morality and outward customs—for example, "whether one worships the cross, venerates images or not, the differing opinions on the **Real Presence**" (in the Lord's Supper) <note>term: "Real Presence" - the theological doctrine that the body and blood of Christ are truly present in the bread and wine of the Eucharist, a major point of contention between Catholics and various Protestant groups</note> "and such, are non-essential to true Rosicrucian wisdom." I will add further (which Mr. B. wisely omits) that Fludd explicitly adds at the end: "I conclude, therefore, that these profound men (namely, the Brothers of the Rose Cross) diligently observe both the moral and mystical laws of the Holy Bible (as I believe) within any Christian sect." <note>original: "Concludo igitur, quod iſti viri profundi (nehmlich Crucis Roseae fratres) tam morales Bibliorum sacrorum leges (ut opinor) quam mysticas, in qualibet Christiana secta obſervent diligenter."</note> Is there any other example of a writer making such a fool of his readers? That he presumes to claim something which possesses not even the slightest historical probability, yet boldly cites historical data, and then presents a passage of evidence that proves the exact opposite of what he had asserted with such arrogance? And all this merely to cast suspicion on the truthfulness of another writer who spared no effort in the most painstaking investigations to bring as much truth to light as possible.

I will cite one more striking example of how little Mr. B. often understands what I am presenting, and how severely he contradicts himself.

Herder <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803), a highly influential German philosopher, poet, and theologian who wrote on the history of secret societies.</note> in his attack on me, had quite boldly said: "the Rosicrucian Society has no connection at all with the Freemasons, and is even less one and the same with them." He said this just as entirely without basis as Mr. Buhle now conversely claims: that Freemasonry was originally one and the same with the Rosicrucians. To prove that I was right to derive Freemasonry from Rosicrucianism, I showed in the second part of my *Essay* (p. 203 ff.), that most of what appears in the first beginnings of Freemasonry—even part of the signs by which Freemasons recognize each other—can clearly be found in two Rosicrucian books printed in Germany as early as the years 1617 and 1618, although in 1617 and 1618 certainly no Rosicrucian was yet a Freemason. 

I supported this, among other things, with an engraving <term>engraving: "Kupferstich" — a print made from a carved copper plate, the standard method for detailed book illustrations in the 17th century.</term> found in the first of the aforementioned Rosicrucian books. I could hardly have given a clearer proof that my derivation of Freemasonry from the old Rosicrucianism is the true one, and that I did not pull my assertions out of thin air, but rather had read through books and conducted investigations that no one before me had read or conducted. But little attention seemed to be paid to that either. It shows very clearly that he by no means intended to found a new order of that kind which would have nothing to do with the *Fama*. The fact that *Irenäus Agnostus* also wrote epigrams against the **Rosicrucians** is truly no proof that all his writings are satires, as that "excellent" author in the *Repertorium*, and Mr. *B.* <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a philosopher and historian who wrote a skeptical history of the Rosicrucians.</note> following him, boldly assert. What! If someone were to conclude that the author of the book published in 1780, titled *Stumbling Block and Rock of Offense* <note>original: "Stein des Anstoßes und Fels der Aergerniß"; a work critical of contemporary Freemasonry.</note>, could not possibly have been a zealous Masonic **Templar**, simply because he stings so bitterly against the **Strict Observance** and the **Templars**! In such investigations, one must truly consider many factors, and not immediately pass judgment based on the first glance one takes into a few writings. A person can belong to a society and yet, when things do not go as they wish, write very mockingly about it under a disguise. Such was the case with *Irenäus Agnostus*, as can be clearly seen if one has studied the Rosicrucian writings more closely.

On page 226, Mr. *B.* falls into a ridiculous literary error, once again because he is so fond of judging writings that he has not read. *Wilhelmus ab Indagine* <note>A pseudonym for a contributor to the *Repertorium*, often identified as the scholar and diplomat Baron von Schröder.</note> in the *Württemberg Repertorium* was the first to publicly point out that in the appendix of the second edition of *Thomas Campanella’s* <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian Dominican friar and philosopher.</note> book on the *Spanish Monarchy* (from the year 1623), a notice is contained: that the *Reformation of the Whole World* <note>original: "Reformation der ganzen Welt"; a satirical text often printed with the Rosicrucian manifestos.</note> attached to the *Fama* <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis*, the first Rosicrucian manifesto (1614).</note> was taken from the first part of *Boccalini’s Reports from Parnassus* <note>original: "Boccalini Raguagli del Parnasso"; a famous work of political satire by Trajano Boccalini where Apollo and various historical figures discuss contemporary issues.</note>. Mr. *B.* correctly notes that the first *Century* of these *Reports* was printed in 1612 in Venice (which was unknown to me in 1783, though I subsequently—

A true, truth-loving man of letters must write nothing that he has not personally verified. The author of this appendix is **Christoph Besold** <note>Christoph Besold (1577–1638), a prominent German jurist and scholar.</note> (at that time still a Protestant, for it was only in the year 1635 that he became Catholic). At that time, the House of Austria famously followed Spanish counsel and sought to increase its power through measures similar to those **Campanella** advised for the Spanish Monarchy. Perhaps many readers will believe that I have explained Mr. **Buhle's** <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle (1763–1821), a philosopher and historian whom the author frequently criticizes for his work on the origins of the Rosicrucians.</note> ridiculous error—that **Campanella** <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), a famous Italian Dominican friar and philosopher whose political and eschatological works were influential in the 17th century.</note> himself wrote against the **Rosicrucians**—at too great a length. Anyone who wishes to discuss things of this kind, yet does not make the greatest precision in the relevant literary investigations their law, introduces untruths and confused views into history instead of truth.

That **Besold** <note>Christoph Besold (1577–1638), a prominent legal scholar and historian at the University of Tübingen.</note> really is the author of the appendix to Campanella is evident from his own confession, the precise reference for which I owe to one of the greatest literary scholars currently living. Namely, in the first edition of **Besold’s** *Practical Treasury, containing the explanation of terms and clauses used in the courts and tribunals of the Romano-Germanic Empire, etc.* <note>original Latin title: *Thesaurus practicus continens explicationem terminorum atque clausularum, in Aulis et Dicasteriis Romano-Germanici Imperii usitatarum etc.*</note> Tübingen 1629 in quarto, he says on page 630, under the heading **Prophecy**: “Those have always failed who wanted to calculate the Last Day or other events, as I have shown from the author of the *Bundle of Time* <note>original: *Fasciculi temporum*, a popular world chronicle from the late Middle Ages.</note>, in the appendix to *Campanella* at the end, where I treat the vanity of these predictions succinctly.” <note>original Latin and German: *ut docui ex Auctore Fasciculi temporum, in dem Anhang deß Campanellae ad finem, ubi de vanitate harum praedictionum succincte tracto.*</note> Under that same heading he adds: “We see that it has happened in almost every age, that when great changes are imminent, new prophets [arise]...” <note>original Latin: *Omnibus fere seculis accidisse videmus, ut impendentibus magnis mutationibus novi Prophetae*</note>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 99, proving that Christoph Besold was the true author of the anti-Rosicrucian appendix in the German translation of Campanella's work. It specifically addresses and refutes the errors made by the scholar Johann Gottlieb Buhle regarding the origin and naming of the Rosicrucians in Spain.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

"...pretenders come forward, see me in the appendix to *Campanella*." <note>Original Latin: "putativi prodeant, vide me in appendice Campanellae."</note> — The first edition of the ***Thesaurus*** <note>Referring to Besold's *Thesaurus Practicus*, a legal and historical reference work.</note> (66) is cited here because it was printed just a few years after the appearance of the appendix; it is therefore clear that Besold made no secret of this matter right from the start.

On page 230 in the note, Mr. **B.** <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle, the scholar being critiqued.</note> once again makes a doubly ridiculous error. He says: "In Spain as well, the rumor of the secret Rosicrucian Order had begun to circulate. There, the Rosicrucians were called the ***Alumbrados***, the Invisibles." Whether such a rumor really reached Spain remains to be seen until certain proof is provided, which is currently lacking. In any case, however, ***Alumbrado*** in Spanish does not mean an "invisible person" (which would be ***Invisible***), but rather someone who is deceived, or a visionary <term>Schwärmer: a German term for a religious enthusiast or fanatic, often used dismissively to describe people with "excessive" mystical ideas</term>.

How then does Mr. **B.** arrive at such a ridiculous error? Through negligence in transcription, as he usually draws his small amount of knowledge only from the books closest at hand—especially from Mr. **von Murr’s** book. While von Murr patches things together with little critical judgment, he has at least actually read somewhat more than Mr. **B.**

Mr. **von Murr** says on page 52: "The Rosicrucians revealed themselves in France at the same time that the ***Alumbrados***, or the Spanish **Illuminati**, were known there. They were ridiculed as 'Invisibles' through public placards." Mr. **B.**, in his hasty copying, did not look closely enough; he confused France with Spain and turned the Spanish ***Alumbrados*** into Spanish Rosicrucian "Invisibles"!

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of a contemporary writer (referred to here as Mr. V.) regarding the historical origins of Freemasonry. The discussion centers on the linguistic transition from operative masonry (the trade) to speculative masonry (the philosophical society).</meta>

...gathered in the Masons' Guild in London. It entered the Guild of Masons, which is already evident from the fact that even now the Society of Freemasons in England uses the very same coat of arms that belongs to the Masons' Guild; and it then adopted the name: **Free and accepted Masons**.

It would be incomprehensible how Mr. V. could write something so completely false without concern, were it not for the fact that so many examples in his book show that he boldly asserts whatever seems good to him. It is, after all, well-known and obvious: that the Freemasons in England call themselves **Free and accepted Masons** in all their writings; that this latter epithet is found only among them; and that it is customary neither for the craft of masonry nor for any other craft.

I have consulted native-born Englishmen—both Freemasons and non-Freemasons—and especially those who have studied their own language. All stated unanimously that the word **accepted** is never used for admission into a guild, but rather the word **admitted**. Mr. B. <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a scholar whose work on the history of secret societies is being scrutinized here.</note> adds on page 261: “It is probable that around the same time, similar mason’s lodges were formed in England and Scotland, which maintained the same guild-like constitution and were favored by kings due to their importance and necessity for major construction projects.” What leaps of logic! Where is the probability that such lodges—and lodges that were supposedly *Masonic* lodges, no less—must have existed in England just as they did in Germany? And what a perverse addition to suggest that the constitution of these mason’s lodges must have been favored by kings in England! Was this even the case in Germany?

Neither Mr. von Murr <note>Christoph Gottlieb von Murr (1733–1811), a prominent German scholar and historian.</note> nor his copyist Mr. Buhle provides a reason why Grandidier’s <note>Philippe-André Grandidier (1752–1787), an Alsatian historian who popularized the theory that modern Freemasonry began with the Strasbourg cathedral builders.</note> derivation is inadmissible. I intend to prove here that it is indeed inadmissible, as this will simultaneously serve to refute the many similar, silly fabrications regarding the origin of Freemasonry.

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument against the historical claims of Philippe-André Grandidier. The previous page discussed an imperial decree from 1707 that stripped the Strasbourg stonemasons of their authority over German craft guilds because Strasbourg was then under French control. The author is now explaining the technical terminology of medieval guilds to show how easily these terms have been misinterpreted as modern Masonic concepts.</meta>

As is well known, craft guilds in several cities have always corresponded with one another, particularly regarding disputed cases. It is equally well known that every trade has a <term>chest<note>German: *Lade*. In guild tradition, this was a physical wooden chest containing the guild’s charter, seal, and treasury. It became a metonym for the guild administration itself.</note></term> in which the papers and funds belonging to the guild are kept. The meeting of a guild always takes place in the location where the chest is present; therefore, when something is undertaken or decided during a meeting of the trade, it is said to have occurred "before an open chest." Those craftsmen who work in <term>huts<note>German: *Hütte*. Originally referring to the temporary wooden workshops built against the side of a cathedral under construction; this is the etymological root of the Masonic "Lodge."</note></term> and keep their tools there use the name "Hut" [Hütte] instead of the word "Chest" [Lade] during their meetings (71). Therefore, both words—Chest or Hut—are often used to refer to the trade organization itself (72).

Now, several abuses had arisen within the trades. Among these was the fact that the "Chests" in certain prominent cities claimed greater privileges for themselves, insisting on being called "Main Chests" [Hauptladen] and labeling the chests of other cities as merely "Secondary Chests." These and further abuses were completely abolished by the <term>Imperial Constitutional Patent<note>The *Reichskonstitutions-Patent* of 1731 was a landmark piece of legislation by the Holy Roman Empire intended to break the political and judicial power of the guilds and bring them under state control.</note></term> of August 16, 1731, which was adopted throughout Germany. There, Section 6 states (73):

> "All and every such Main Chests or Main
> Huts are hereby completely annihilated, abolished,
> and done away with; also, all <note>original: *Provocationes*</note> appeals to trade-jurisdiction
> that have been abusively raised here and there
> are forbidden,"

etc. Is it not, therefore, completely absurd to take the stonemason trades of Vienna, Strasbourg, and so on—which abusively set themselves up as "Main Huts"—and try to turn them into Masonic "Grand State Lodges," foisting this deception upon the brothers in a Masonic speech?

<summary>The author explains how the physical "chests" and "huts" of medieval craft guilds were administrative tools, not mystical lodges. He cites a 1731 Imperial decree to prove these "Main Huts" were merely illegal administrative monopolies, further debunking the link between operative masonry and modern Freemasonry.</summary>

<keywords>craft guilds, Lade, Hütte, stonemasonry, Imperial Constitutional Patent of 1731, Grand Lodges, Strasbourg, Vienna, guild history</keywords>

demonstrated in the various explanations of the number Three; moreover, the symbol of the number Three discovered there clearly passed from **Rosicrucianism** into **Freemasonry**. Therefore, Mr. Buhle's premise is "false," and the conceit of the Mr. <term>Hofrath</term> <note>original: "Hofrath"; a title for a high-ranking court official or "Aulic Councilor," which Buhle held.</note>, with which he so boldly presumes to declare all other explanations false, is certainly "ridiculous."

<meta>This page continues the author’s sharp critique of Johann Gottfried Buhle’s scholarship, specifically regarding the origins of Masonic numerology. On the previous page, the author began questioning Buhle's claim that Masonic numbers like "Three times Three" derive from Paracelsian philosophy.</meta>

I have been told that although the **Freemasons** speak of "Three times Three" and "Four times Four," they never actually speak of "Nine" or "Sixteen." This is said to be because the units within Masonic numbers have a unique property: some of them are larger than others. Therefore, anyone who wishes to derive the origin of these so-called **sacred numbers** and explain their meaning would, above all else, have to demonstrate why some of their units are larger and some smaller; for this is likely the reason why they are not ordinary numbers, but are peculiar to Freemasonry. In this regard, **Paracelsus’s** triangle certainly would not fit the bill, for it is, after all, equilateral <note>A triangle where all three sides are of equal length; the author argues this contradicts the idea of "unequal" units.</note>. And regarding the four elements, surely a few of them are not merely half as large as the others. If the four elements were to be the model for a Masonic "Four," then the square would either be very unequal, or rather, a parallelogram would have to result! Thus, Mr. **Buhle’s** explanation derived from Paracelsus is clearly not fitting at all; it is therefore false, and one will likely have to search for another. Oh, if only Mr. B. truly understood what is required for such a matter, and would first properly consider everything he writes before he presumes to judge so boldly and *decisively*!

Michael Maier’s work is so extremely rare that it is not to be found in the largest libraries; perhaps not even ten copies of it still exist in all of Germany. <meta>Continuing from page 125, Nicolai is defending his linguistic preference for the term "Engländisch" (pertaining to the country of England) over "Englisch" (which can refer to the language or even the "angelic"). He is responding to mockery from his critic, Johann Gottfried Buhle.</meta>

...even if one does not think of indicating the difference in meaning between *English* <note>original: "anglicus"</note> and *angelic* <note>original: "angelicus"</note>. All those adjectives by which we designate the language, the character, the constitution, and so on of various countries are, in German, generally formed not from the name of the country, but from the name of its inhabitants. We say:

->Denmark, the Dane, Danish.<-
->Germany, the German, German.<-
->France, the Frenchman, French.<-
->Portugal, the Portuguese, Portuguese.<-
->Russia, the Russian, Russian.<-
->Scotland, the Scot, Scottish.<-
->Italy <note>original: "Wälschland," an older German term for Italy or Romance-speaking lands</note>, the Italian <note>original: "der Wälsche"</note>, Italian <note>original: "wälsch"</note>.<-

On the other hand:

->Holland, the Hollander, Hollandish.<-
->Livonia, the Livonian, Livonian.<-
->Southland, the Southerner, Southern.<-
->England, the Englishman, English <note>original: "engländisch"</note>.<-

The best German dictionaries—**Adelung**, **Frisch**, **Henisch**, and **Spate** <note>These were the standard authorities of the time: Johann Christoph Adelung, Johann Leonhard Frisch, Georg Henisch, and Caspar von Stieler (known as "Der Spate").</note>—include the word **English** <note>original: "Engländisch"</note>, with only the remark that it is usually contracted into **English** <note>original: "Englisch"</note>. Even **Gottsched** <note>Johann Christoph Gottsched (1700–1766), an influential literary reformer who sought to standardize the German language.</note> constantly used the word **Engländisch**. Several other good writers did the same. For example, **Büsching** <note>Anton Friedrich Büsching (1724–1793), a renowned geographer.</note> writes: "the English language, the English Church, the English legal scholars." Anyone who intends to judge whether one should use only the adjective **English** <note>original: "Englisch"</note>, or rather **English** <note>original: "Engländisch"</note>, ought in fairness to know all of the above. <meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on linguistic precision, Nicolai shifts from defending his use of the word "Engländisch" to a direct attack on his critic's (Mr. V.) knowledge of the English language and its historical guild structures.</meta>

On page 312, Mr. V. asserts with the greatest audacity a gross **untruth** regarding the English word *Mystery*, merely because he wants to contradict me and disparage me; for on this occasion he accuses me: "I supposedly wanted to present Freemasonry with a mystery of my own invention <note>literally "from my factory," an 18th-century idiom meaning something fabricated or made up</note>." All of this is merely invented by Mr. V. *Johnson’s* <note>referring to Samuel Johnson’s famous "A Dictionary of the English Language," first published in 1755</note> and all other dictionaries say that *Mystery*, used by guilds, means *trade*, *calling*, thus in every way, the craft, the occupation, the guild itself, but never the science or the masterpiece. The word *Master* is not written *Mister* or *Mystery*; although when it means the polite address *Sir* <note>original: "Herr"</note>, it is pronounced *Mister*; but as soon as it signifies a master or a lord, it sounds as *Master*. Everything Mr. V. puts forward here is entirely wrong, and at the same time shows a total lack of knowledge regarding the constitution of the guilds and crafts in England. It is ridiculous to say that a masterpiece is the secret of a craft. A masterpiece, insofar as it is a masterfully successful...

<summary>Nicolai refutes a critic's claim that the English word "Mystery" in the context of guilds refers to a "masterpiece," using Samuel Johnson's dictionary to prove it actually refers to the trade or guild itself. This linguistic debate is part of a larger argument over the historical origins and terminology of Freemasonry.</summary>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicolai’s aggressive rebuttal regarding the terminology of English guilds and Freemasonry. He is specifically refuting the claim that the word "Mystery" implies a "masterpiece" requirement for guild entry in England, as it did in Continental Europe.</meta>

work in any art, it is called a Master-piece <note>Nicolai is distinguishing between the general meaning of a "masterpiece" and the formal legal requirement in Continental guilds</note>. But from the craftsmen in England, that which we call a *Meisterstück* in Germany is never demanded or made; that is, a work of their craft, "through which they qualify to obtain the master’s right in their guild." Various Englishmen whom I have asked about this knew nothing of it, and Chambers <note>Ephraim Chambers (c. 1680–1740), whose *Cyclopaedia* was a precursor to the French *Encyclopédie*</note> in his Encyclopedia says explicitly: "Master-piece or *Chef-d’oeuvre* is used by the French in the specific meaning of a work which those who wish to become masters in an art or craft must produce as a sign of their ability for it."

The master's right which someone gains in a craft or in any guild, the guild-right which he attains as a member of a <term>Mystery</term> <note>original: "Mystery"; used here in the sense of a trade or occupation, derived from the Latin *misterium*</note>, is called in both cases in English *Freedom* <note>original: "Freedom"; referring to the "Freedom of the City," the status of being a "freeman" of a guild or company</note>, because everyone who receives the right to a society or guild is called *Free* in respect of this right, as I have already indicated in the 1st part of my Essay, p. 195. I wish to confirm it with a new example, however distasteful it is to have to provide proof for such a well-known matter, because such an ignorant corrupter of historical truth as Mr. B. is in his book on <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> does not know the most common things and boldly asserts untruths. In the life of Richardson, famous for his novels, who was a printer, his biographer Mrs. Barbauld says: "After the expiration of his apprenticeship, our author continued five or six years working as a compositor and corrector of the press to a printing-office, and part of the time as an overseer" <note>This quote from Anna Laetitia Barbauld’s 1804 biography of Samuel Richardson was originally in English in Nicolai's text</note> (the German printers

<meta>catchword: call</meta>

...call this a factor); "and, at length thus working his way upwards into daylight, he took up his freedom, and set up for himself." (79) Can anything be clearer! When the apprentice is enrolled into the trade or the art, one says, *he is bound apprentice*; as long as he works as a journeyman <note>original: "Geselle"</note>, he is still bound to some master for whom he works, but as soon as he gains the master’s right <note>original: "Meisterrecht"</note>, he is now *free*. *the mystery of the grocers, the mystery of the fishmongers, the mystery of the ironmongers, the mystery of the barbers, the mystery of the cooks, the mystery of the tallow-chandlers.*

How, then, could the grocers, the fishmongers, the ironmongers, the tallow-chandlers, the barbers, or the cooks produce a "masterpiece" <note>original: "Meisterstück"</note> to qualify themselves to enter their guild, even if a masterpiece were otherwise required for crafts in England? To be sure, the guild constitution <note>original: "Zunftverfassung"</note> originally arose from the trades; but there are many guilds in London which do not...

[These] consist of trades, and the word *Mystery* is used for these as well, although there are no actual <term>Masters</term> <note>original: "Meister"</note> in them, but only such persons who have received the guild-right, i.e., are *free* in this *Mystery*. Therefore, if Mr. B. <note>Possibly a reference to another critic or writer in the same debate</note> were to hide behind a word again and pretend that by the word <term>Mastership</term> <note>original: "Meisterschafft"</note> he meant the entire assembly of <term>Masters</term> <note>original: "Meister"</note>, this excuse likewise cannot hold.

Was I then not correct to point out in the 1st part of my essay that when one finds the word *Mystery*, especially in writings intentionally composed to conceal this or that, one must be on one's guard as to whether a "secret" <note>original: "Geheimniße"</note> is actually being discussed, so as not to have a secret foisted upon oneself where none exists? ...held behind closed doors. However, a **masons' inn** <note>original: "Maurerherberge." <meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s sharp rebuttal of Johann Gottlieb Buhle (referred to as "Mr. B."). This page focuses on Andrew Michael Ramsay and Buhle’s claim that Freemasonry is essentially a vehicle for "magic" and "Kabbalistic Christianity," which Nicolai views as a dangerous corruption of religion.</meta>

It [Freemasonry] becomes distorted and crippled when one attempts to make it the object of a secret society—that is, when one wishes to present it only as the property of a few. Especially when one mixes magic (no matter how artificially one seeks to explain it) with the true Christian Church, what superstition and nonsense will arise from it? And would such shameful nonsense be the "core and essence" which modern Freemasonry has supposedly lost, leaving behind only the shell and the form? If any reasonable person could still doubt that the most abominable corruption of religion must result when religion is made esoteric <note>In this context, "esoteric" refers to knowledge intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge; here it implies turning public religion into a secret cult.</note> and shrouded in mysteries, let him read that excellent short work: *Wegscheider's "On why the mysteries of the Greeks should not be forced upon religion" (Göttingen 1805. 8vo).* <note>original: "Wegscheider de Graecorum mysteriis religioni non obtrudendis." Julius Wegscheider (1771–1849) was a prominent rationalist theologian; Nicolai cites him to argue that mixing secret "mysteries" with religion leads to irrationality.</note>

Now, however, according to Mr. B.’s reasoning, anyone who in the year 1730 knew nothing of this brand-new, just-invented hypothesis—that Masonry originated solely from magic and the true Christian Church—and who therefore did not already practice Masonry according to the spirit of this hypothesis at that time;

such a Freemason is supposed to know nothing of the older state of Masonry for that reason alone!

He is to be dismissed entirely, his testimony considered invalid!

After all, Ramsay <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), or Chevalier Ramsay, was a Scottish expatriate in France whose 1737 speech famously claimed Freemasonry descended from the Crusader knights rather than medieval stonemasons.</note> could practice Masonry in a different manner and yet still know very well what its older state had been. Furthermore, he could have historical information from some other source that the restoration of King Charles II <note>Charles II (1630–1685) was restored to the English throne in 1660. Some Masonic legends claim he was initiated into a lodge during his exile.</note>, around the year 1649, was arranged in a Freemason lodge. ...of the Pretender <note>James Francis Edward Stuart (1688–1766), known as the "Old Pretender," who claimed the British throne for the House of Stuart.</note>, who entrusted Ramsay <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), a Scottish-born writer and influential Freemason who lived in France and served as tutor to the Pretender's sons.</note> with the education of his sons, possessed very reliable information regarding how the first purely secret measures had been taken by the supporters of the monarchy in England for the restoration of his family. Even in England, where the freedom of the press is so great, much remains hidden from the public that several people know very well. This might perhaps apply particularly to the period following the Treaty of Utrecht <note>signed in 1713; it ended the War of the Spanish Succession and had significant implications for the Stuart claims to the throne.</note>.

It is quite clear from the circumstances that such a suspicion (or even an accusation) must have existed; otherwise, they would not have gone to such lengths to avert it. It says in this essay: "There are indeed men of all parties in the lodges—Whigs, Tories, Episcopalians, Presbyterians, and even kings and princes." ...of the Pretender <note>James Francis Edward Stuart (1688–1766), the "Old Pretender," who claimed the British throne.</note>, who entrusted Ramsay with the education of his sons, possessed very reliable information regarding the manner in which the first merely secret measures for the restoration of his family were taken by the supporters of the monarchy in England. Even in England, where the freedom of the press is so great, much remains hidden from the public that several people know very well. This might apply especially to the period following the Treaty of Utrecht <note>Signed in 1713, this treaty ended the War of the Spanish Succession and had significant implications for the Jacobite cause.</note>.

Furthermore: Ramsay did indeed give this report of the arrangement in a Masonic lodge, not in <term>Cant</term> <note>original: "Cant"; refers to a secret jargon or coded language used by specific groups.</note> (an expression not very fitting on this occasion), but in clear words. Moreover, if such a secret connection initially took place, it is also possible that it existed and yet may have had nothing to do with what is actually called Freemasonry.

I here ask permission to further note the following. There are certain handwritten essays concerning the nature of English Freemasonry which are in only a few hands and have not been printed, at least not <term>in full</term> <note>original: "in extenso"</note>. In these it is

It appears quite clearly from the circumstances that such a suspicion (or even an accusation) must have existed; otherwise, one would not have gone to such lengths to avert it. The essay states: "Indeed, in the lodges there are men of all parties: Whigs, Tories <note>The two primary, often opposing, political factions in England starting in the late 17th century.</note>, Episcopalians, Presbyterians, and even kings and princes." It is also specifically assured therein that the Freemasons never took a side during the political upheavals in England.

I gladly allow this defense its value, insofar as it is intended to prove that the lodge institutions do not have such interference in political affairs as their purpose. But the question here is only: whether around the year 1649 <note>The year of the execution of King Charles I during the English Civil War.</note>, on the occasion and under the pretext of the Masonic society, the Royalists <note>Supporters of the monarchy against the Parliamentarians.</note> formed a special committee which met separately, without the knowledge of the others whom they did not wish to involve? <meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s linguistic critique of Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle’s theories regarding the origins of Freemasonry. “is named.” Thus it seemed to me, “the word *Massonya*, which also has both meanings, would likely indicate a closed society.” — Against this, Mr. *Buhle* now says: “How should one have connected the concept of a **club** <note>original: *Keule*</note>, a **bolt**, or a **key** with the concept of a **closed society**?” But there is no need here for hair-splitting over the “how”; for it is a fact that *Massonya* means a club, and that a closed society is also called a **club** (*Club*) by the English. In *Scherz’s German Glossary of the Middle Ages* <note>original: *Scherzii Glossarium germanicum medii aevi*, a scholarly dictionary of Old and Middle High German</note>, it can be found that in the 14th century, the word **Kolben** <term>Kolben: literally a mace or a heavy club, often used as a symbol for social guilds or drinking societies</term> (known to be synonymous with a club) meant not only a **banquet** (81), but also a unifying—and thus closed—society. Should one also ask here, as Mr. *B.* does: “How could one have possibly connected the concept of a mace with that of a banquet or a society?” Suffice it to say, it rests upon irreproachable testimonies that these concepts were indeed connected. In Strasbourg in the 16th century, such a unifying society was even called an **established kingdom** <note>original: *angefengt Ruinigreich*</note> (82). That seems even more peculiar, and yet, against the clear testimony of history, no one can doubt that the concept of such a society was indeed linked with the concept of a **kingdom**, just as it was with the concept of a **mace**. *Frisch* <note>Johann Leonhard Frisch (1666–1743), a German linguist and lexicographer</note>, in his dictionary, also cites a “Society of the Mace” <note>original: *Gesellschaft zu dem Kolben*</note> (83) from the 14th century under the entry for *Kolben*.

All these words, therefore, always signify some kind of assembly or closely linked society. This is the general concept. In no way do they signify a *place of residence*, much less a *building for several persons*. Nevertheless, Mr. B. is not ashamed to assert this quite boldly, and relies on the fact that no one will look further to see if he speaks the truth. ## 1. The Great Discovery!
### which is intended to strike down everything that has ever been written about the origin of Freemasonry!

This discovery is shared with us by *Mr. B.* <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle (1763–1821), whom Nicolai is mocking here for his supposedly "groundbreaking" theories.</note> in the following words (p. 369 ff.): “The oldest Freemasons were a society which formed during the years 1630 to 1640, and had the purpose of seeking—partly in common and partly, insofar as they believed themselves already in possession of it, communicating to one another—the true **magic** in a **kabbalistic** <term>Kabbalah: A school of Jewish mysticism that Christian scholars of this era adapted to find hidden meanings in the Bible.</term> and **alchemical** sense, or the secret wisdom revealed figuratively and parabolically in the Bible, which encompasses the true philosophy of nature, and with it all the secrets of nature, true theology, and religion.” — “The purpose of Freemasonry was represented under the symbol of **Solomon’s Temple**, which was to be built. This symbol was chosen to allegorically designate the true Christian Church, whose foundation and cornerstone is Christ, and to which all people are to approach as living stones. The way and the means, however, to build Solomon’s Temple, are the study and the practice of true magic in the sense stated above.” The entire proof of this excellent discovery consists of a few passages from the book *The Highest Good* <note>original: "Summum Bonum"</note>, published in 1629, which is attributed on the title page to a *Joachim Friz*, but which most people, and among them also *Mr. B.*, attribute to *Robert Fludd* <note>Robert Fludd (1574–1637) was a prominent English physician and mystical philosopher; Nicolai is pointing out that Buhle's "discovery" relies on a text written before his own estimated date for the society's founding.</note>.

This new discovery is thus a brand-new secret with which *Mr. B.* presents Freemasonry; only

this new mystery does not come from Mr. Buhle's factory. The essentials of it could already be read in printed books. That "magic in a kabbalistic and alchemical sense is revealed figuratively and parabolically in the Bible" has already been said by many Jewish and Christian fanatics <term>fanatics: The original German "Schwärmer" refers to religious enthusiasts or visionaries who claim direct divine inspiration, often viewed with suspicion by Enlightenment thinkers like Nicolai.</term>, whom one must consider either creeping frauds or mindless simpletons. To be sure, that a professor of philosophy writes down such nonsense without blushing, and even calls this philosophical Freemasonry (p. 256 and elsewhere), is indeed something quite new!

Mr. von Murr <note>Christoph Gottlieb von Murr (1733–1811), a historian whom Nicolai accuses of being the source for many of Buhle's errors.</note>, whom Mr. B. copies so extensively, says on page 69 of his book: "The seed of the origin of Freemasonry is to be placed in the year 1633." He adds no other proof than a passage in the *Key to Fludd’s Philosophy* <note>original: *Clavis philosophiae Fluddanae* (Frankfurt, 1633, folio)</note>, where it says on page 50: "The Brothers of the Rosy Cross, formerly so called, whom we today call Wise Men, having set aside that name as one held in hatred by wretched mortals who are veiled in ignorance, and [the name] being now almost buried in human oblivion." <note>original Latin: "Fratres R. C., olim sic dicti, quos nos hodie Sapientes vocamus, omisso illo nomine tamquam odioso, miseris mortalibus velo ignorantiae obductis, et in oblivione hominum jam fere sepulto."</note> Semler <note>Johann Salomo Semler (1725–1791), a theologian known for his historical-critical method.</note> was the first to cite this passage in his additions to Fludd’s defense (p. 160), which Mr. von Murr, who unfailingly copied it from there, does not indicate. From the entire context of the passage—which Mr. von Murr certainly did not read for himself in Fludd’s own work—it appears that likely only Fludd, in his fierce zeal to defend the Rosicrucian Brotherhood against **Gassendi** and **Mersenne** <note>Pierre Gassendi and Marin Mersenne were prominent French philosophers and scientists who were contemporary critics of Fludd’s mystical theories.</note>, found it good to no longer call them Rosicrucians, but rather Wise Men. The phrase "we call" <note>original: *nos vocamus*</note> indicates, as is very common in Latin, a single man <note>Nicolai is arguing that Fludd was using the "authorial we" to refer to himself, not describing a formal change in a society's name.</note>. It is also not even true that the name of the Rosicrucians around the

<summary>Nicolai argues that Buhle's theories are recycled from earlier authors like von Murr and misinterpret a Latin passage by Robert Fludd. <meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critique of Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle’s theories on the origins of Freemasonry. ...that [the name] had become hated or forgotten in the year 1633. As late as the year 1657, Johann Heydon published someone else's book under the title he himself created, **Rosi-crucian Physick**, and intended to recommend it by using this title (see my *Attempt*, Vol. I, p. 212). But even assuming that the Rosicrucians had abandoned their old name at that time and called themselves *Sapientes* <note>Latin: "Wise Men"</note>, and assuming they were also called so by all other people—although not the slightest trace of either can be found—how does it follow from this that the beginning of Freemasonry should be set at this time? This is merely empty daydreaming, which Mr. B. <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle</note> thoughtlessly parrots after Mr. von Murr. Even if the **Rosicrucians** had been called **Wise Men**, they were never called **Masons** anywhere.

Mr. von M. <note>Christoph Gottlieb von Murr</note> likewise stated on page 65 of his book, during the defense of the Rosicrucians in the *Highest Good* <note>original Latin title: *Summum Bonum*, a 1629 work by Robert Fludd</note>: "Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath who attacked Rosicrucianism as heretical.</note> had asked: Where then does this society of Rosicrucians reside? Fludd answers: In the houses of God, where *Christ* is the cornerstone." Here Mr. Buhle—who so enjoys drawing from the nearest brook, and so very rarely, or almost never, goes back to the more distant primary sources—was even given the idea by Mr. von Murr that *Christ* is intended to be the cornerstone of the house; for we shall see below that according to Mr. Buhle, ***Christ*** and ***Hiram*** <note>Hiram Abiff, a central figure in Masonic allegory, said to be the master builder of Solomon's Temple.</note> are one and the same. Thus Hiram—who in true history is by no means the master builder of Solomon’s Temple, but who, according to Masonic legend, is supposed to be this builder—would, according to Mr. B.’s learned derivation, actually be the cornerstone of an allegorical house, of which he would simultaneously be the master builder; a house which points to no allegory of a temple at all, least of all the Temple of Solomon!

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critique of Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle’s theories on the origins of Freemasonry. Nicolai argues that Buhle has misunderstood and over-extended Nicolai’s own scholarly research to create a false historical link between the Rosicrucians and the Freemasons.</meta>

Finally, I, in my humble self, had already provided Mr. B. <note>Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle (1763–1821), a German philosopher and historian</note> with the information in the second part of my *Attempt* <note>Referring to Nicolai’s book *Versuch über die Beschaffenheit und Geschichte der Freymaurer-Gesellschaft* (Attempt at the Nature and History of the Masonic Society)</note> on page 209, that in the *Highest Good* <note>original: *Summum Bonum*, a 1629 work by Robert Fludd</note> on page 49, it is stated: 

"Finally, the brother works under the figure of an **Architect** toward the perfection of this work. Wherefore the Apostle says: *According to the grace of God which is given unto me, as a wise masterbuilder, I have laid the foundation etc.* (1 Cor. 3:10-11). — And under the type of such an **Architect**, the Prophet warns us that we should ascend the **Rational Mountain**, so that we may build the **house of wisdom**." <note>original Latin: "Denique sub Architecti figura operatur frater ad hujus operis perfectionem... Atque sub istiusmodi Architecti typo nos monet Propheta, ut ascendamus Montem rationabilem, ut aedificemus domum sapientiae."</note> 

I cited this there, of course, only as an example of how the **Rosicrucians** often speak of their building (house, college), and thus I do not infer more from it than what is actually contained within it. Mr. B., however, is a "genius" whose imagination leaps wherever it wishes, who does not bind himself to tedious, cold investigation, but rather grasps without much effort whatever suits him and adds whatever first comes to mind. 

He could have learned from my book—and certainly did learn—that **Freemasonry** originated from **Rosicrucianism**. Only, he certainly did not know how this actually occurred, because I did not clearly indicate it for the reasons given above on page 23, and because he had almost never read or examined the Rosicrucian writings himself (of which I have cited so many examples). Furthermore, he actually knows very little about the nature and history of Freemasonry, as anyone who knows slightly more than he does will find. 

Since he was intent on deriving **Freemasonry** from **Rosicrucianism** according to the direction I provided, he eagerly seized upon the evidence found in my work—that the Rosicrucians had already adopted the image of a <term>Baumeister: A master builder or architect; a term used both literally in construction and metaphorically in spiritual or philosophical "building"</term>—and immediately transformed them from **master builders** into **Freemasons**.

<summary>Nicolai accuses Buhle of being a lazy scholar who misinterpreted a metaphorical reference to "architects" in Rosicrucian texts as literal proof that they were the founders of Freemasonry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s sharp critique of Johann Gottlieb Gerhard Buhle. It might seem as if I were attributing too much influence to the passage I extracted from the *Highest Good* <note>original: *Summum Bonum*, a 1629 work by Robert Fludd</note> so long ago in my book regarding **Mr. Buhle’s** hypothesis. But if he had not merely followed my lead, it would be incomprehensible why he stops only at the *Highest Good* that I pointed out. For, if a writer truly well-versed in **Rosicrucian writings** allowed himself to apply everything that appears there concerning a "building" without further ado to the **Temple of Solomon** and to the construction of the **Freemasons**, quite a different "castle in the air" <note>original: *Luftgebäude*; Nicolai is mocking the fragility of Buhle's theoretical "architecture"</note> could be erected!

One must give **Mr. B.** the credit of admitting that he not only used very little material for the construction of his hypothesis, but also sought, with great caution, to make several preparations to support it, or at least to prevent its weakness from being discovered. It is true that he behaved quite clumsily in this regard, so that the structural decay was, upon closer inspection, impossible to hide, and the building had to collapse in on itself.

1) First, in his preface (*page VIII*), he allowed it to be subtly suggested that he had accidentally not read my book until “after the correct historical trace, which led more surely to the goal, had already been found by him.” By this, he wanted to conceal that he owes the only trace upon which he builds everything in his supposed discovery—the *Highest Good*—truly his own “highest good”!—to my book, which he certainly read early enough. I spoke of this above on *page 56 and following*.

Not the slightest proof can be given that there ever were **Fluddists** who assembled in such a manner, or possessed such a secret, as the **Freemasons**. **Fludd** stood almost entirely alone and was not respected at all in his own country (84). If there were to have been **Fluddists** in such numbers that they could have established meetings according to a specific and permanent plan, they would much sooner have been found in **Germany**, where all of **Fludd’s** writings were printed and were much better known than in **England**. But in **Germany**, there were the fewest **Fluddists** of all. So little does Mr. B. think to make his arbitrary claims even slightly consistent and not patently contradictory!

...argued, he could not possibly have claimed that the *house of God* <note>original: "domus Dei"</note> in which the Rosicrucians (and according to Mr. *Buhle’s* dream, the Freemasons as well) lived, or upon which they were building, was the Temple of Solomon; for *Father Mersenne* <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French priest and mathematician who was a fierce critic of magic and alchemy</note> would have immediately shouted back with the greatest zeal: that it was not true, and not possible, precisely because the Rosicrucians were damned heretics. In *The Highest Good* <note>original: "Summum Bonum," a 1629 work attributed to Fludd</note>, it is actually—as is evident and as the title of the book itself states—a matter of true *magic*, *Kabbalah*, and *alchemy*; that is, it deals with the knowledge of nature as it was understood at that time. Physical knowledge was mixed with baseless ideas and superstitions, as I have already indicated above on page 61. Even the highly celebrated *Kepler* <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the astronomer who discovered the laws of planetary motion, but who also practiced astrology and sought a mathematical harmony in the universe</note> was not entirely free from this. ...was obtained, was at that time a widespread prejudice, which is particularly found in most Rosicrucian writings. When Fludd says on page 40:

> “*Let us ascend to the mountain of reason, and let us build the House of Wisdom*,” <note>original: “Ascendamus ad montem rationabilem, et aedificemus Domum Sapientiae”</note>

it was then clearly evident that this House of Wisdom was to be founded on reason, and thus was intended to be directed toward researching the causes of things, just like Bacon’s House of Solomon <note>A fictional research institution described in Francis Bacon’s *New Atlantis* (1627), dedicated to the study of natural philosophy and the "enlarging of the bounds of Human Empire."</note>. (See the 1st Part of my *Essay* <note>Friedrich Nicolai, *Versuch über die Beschuldigungen welche dem Tempelherrenorden gemacht worden* (1782)</note>, pp. 133, 194, and also above, pp. 61, 68.) The fact that he also called this house the *House of God* <note>original: "Domus Dei"</note> on page 41 arose from the belief that all wisdom, and thus even reason itself, was derived directly from God and from union with him; and so it was once again consistent that Christ should be the cornerstone of this house.

But that Fludd truly intended this wisdom and this House of God to be focused on the knowledge of nature is clear from the fact that he explicitly states on page 41 that it is so named after the stone upon which the patriarch Jacob had his well-known prophetic dream, and therefore called the stone *Beth-El* or "House of God" (Genesis 28:17). Now, in the 45th note, I have cited proof from this same author's *Mosaic Philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophia Moysaica," published posthumously in 1638, where Fludd attempts to explain physics and natural philosophy through the lens of the Book of Genesis</note> that in the introduction to this book, he "divides everything existing in nature into three worlds," and says:

> “the *mental intuition* <note>original: "Intuitus mentalis"</note> by which one penetrates into the eternal light that animates all creatures, is the triple measure of Jacob’s Ladder, which he saw in a dream while sleeping on a stone, in whose length, breadth, and depth the characters of the three imagined worlds were engraved, for which reason this stone is called the house or *tabernacle* <note>original: "tabernaculum" (hut, dwelling)</note> of God.”

<sig>L 2</sig>

<summary>Nicolai argues that Robert Fludd's "House of Wisdom" is a rational, scientific concept modeled after Bacon's "House of Solomon" rather than a religious temple. He uses Fludd’s interpretations of Jacob’s Ladder and the stone of Beth-El to show that these symbols represented a mystical but nature-focused "mental intuition."</summary>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s argument that Robert Fludd’s “House of Wisdom” or “House of God” was not synonymous with the Temple of Solomon. Nicolai is refuting the historian Johann Gottlieb Buhle’s theory that the Rosicrucians (and by extension, the Freemasons) were building a literal or symbolic Temple of Solomon, arguing instead that their imagery was rooted in a mystical approach to natural science.</meta>

...disputed, he could not possibly have claimed that the *House of God* <note>original: "domus Dei"</note>, in which the Rosicrucians (and, according to Mr. Buhle’s dream, also the Freemasons) lived, or which they were building, was the Temple of Solomon; for Father Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French polymath and Catholic priest who famously engaged in a bitter intellectual dispute with Robert Fludd, accusing him of magic and heresy.</note> would have immediately shouted at him with the greatest fervor: that this was not true, and not possible, precisely because the Rosicrucians were damned heretics. To older Catholic theologians in particular, the Temple of Solomon is the symbol of the one true Catholic Church. I will provide proof of this in a note (85) taken from just two books by Catholic theologians printed in 1613 and 1623. Fludd undoubtedly knew the Catholic meaning of this symbol, as he was by no means lacking in learning and erudition. He could therefore hardly have attributed the building of Solomon’s Temple to the Rosicrucians in this polemic, especially since the Rosicrucians generally never made use of this symbol anyway.

In the *Highest Good* <note>original: "Summum Bonum," a 1629 work published under the name Joachim Frizius, but widely attributed to Robert Fludd. It defended Fludd's mystical philosophy against Mersenne's attacks.</note>, as is visually evident and even stated in the book's title, the subject is actually true *Magic*, *Kabbalah*, and *Alchemy*—that is, the knowledge of nature, as it was then understood. Physical knowledge was mixed with groundless ideas and superstitions, as I have already indicated above on page 61. Even the highly praiseworthy *Kepler* <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the famed astronomer, who despite his scientific breakthroughs, also sought a harmonic, mathematical "music of the spheres" in the cosmos.</note> was not entirely free of this. Is it any wonder then that such a high-flown hothead as *Fludd*, who indulged so much in obscure ideas, mixed his knowledge of nature with daydreams even more than anyone else? That the more precise insight into physics through the help of union with God

<meta>This page continues Nicolai's argument that Robert Fludd’s mystical "House of Wisdom" was a metaphor for natural philosophy and reason, rather than a literal religious structure or the Temple of Solomon as others claimed. ...attained, was at that time a widespread prejudice, which is found particularly in most Rosicrucian writings. When Fludd says on page 40: "Let us ascend the rational mountain, and let us build the House of Wisdom," <note>original: "Ascendamus ad montem rationabilem, et aedificemus Domum Sapientiae"</note> it was nonetheless evident that this House of Wisdom was to be founded on reason, and was thus intended to research the causes of things, just like Bacon’s Solomon’s House. <note>In Francis Bacon’s utopian novel *New Atlantis*, "Solomon’s House" is a fictional institution dedicated to scientific discovery and the study of nature.</note> (One may see the 1st Part of my *Essay* pages 133, 194 and also above pages 61, 68.) That he simultaneously called this house the "House of God" <note>original: "Domus Dei"</note> on page 41 arose from the fact that all wisdom, and therefore reason itself, was derived directly from God and from union with Him; and it was then consistent that Christ should be the cornerstone of this house. But that Fludd really wanted this wisdom and this House of God to be directed toward the knowledge of nature is evident from the fact that he explicitly says on page 41 that it is so named after the stone upon which the patriarch Jacob had the well-known prophetic dream, and therefore called the stone Beth-El or God's House (Genesis Chapter XXVIII, Verse 17). Now, in the 45th note from this same author's *Mosaic Philosophy* <note>original: "Philosophia Moysaica"</note>, I have cited proof that in the introduction to this book he "divides everything present in nature into three worlds," and that he says:

> "the 'mental intuition' <note>original: "Intuitus mentalis"; a term for direct, non-discursive spiritual understanding</note> with which one penetrates into the eternal light that animates all creatures, is the threefold measure of Jacob's ladder, which he saw in a dream as he slept upon a stone; in its length, breadth, and depth the characters of the three imagined worlds were engraved, which is why this stone is called the house or 'tabernacle' <note>original: "tabernaculum"</note> (hut, dwelling) of God."

<meta>This page continues Nicolai's argument that Robert Fludd’s mystical writings were misunderstood by later historians. He asserts that Fludd's "House of Wisdom" was not a literal masonic or occult blueprint, but a metaphorical institution for natural philosophy, modeled after Francis Bacon’s scientific ideals.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>

It is therefore clearly visible that it is highly unwise to think of Freemasonry here; rather, the underlying idea is the very same one that Bacon intended with his proposal for a society, which he called the **House of Solomon**, of which I have already treated in my *Essay* <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angeführten Ort); refers to Nicolai's previously cited "Essay on the Accusations against the Order of the Knights Templar."</note>, and showed that the society of the **Rosicrucians** in England, when it transitioned into the Freemason society in that country, also wanted to carry out this idea.

To be sure, there is the difference that those who followed Bacon's reason at that time executed the idea rationally, whereas a dark visionary <note>German: "Schwärmer"; a term of Enlightenment-era criticism for someone caught up in religious or mystical delusions.</note> like **Fludd** mixed fantasies into it and set about schematizing wildly about houses and (p. 42) about castles, palaces, and large or small stones; but, as far as we know, he had no or very few followers. Therefore, when one examines the matter closely, the *Highest Good* <note>original: "Summum Bonum," the title of a work published under a pseudonym but attributed to Robert Fludd in 1629.</note> confirms my opinion regarding the original intention of the Rosicrucians in England, instead of refuting it.

I have also already shown above on page 62 that the union of prejudiced and unprejudiced men for a rational purpose could be very advantageous at that time. However, Mr. **Buhle** <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a historian who argued that Freemasonry grew directly out of Rosicrucianism, a view Nicolai is refuting here.</note> leans exclusively toward the side of prejudice and acts as if **alchemy**, **magic**, **schematism**, and **mysticism** were the essential elements, whereas they were merely incidental. And once he had grasped this thought, he proceeded blindly without looking left or right or investigating the matter thoroughly, imagining he had made great discoveries because, of course, a large part of what is most widely known had remained hidden from him.

But Fludd nevertheless says: "He works under the appearance of an architect..." <note>original: "Sub Architecti specie laborat"</note>
