"brother?" <note>original: "frater"</note> Indeed. I have pointed this out myself long ago; only Mr. B. <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a scholar who argued that Freemasonry evolved directly from Rosicrucianism—a theory Nicolai strongly opposed.</note> provides a new example of how shallowly he treats everything when he tries to force an application to **Freemasonry** from this. He cites on page 308 that it is stated in the *Highest Good* <note>original: "Summum Bonum," a 1629 work by Robert Fludd, writing under a pseudonym.</note> in the place cited: "The Apostle, in his particular instruction, reasoning by way of analogy, teaches us to reach the perfection of the mystery, either under the type of the **Farmer** or the **Architect**," <note>original Latin: "Apostolus in particulari sua instructione more analogico discurrens nos docet ad mysterii perfectionem, vel sub Agricolae vel Architecti typo, pertingere."</note> and adds: "Had the founders of the first Masonic Lodge chosen the image of the **land-cultivator** or **gardener**; we would now have **Free-farmers** or **Free-gardeners** in the allegorical sense instead of Freemasons. Mr. B. did not consider that for this passage, the 1st Epistle to the Corinthians, Chapter 3, Verses 10 and 11, is cited, and that in the verse immediately preceding, the Apostle Paul says: "You are God's **field** <note>original: "Ackerwerk"; often translated in Bibles of the time as "husbandry."</note>, and God's **building**," and that following this, in the cited verse, it says: "I have laid the foundation as a wise **master builder**." The *Highest Good* refers to this, and in no way can this have had an influence on the founders of the first Masonic Lodge, as Mr. B. dreams, for they likely never took this saying of the Apostle Paul into account.

Thus does Mr. B. play with words!

He says on page 323, just as perversely, regarding the magic connected with religion—which is supposed to have been the original constitution of Freemasonry: "It breathed the spirit of a liberal Protestantism." What? That would be a shell better than the kernel! He should also be requested to show the following completely perverse assertions in a rational context. If, according to him, <term>Rosicrucianism</term> and Freemasonry were originally one and the same, and (p. 30) "separated very soon after the founding of their institution," then it would be his duty to determine historically when this happened, and why it happened? It is strange, indeed, that he claims in that very same place that the Freemasons pursued philosophical goals, and the Rosicrucians pursued <term>fanaticisms</term> <note>original: *Schwärmereyen*; in this period, the term referred to irrational religious enthusiasm, delusions, or mystical "fever."</note>.

Now that Mr. Court Councilor *Buhle’s* great discovery has been presented more or less in its true form, there only remains to evaluate

## 2. the minor discovery.

This discovery is aimed at nothing more than completely striking me, poor man, down.

I already mentioned above on page 21 that in the first part of my *Essay* <note>Nicolai refers to his "Versuch über die Beschuldigungen, welche dem Tempelherrenorden gemacht worden" (1782), where he attempted to rehabilitate the reputation of the Knights Templar.</note> on page 89 ff., I had attempted a likely conjecture regarding a certain image. This image appears frequently in the testimonies of the old <term>Knights Templar</term> <note>A medieval military order suppressed in the early 14th century amid accusations of heresy, including the worship of a mysterious idol.</note>, which they are said to have worshipped in their general chapters, and in secret. Regarding this image, two French knights testified: the first said it was "an idol made in the shape of Baphomet," <note>original: "une Idole faite in figuram Baffometi"</note> and the other, "an idol, where the figure of Baphomet was depicted." <note>original: "une Idole, ubi erat depicta figura Baffometi"</note> In the Latin of the Middle Ages, the word *Figura* by no means signifies a figure [in the sense of a physical shape], but consistently only a sign; thus, it refers to an image upon which a sign was painted, or which was made in such a way that it could serve as a sign for something.

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critique of Johann Gottlieb Buhle’s theories regarding the Knights Templar. The previous page ended with Buhle’s claim that the Templars created a secret battle cry by combining the names of their enemies: the Pope and the Prophet Muhammad.</meta>

"...heads against which the Order of the Knights Templar had to fight."

One must truly call it a brand-new discovery that the Templars—just as they fought against Mahomet <note>An archaic European spelling of Muhammad</note> with swords and lances—also intended to fight against the Pope, and for that reason even changed their battle cry <note>original: *Feldgeschrey*</note>. The small detail that, regarding the Baphomet, the records spoke of a sign <note>original: *Figura*; Nicolai is distinguishing between a symbolic mark or character and a literal statue</note>, and not a human figure—least of all a two-headed one—is far too trivial for a man whose historical imagination operates on such a grand scale! Likewise, he skips over the difficulty that if the common Knights Templar had constantly used the name Mahomet (which was understandable to all of them) as their general battle cry, and were then given the name Baphomet (which was unintelligible to them) as their new battle cry, this surely would have caused a great stir and much inquiry. This, in turn, could have easily endangered the secret by leading to its betrayal; this "secret of the Order" <note>original: *Ordensgeheimniß*</note> that supposedly became a public battle cry!

But how in the world did the Templar Order manage to create not only a single figure out of Mahomet and the Pope, but also an entirely new name, *Baphomet*? Mr. V. <note>likely "Verfasser," the German word for "Author," referring here to Buhle</note> solves the riddle for us. He says:

> "The Order combined both names, in an
> "extremely natural and simple way, yet so
> "effectively that the greatest linguists and most shrewd
> "modern scholars have racked their brains in vain over
> "the meaning. Namely, the last two syllables of
> "Mahomet, *-homet*, were kept, and
> "in place of the *Ma-*, *Pap-*, or *Bap-* was substituted."

<summary>Friedrich Nicolai mocks the theory that the name "Baphomet" is a portmanteau of "Pope" and "Mahomet," arguing that a secret society would never use an unintelligible word as a public battle cry.</summary>
<keywords>Baphomet, etymology, battle cry, Knights Templar, Pope, Muhammad, Friedrich Nicolai, linguistic theories, secret societies</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page concludes Friedrich Nicolai’s polemic against Johann Gottlieb Buhle’s theories. Nicolai has been mocking Buhle's linguistic gymnastics regarding the origin of the name Baphomet, which Buhle claimed was a hybrid of "Pope" and "Mahomet" (Muhammad).</meta>

“Thus did the ill-famed <term>Baffomet, Baphomet</term><note>A mysterious idol or symbol that the Knights Templar were accused of worshipping during their trial in the early 14th century.</note> come into existence.”

Is that not quite charmingly natural and simple? Mr. B. <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a German philosopher and historian whom Nicolai is critiquing for his imaginative historical claims.</note> triumphantly adds: “The Baffomet of the Knights Templar was nothing else!”

Councilor Buhle remains consistently the same throughout this book, right until the end! It is not worth the effort to waste another word on him.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Remarks" or "Notes" section of Friedrich Nicolai's work. It follows the conclusion of his critique of Johann Gottlieb Buhle and provides clarifying details on his own theories regarding the origins of the Freemasons.</meta>

# N o t e s.

---

(1) See my *Essay on the Knights Templar* <note>original title: *Versuch über die Tempelherren*</note>, Part II, p. 7.

(2) I said deliberately: I wanted to deal with the origin of the **Freemason society**. The word **Freemasonry** can have at least twenty different meanings, and whoever does not precisely define the meaning intended each time is in danger of being misunderstood; it may also happen that he himself does not quite know what he is actually talking about, or perhaps has reasons to hide this from his readers. By using the word **society**, I intended to clearly and distinctly indicate to anyone not entirely inexperienced in the subject that I derive only the very first beginning of **Freemasonry** in general—where there was no talk of an **Order** yet, or what are now perhaps called the first two degrees—from **Rosicrucianism** <note>A mystical movement of the 17th century that claimed to possess secret knowledge of nature and the spiritual world.</note>. I maintained that what is now called the third degree first took its origin after the death of **King Charles I** <note>Charles I of England was executed in 1649; Nicolai links the "Master Mason" degree to the political efforts to restore the Stuart monarchy.</note>, through the secret plan of the **Loyalists** to restore the kingship, although it was changed significantly afterwards. In all of this, there is still no talk of Freemasonry as an **Order**. I have indicated this clearly enough in my book more than once for those who understand the matter even slightly. For example, Part II, p. 150: “The author of the *Treatise on Ancient and Modern Mysteries* (Berlin 1782) says on p. 279: ‘Masonry recognizes not Germany, but Britain, for its’”

<summary>Friedrich Nicolai defines his terminology, arguing that while the first two degrees of Freemasonry originated from Rosicrucianism, the third degree was a political creation by English Royalists following the execution of Charles I.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Rosicrucianism, King Charles I, Loyalists, Masonic degrees, Friedrich Nicolai, Knights Templar, Mysteries</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai's critique of Johann Gottlieb Buhle's scholarship. Nicolai is explaining how he previously hinted at a distinction between "Masonry" (the practice) and "the Order" (the formal organization), a distinction he believes Buhle fails to grasp.</meta>

...next fatherland," and again on p. 293: "It may be very well-founded that the Order was brought from France to Britain." However wavering this may seem, this author may well know exactly what he means. One only needs to understand precisely what is being discussed. This was a candid hint that this writer clearly distinguishes between **Masonry** and **the Order**: a hint that was perhaps taken poorly, and was perhaps the first cause of the disfavor afterwards publicly shown against me by this writer; even though at the time I wanted to indicate nothing more than that a man well-versed in **Freemasonry** makes a manifest distinction between **Masonry** and **the Order** <note>Nicolai distinguishes between the "craft" or "society" of Masonry and the more rigid, hierarchical "Orders" that developed later.</note>.

Mr. *Buhle*, who, as can be clearly seen, notices none of this, and who has only a highly superficial concept of **Freemasonry**—about which he nevertheless decides so boldly—promises on the title page of his work to conduct "a historical-critical investigation of the origin and the most significant fates of the **Order of Freemasons**." But he has said almost nothing in his book about the most significant fates of the **Freemason society** in general, which were so remarkable in the 18th century, and least of all has he dealt with them in a historical-critical manner. This would have primarily required showing: how the brotherhood actually became an **Order**, and what branches, sub-orders, and changes this **secret order** had and still has? To investigate all of this historically and critically would be an undertaking in which a writer could display much historical knowledge, power of judgment, and love of truth. One might call out to Mr. Court Councilor *Buhle*, while pointing a finger at the title of his book: *Here is Rhodes, jump here!* <note>original: "Hic Rhodus, hic salta!" A Latin proverb meaning "Prove what you can do right here and now," originating from Aesop's fables where a boaster claims to have made a giant leap in Rhodes and is told to repeat the feat on the spot.</note>

(6) The Unknown Superiors <term>Unbekannten Obern: The secret, high-ranking leaders who supposedly directed the Masonic Rite of Strict Observance from the shadows</term> gave coats of arms with symbols to the so-called nine provinces of the Order of the Knights Templar. I believe it is not inappropriate to make public here what very few likely know: that these artistic designs are taken from a rather forgotten but very remarkable book titled: *Jacobus Typotius's Divine and Human Symbols, etc.* <note>original: "Jacobi Typotii Symbola divina et humana", a famous emblem book from the early 17th century</note>, in three folio volumes, published in Prague from 1601 to 1603. The coats of arms and mottos of the alleged nine provinces of this supposed Order of the Knights Templar are to be found in the aforementioned work at the following locations:

I. *Aragon*: *In virtute tua* (In your strength). Vol. III, p. 37.
II. *Auvergne*: *Qui cupit* (He who desires). Vol. I, p. 27.
III. *Occitania*: *Prospero motu* (With a favorable movement). Vol. III, p. 49.
IV. *Leon*: *Audaces juvat* (Fortune favors the bold). Vol. III, p. 9.
V. *Burgundia*: *Mors omnia aequat* (Death makes all things equal). Vol. II, p. 74.
VI. *Britannia*: *Fata viam invenient* (The Fates will find a way). Vol. I, p. 44; Vol. II, p. 165.
VII. *Sub Albi et Odera* (Lower Elbe and Oder): *Labor viris convenit* (Labor is fitting for men). Vol. I, p. 34.
VIII. *Sub Rheno* (Lower Rhine): *Ultorem ulciscitur ultor* (The avenger avenges the avenger). Vol. I, p. 37.
IX. *Archipelagus* (The Archipelago): *Veritas persuadet* (Truth persuades). Vol. I, p. 9.

My readers will presumably not find it unpleasant that I have had these so-secretly-held coats of arms of the so-secretly-held provinces of the secret Knights Templar depicted on the copperplate frontispiece, based on the originals in Typotius's work. Of the four symbols and mottos which the Grand Master <term>Heermeister: The title used by the head of the Order</term> *ab Ense* <note>The Latinized Masonic name for Baron von Hund, meaning "of the Sword."</note> bestowed—through his legate <term>Legatum a Latere: A high-ranking deputy or envoy</term>, the Knight *a Struthione* <note>The Masonic name for von Hund's deputy, meaning "of the Ostrich."</note>—upon the four Masonic degrees he had (God willing) improved, I have been able to find only two in Typotius's book, namely:

> “To us, a **Hierography** is a more sacred representation of a thing or person: by marks or letters: not so much expressed as impressed. It is called more sacred, not only because the invention is sacred—as almost all things were sacred to the pagans, indeed mostly held by divine rite—but because, like mysteries, they hide more than they reveal.” <note>original Latin: "Hierographia nobis est significatio rei aut personae sacratior: notis aut litteris: non tam expressa, quam impressa. Sacratior dicitur, non tantum quod inventio sacra sit: ut res ipsae paganis omnes ferè sacrae, imò plerumque ritu divino habitae; sed quod mysteriorum instar, plus abdant, quam promant."</note>

The honorable man could not have said more clearly that his explanations of the **symbols** are actually no explanations at all, and, like the emblems themselves, are meant to be "holy" because they seem to say a very little bit quite clearly at times, but withhold the most; just as one finds this to be the case when one compares the explanations with the symbols themselves. Thus: the inscription of the eagle, *In Your strength* <note>original Latin: *In Virtute tua*</note>, is also found on another symbol representing a wreath or a crown, in the center of which one sees the name *Jehovah* in Hebrew (*Vol. I, p. 48*). *Typotius* says on *p. 30* in his quasi-explanation of this *simple sacred-drawing* <note>original Latin: *Hierographia Simplex*</note>: “It is a symbol of King *James Stuart of Scotland* <note>Referring to James VI of Scotland (later James I of England)</note>, who held God’s name higher than his crown, which is indicated by the phrase *In Your strength*.” However, this *In Your strength* also appears (as can be seen here on the title engraving) as the inscription around the coat of arms of the so-called First Province, titled *Aragon*, which features the cross as its emblem. Some wicked people have even come to the thought that the First and Ninth Provinces of the Order of the Temple, *Aragon* and *Archipelagus* <note>The "Strict Observance" divided Europe into nine administrative "provinces"; Nicolai is mocking the idea that these districts actually existed as anything more than fantasies</note>, were merely mystical and allegorical provinces that lay not in Europe but on the moon—where so much lies that concerns the newly created Order of the Temple and its Freemasonry.

(11) Everything said about the older history of **Freemasonry** in all the so-called Books of Constitutions published in England and Scotland is deceptive—I can assert this with conviction after careful and repeated investigation—as is much of what is found in English and French Masonic calendars. The same applies to several other English Masonic books: for example, the *Illustrations of Masonry* by **William Preston**, which is patched together entirely from lies and secret allusions, knowingly obscuring the truth; or the silly *History of Free-Masonry, with an Account of the Grand Lodge of Scotland* by **Alexander Lawrie** (Edinburgh 1804, large octavo), which is entirely unhistorical regarding the older history. (It is shameful that someone is even now solemnly announcing a translation of this useless book). In these and similar books, historical truth is intentionally mixed with manifest historical falsehoods, as far as one can judge from an impartial examination and attention to the allegorical allusions written in a kind of <term>cipher language: A coded way of writing used by secret societies to hide meanings from outsiders while signaling to initiates</term>. It has finally become necessary to say frankly that English Masonic writings are by no means free of secret interpretations regarding the purpose of the Order. Many well-meaning Freemasons are of the opinion that everything in England is "pure." <note>By "pure," Nicolai means free from the mystical, irrational, or political "higher degrees" that plagued continental Freemasonry at the time.</note> Such people do not understand what lies right before their eyes in the Book of Constitutions and the *Freemason’s Calendar for 1778*. They do not know the <term>Royal Arch: A high degree of Freemasonry that focuses on the rediscovery of lost sacred knowledge</term> and its clerical system, which already existed in London in the year 1760, as I know from someone who was himself initiated into the *Chapel* of the *Clergy of the Chapter of the Royal Arch*. <meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critique of Masonic histories. ...printed writings are traces thereof. One need only read the *Monthly Magazine for Oct. 1798*, p. 255. There one can find: "that after the Royal Arch Chapter had separated from the Grand Lodge in London in the year 1766, soon thereafter a Royal and Grand Conclave of the Knights Templar of St. John of Jerusalem emerged, which was in connection with Germany, and in which the institution of the Holy Supper <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion, suggesting a religious or sacramental element to their rituals</note> was also held." A word to the wise is enough! <note>original: "Sapienti sat!"</note> — Even now, this Order of the Knights Templar is constantly continued in England and even made public, just as this was once the plan in another country <note>likely referring to the "Strict Observance" movement in Germany</note>, which, however, was not carried out. I will cite only one highly remarkable example.

In the *Freemason's Magazine or general and complete library* Vol. II, large octavo, one finds in the issue for May 1794, p. 335, a circular letter from the Grand Master of the spiritual and military Order of the Knights Templar in England, addressed to the chapters of this brotherhood. The gentleman's name is **Thomas Dunckerley** and he resided in Southampton. The letter is dated April 11, in the Year of our Lord 1794, and in the 676th year of the Order <note>original: "Ao. ord. 676"; the Knights Templar often dated their documents from the founding of the order in 1118</note>. The Grand Master asserts it is his duty to charge every **dear Brother** and **Knight-Companion** that, should the enemy attempt an invasion, they—as Knights Templar—should hold themselves ready to join the military corps and be under the command of its officers in their local area, and to adopt the name "Prince Edward's Royal Volunteers." The battle cry was to be: *The Will of God!* The knights (**knight's companions**) are requested to wear the uniform of the corps where they serve as volunteers, along with the cross of the Order of the Knights Templar on a black ribbon between two buttonholes of the vest. An answer to this circular can be found in the same place. It is from **I— K—**, dated from Redruth in Cornwall on May 3, 1794.

> ->Distinguish'd all by black, red, blue,<-

> ->Adorn the heart that's just and true.<-

(14) The Ducat Society <term>Dukatengesellschaft; a short-lived society in Neuwied that functioned similarly to a modern pyramid scheme</term> was founded in the year 1746 in Neuwied, under the pretext that it should serve sociability and the common good. Every initiate had to pay one ducat monthly into the society’s treasury. As soon as he recruited three members, he was to receive one ducat monthly for himself; for the fifth man, again one ducat monthly for himself; further for the seventh, ninth, eleventh, thirteenth, and so on for every odd number. This Ducat Society had already grown to 416 members in a short time; but soon they also fell into disagreement among themselves over the ducats. The collecting of ducats was thereafter forbidden in the Prussian and other states at the end of the year 1747. One may see the *Berlin Monthly Journal* <note>original: "Berlinische Monatsschrift," a leading journal of the Berlin Enlightenment published from 1783 to 1796</note> from June 1790, page 555 ff. (21) In the year 1758, the first disclosures of the so-called **Strict Observance** <term>Strict Observance: A Masonic rite founded by Baron von Hund that claimed to be the successor to the Knights Templar and demanded absolute obedience to "Unknown Superiors"</term> occurred in Germany, through which the idea of **Unknown Superiors** was introduced. Yet a certain Rosa <note>Philipp Samuel Rosa (1720–1796), a disgraced pastor and Masonic recruiter who helped spread von Hund's system</note> had already been the precursor; I have found evidence in the papers of a deceased Freemason that similar ideas were being spread from France in a German lodge as early as the year 1745.

(22) In the year 1763, the secret Order of the Templars was first set in motion in Germany by the aforementioned **Johnson** <note>An adventurer who called himself George Frederick Johnson and claimed to be an authorized messenger of the "Unknown Superiors" of the Templars; he was later exposed as a fraud</note>.

(23) In the year 1773, the **Gold and Rosicrucians** <term>Gold- und Rosenkreuzer: A mystical, alchemical secret society that emerged as a conservative, occult rival to the Enlightenment-oriented Freemasonry</term> in southern Germany first made themselves heard.

(24) Lavater <note>Johann Caspar Lavater (1741–1801), a Swiss theologian and the leading proponent of physiognomy</note> was responsible for the gossip mentioned in the text on page 17. I had been in friendly correspondence with Lavater since 1767, at Zimmermann's <note>Johann Georg Ritter von Zimmermann (1728–1795), a prominent Swiss physician and philosopher</note> suggestion; this became quite lively in 1774, as it related mainly to **physiognomic subjects** <term>Physiognomy: The study of a person's facial features to determine their character or intellectual abilities</term>, with which I was very occupied at the time, and about which Lavater wanted to publish his well-known major work. He sent me various **silhouettes** <term>shadow portraits used as primary data for physiognomic analysis</term> to get my opinion on them. Among them was the portrait of Herder <note>Johann Gottfried Herder (1744–1803), a hugely influential philosopher and theologian</note>, with Lavater’s attached judgment—concerning which he later blamed himself, saying he had initially been mistaken and had considered the man to be weak (the silhouette was indeed inaccurate, much too rounded)—but he added: "nevertheless, his **Document** <note>Original: *Urkunde*; a reference to Herder's 1774 work *Aelteste Urkunde des Menschengeschlechts* (Oldest Document of the Human Race)</note> remains a pyramid." To this I replied: "Yes! The *Document* is a pyramid, an immense building, decaying on the outside and empty and dark on the inside, at whose layout one is astonished, and whose purpose one can only uncertainly surmise; the curious man looks at it once, but will hardly return there a second time."

<summary>Nicolai provides a timeline for the rise of the Strict Observance and Rosicrucianism in Germany while recounting a 1774 critique he shared with Lavater regarding Herder's work and character.</summary>

> “I confess, I do not love books that resemble masses of stone

Lavater was rude enough to share this letter with Herder—and, if I am not mistaken, through a third party at that. Herder flew into a most violent rage over it. — I must remark here that in everything I wrote concerning the *Oldest Document* <note>Referring to Herder's *Aelteste Urkunde des Menschengeschlechts* (The Oldest Document of the Human Race, 1774), a dense and poetic work on Genesis that frustrated Enlightenment rationalists</note>, nothing related to the **Freemasonic** intentions that Herder had with this book. Even in a friendly letter to Herder, in which I brought to his attention how much damage he was doing to himself by writing so pretentiously and obscurely, nothing was said about that. Whether Herder also took offense at this frankness of mine, I do not know; I do know, however, that through Lavater's indiscretion, the situation only grew worse. Suffice it to say, I had not touched the somewhat "out-of-tune string" of Freemasonry, though it pained me that a man of Herder’s caliber confirmed the delusions of the visionaries. D. In these, one of the most distinguished German scholars demonstrates what a completely confused imagination reigns in this book, paired with a most meager, borrowed erudition—and yet, in this same work, the greatest and most thorough scholars are treated with the utmost contempt. <meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critique of Johann Gottfried Herder’s later, more mystical works, which Nicolai views as a departure from Herder's earlier intellectual brilliance. Nicolai then pivots to defend the possibility of a factual history of Freemasonry against contemporary skeptical scholars who claimed such a history could only ever be speculative.</meta>

[I] believe) he would have been ashamed of upon more mature reflection (except perhaps for a few individual passages). It deserves to reach posterity just as little as his book on the Apocalypse <note>Herder's *Maran Atha* (1779), an interpretation of the Book of Revelation</note>—which was more fanatical than imaginative—even though the latter is occasionally clever in its hypotheses; for a brilliant mind, even when following follies, rarely writes in a completely foolish manner. Both works, however, are on the whole senseless and—for the Herder who wrote *The Spirit of Hebrew Poetry*, the *Ideas for the Philosophy of the History of Mankind*, the *Letters on Humanity*, and the *Adrastea* <note>These are Herder's most celebrated Enlightenment-era works on culture, history, and aesthetics</note>—I say it frankly—entirely unworthy.

(26) I owe it to myself to speak these sad truths plainly here, because the shameless bias of certain people still drives them to speak against the truth and against clear evidence regarding Herder's attack. See, for example, the *Jena Literary Journal* <note>original: *Jenaische Literaturzeitung*</note> of 1805, No. 311. Such a person will then see clearly on whose side knowledge, love of truth, and fairness resided.

(27) An insightful scholar says many good things about this subject in Nos. 182 and 183 of the *Göttingen Scholarly Announcements* <note>original: *Göttingische gelehrte Anzeigen*</note> from the year 1805; but I cannot hide the fact that, in his commendable zeal for truth and justice, he dismisses **Freemasonry** and its history far too broadly, and from the few individual details he may know, he concludes far too quickly about the whole. He asserts, for example, on page 1811:

> „Whoever has even the slightest sense for historical criticism must be convinced that in **Masonic history** only hypotheses can be expected, 'because genuine, continuous documents are lacking.'“

How could this brave man possibly assert the latter with such certainty that he even wants to deter everyone from undertaking a history of **Freemasonry**...

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of his historical methodology regarding Freemasonry. He is responding to critics who argued that a factual history of a secret society is impossible due to a lack of public documentation. Nicolai argues that logical deduction and "historical criticism" are necessary tools when archives are hidden or incomplete.</meta>

[to take] from? Certainly, there are many very important documents in <term>Freemason archives</term> <note>Collections maintained by Masonic lodges containing rituals, minutes, and correspondence, often kept hidden from the public</note>, as well as in the possession of private individuals in several places; some of these are even well known to me personally. To be sure, a history built entirely without documents and based only on hypotheses is as good as nothing at all; however, few or essentially no histories are based entirely on a continuous chain of documents, and no one should say that where documents are occasionally missing, one should only expect mere guesswork. If two or three credible narrators contradict each other regarding old and new events, must one not then choose the most probable account, or seek to reconcile the various reports? This, however, is achieved through tested hypotheses. Their value is unmistakable, and it is precisely through them that genuine <term>historical criticism</term> <note>The scholarly practice of analyzing the authenticity and reliability of historical sources</note> can be most effectively utilized. 

Is it then fair to place even stricter demands on someone who attempts to unravel the dark history of a secret society—where everything is designed to hide its true nature in order to preserve the secret and to mislead those who wish to fathom it—than on any historian who deals with public events? Should the former not rather be entitled to greater leniency when he, in a field where nothing at all had yet been produced, first dared to break the path? In great matters, even to have willed is enough! <note>original: "*In magnis et voluisse sat est!*" — A quote from the Roman poet Propertius, often used to suggest that in difficult or noble undertakings, the merit lies in the attempt itself</note> It would be another matter if it were said that one should not expect to obtain a true history of Freemasonry publicly. I have stated the reasons for this on page 15 and page 23.

Just as little can I agree with the opinion of this otherwise well-meaning man when he judges Freemasonry in general far too contemptuously on pages 18 and 19, and wishes to deny this society almost all utility. A long experience has taught me quite differently, although I, as...

<summary>Nicolai defends the use of "tested hypotheses" and historical criticism to reconstruct the history of secret societies like the Freemasons, arguing that the inherent secrecy of such groups requires a different standard of evidence than public history.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of Freemasonry, shifting from the historical methodology discussed on the previous page to the practical, social, and psychological benefits of the society. The author is likely Friedrich Nicolai, responding to a specific scholar's dismissal of the organization as mere "folly."</meta>

...like him, I detest all abuses and smile at all follies; yet some things which he considers entirely foolish, but perhaps does not even know well enough, might just not be mere folly. How such a society first arose, and how it was subsequently changed? Also whether, if it did not yet exist, it ought to be established? That is not the question here. 

Suffice it to say, it exists; and, according to my experience, the **Freemason society** <note>The fraternal organization of Freemasonry, which the author argues has social value despite its critics</note> can work manifold good, provided it does not degenerate into a secret order, if it rests upon itself and sound reason free from foreign power, and if the **obsession with secrecy** <note>original: "Geheimnißsucht"</note> and dependence on <term>Unknown Superiors</term> <note>original: "Unbekannten Obern"; a reference to the high-degree Masonic systems, such as the Strict Observance, where members were told they were commanded by secret leaders whose identities were hidden</note> are banished from it (and in many places, that is no longer even the case). It is truly not to be regarded as entirely useless—that which leads people to greater **sociability** <note>original: "Geselligkeit"; referring to the Enlightenment ideal of polite and constructive social interaction</note>—and it would be acting unwisely to destroy something that already exists and can be directed toward the better. 

Even the fact that in the Freemason society all estates are united and have equal rights could have great utility in many places where pride and pedantry cause every **caste** <note>original: "Caste"; used here to describe the rigid social classes of the 18th century</note> to strictly separate itself from the others, and where the upper class despises that which is only a tiny bit lower; and the utility can be even more manifold for a truly thinking man. The **knowledge of human nature** <note>original: "Menschenkenntniß"; the practical wisdom of understanding the character and motivations of others</note> which I have acquired through my connection with the Freemason society is something I would truly not want to do without for anything. 

— If a scholar judges so unfavorably an intimate association of a great number of people—which perhaps amounts to more than a million—he likely has only his own needs or his own inclinations in mind, rather than the highly varied needs and innocent inclinations of other persons of various kinds and various social ranks,

<summary>The author defends Freemasonry as a valuable social institution that promotes equality between social classes and provides practical insights into human nature, provided it avoids the pitfalls of extreme secrecy and hidden hierarchies.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Enlightenment, sociability, social class, Unknown Superiors, human nature, Friedrich Nicolai</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of the Freemason society. While the previous page focused on the historical origins and the potential for social utility, Nicolai now addresses specific criticisms regarding the internal politics and personal failings of members, arguing that these are universal human traits rather than specific flaws of the brotherhood.</meta>

Age, Activity, and Position in Civil Society.

I certainly fully agree with what this well-meaning **Reviewer** <note>original: "Recensent"; likely a critic in a contemporary journal like the *Allgemeine Deutsche Bibliothek*</note> says on page 1821 regarding the evil consequences arising from envy and hatred, as well as what he says on page 1823 about the **obsession with power** <note>original: "Regierungssucht"; the drive to dominate or control the administration of the lodge</note>; however, in this regard, the Freemason society is merely a miniature image of human society in general: and should we judge the latter contemptibly as a whole simply because, according to the usual course of the world, obsession with power, hatred, and envy are also found within it? Rather, do not reasonable people in the great course of the world distance themselves as much as possible from those driven by hatred, envy, and the obsession with power, and stick together among themselves? It is exactly the same in the small world-course of the Freemason society.

I, at least, can assure with truth that I have found examples of true friendship, brotherly union, and compatibility in the Freemason society that are not at all rare. That I harbor no exaggerated notions of the value of secret societies, I have already expressed several times in my writings, for example, in my *Declaration regarding the Order of the Illuminati (1788. 8)* <note>original: "Erklärung über den Illuminatenorden"; Nicolai’s published defense and clarification regarding his brief association with the controversial secret society</note> on pages 71, 89 to 93. But fairness and impartiality also require that one does not represent the Freemason society, in which several thousand upright and benevolent people take part, as merely contemptible. So many learned men, beginning with **Garve** <note>Christian Garve (1742–1798), a popular philosopher of the Enlightenment known for his work on ethics and social duties</note>, would only too gladly pass judgment on Freemasonry and its history—about which they hear so much and which always eludes the eyes of their scholarship and argumentation—before they have had either the opportunity or enough patience to inform themselves accurately about it!

(28) I have sometimes been accused of a penchant for etymological investigations. It is possible that I

<summary>Nicolai argues that internal conflicts like envy and power-lust within Freemasonry are reflections of human nature at large, rather than unique failings of the order, and he critiques outside scholars for judging the society without proper experience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of his research methods. While the previous page addressed the social behavior within the Freemason society, Nicolai now shifts to defending his etymological approach to history, arguing that studying the origins of words is essential for understanding the history of ideas.</meta>

...on a path where it is even easier to lose one's way than on any other, I also occasionally went astray. But my diligence in investigating the **origin of names** <note>original: "Ursprung der Benennungen"</note> arose solely from my love for defining and **clarifying concepts** <note>original: "Verdeutlichung der Begriffe"</note>. Anyone who merely plays with accidental similarities of words and syllables is engaging in a trivial game. Not so the person who has a rational purpose in **etymological investigations** <note>original: "etymologische Untersuchungen"</note> and does not lose sight of the **history of nations** <note>original: "Völkergeschichte"</note> as well as the nature of language. Words are the signs of our concepts of things, and thereby of the things themselves in relation to us. By investigating the origin of names, one can very often get closer to the trail of the origin and sequence of concepts—and even in history, to the connection of events that have not been sufficiently explained—just as one recognizes the course and currents of a river most surely if one can follow it back to its first source. The Greeks therefore called this endeavor, which we simply call **word-research** <note>original: "Wortforschung"</note>, **truth-research** <note>original: "Wahrheitsforschung"</note>; the investigation through which the **Etymon** <note>original: "Ετυμον"; Greek for "the true" or "the literal meaning of a word"</note>, the True or the Essential, is more closely recognized.

(29) It is truly not my manner to boast of myself. But I must nonetheless remark that after my essay appeared, several people took on the air of looking far over my head, as if I had merely occupied myself with whims and fantasies. These were people whose way was either blocked by my investigations, or who happened to find something—or merely believed they had found something—on the path that I had clearly been the first to show. Consequently, to make themselves feel important, they treated my investigations with contempt. The first among these was a certain disguised **Wilhelm ab Indagine** <note>A pseudonym used by various authors in the 18th century, often for mystical or controversial writings; Nicolai is likely referring to a specific critic of his work on the Templars or Masons</note>, who in the third part of the

<summary>Nicolai defends etymology as a rigorous historical tool for uncovering the "truth" of concepts and events, comparing it to tracking a river to its source. He also begins a rebuttal against critics, specifically a writer using the pseudonym Wilhelm ab Indagine, who dismissed his research as mere fantasy.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, historical method, Friedrich Nicolai, Wilhelm ab Indagine, truth-research, word-research, philosophy of language, history of ideas</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai's defense of his scholarship. Following his discussion on the importance of etymology from the previous page, he now addresses specific critics who have disparaged his work on the history of the Rosicrucians, specifically targeting a reviewer in the Wurttemberg Repertory and the philosopher Johann Gottlieb Buhle.</meta>

...the *Wurttemberg Repertory* <note>original: "Wirttembergischen Repertoriums"; a scholarly journal published in 1785</note> attacked me and, regarding the history of **Rosicrucianism** <note>original: "Rosenkreuzerey"</note>—a subject of which he had only the most superficial knowledge—judged with the utmost boldness. In the old **A. D. B.** <term>Allgemeine Deutsche Bibliothek (General German Library), an influential Enlightenment journal edited by Nicolai himself</term> (Vol. LVI, pp. 586 to 596), I pointed out to this shallow writer, quite gently, his repeated contradictions and his ignorance. For instance, he translated *Naometria* <note>A prophetic-mystical work by Simon Studion (1604) influential to the Rosicrucians; the title is derived from the Greek words for "temple" and "measure."</note> senselessly as "New-measurer" <note>German: "Neumesser"</note>, when it actually means "Temple-measurer" <note>German: "Tempelmesser"</note>, and so forth. 

Mr. Buhle—*a lid worthy of its pot* <note>Latin: "dignum patella operculum"; a proverb meaning two people are a perfect match in their negative qualities</note>—calls this most shallow writer (who, admittedly, has still read somewhat more than he has) "the excellent one." He copies from him without further examination and likewise assumes the air of being able to overlook me, though he has investigated nothing here thoroughly and traced almost nothing back to the sources. I, on the other hand, have truly and faithfully researched, examined the subjects impartially, and have actually read all the books I cite. 

This is often the fate of laborious historical research: someone speaks loudly to disparage it, and one does not expect them to have the audacity to belittle what they have not even thoroughly examined themselves. For this reason, I wish only to remind the rational and impartial reader—who may be unable or unwilling to re-examine the subjects discussed (and how few can be expected to do so!)—that in both appendices of my essay, I clearly and truly made use of a great many books that no one before me had even cited. Others later cited them following my lead, though without me, they would likely never have known of them. From this alone it is clear that I set to work in earnest, and did not shy away from the driest and most laborious reading to find the trail of truth. How much I read in vain cannot be seen in my book; but anyone who has some [knowledge] of such things...

<summary>Nicolai defends the rigor of his historical research against critics like Johann Gottlieb Buhle, highlighting his superior knowledge of primary sources and correcting a specific etymological error regarding the Rosicrucian-aligned work "Naometria."</summary>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s defense of his rigorous historical methodology. He contrasts his own exhaustive research with the superficiality of his rivals, Johann Gottfried Herder and Johann Gottlieb Buhle, specifically regarding the origins of symbols used by the Rosicrucians and Freemasons.</meta>

...understanding of historical research must well know that whoever finds certain new things will very likely not have spotted them right by the wayside, but only discovered them after much research. Therefore, with regard to the history of the **Rosicrucians** and **Freemasons**, I do not deserve to be placed in the same class as people who write all sorts of things without thorough investigation—as **Herder** once did and now **Buhle**.

As proof of my effort and care in looking everywhere I could hope to find anything, I will only cite a few examples. In the book *Abraxas Proteus* <note>A work on mystical gemstones and Gnosticism</note> with the commentary of **Chifflet** <note>original: "Chifletii"; Jean-Jacques Chifflet (1588–1660), a renowned French physician and antiquarian</note> (Antwerp 1647, 4to.), I discovered symbols of the **compass**, the **square**, and the **level** <note>original: "Zirkels, des Winkelmaaßes und der Bleywage"</note> (the latter is shown upside down on the stone in Figure I on the title page of the first part of my *Essay* <note>Nicolai’s *Essay on the Nature of the Oldest Christian Religion* (1782), which included notes on Rosicrucianism</note>) in much older times. These were symbols that the **Rosicrucians**—and after them the **Freemasons** (N.B. along with other signs)—later utilized, and I had them engraved in copper. Probably no one had ever read through Chifflet with this intention before. However, I did not immediately conclude prematurely (as many others might have done) from what I found that when these **Abraxas stones** <term>Engraved gemstones from late antiquity, often used as amulets, featuring mystical figures and symbols associated with Gnosticism</term> were made, Rosicrucians or Freemasons were already in existence. Rather, I only showed that long before the Rosicrucians, these signs indicated the order in the laws of nature, just as I also noted that **Valentin Andreae** <note>Johann Valentin Andreae (1586–1654), a theologian who played a central role in the creation of the Rosicrucian myths</note> himself used them in this sense. (See my *Essay*, Part I, p. 193.)

I also read through **Paciaudi's** <note>Paolo Maria Paciaudi (1710–1785), an Italian archaeologist and librarian</note> book *On the Cult of Saint John the Baptist* <note>original: "de Cultu S. Joannis Baptistae" (Latin)</note> (Rome 1755, 4to.; see the same, p. 160), which everyone concerned with the history of **Freemasonry** should have consulted, but which had never occurred to anyone before to examine for this purpose. My effort in reading this tedious book completely through was not entirely without fruit; for long after, once I had...

<summary>Nicolai defends his scholarly reputation by detailing his discovery of Masonic and Rosicrucian symbols (the compass, square, and level) in ancient Gnostic "Abraxas" gems. He emphasizes that he correctly identifies these as ancient symbols of natural order rather than proof of ancient Freemasonry, unlike his less careful contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>Friedrich Nicolai, Rosicrucians, Freemasons, Abraxas stones, Johann Valentin Andreae, Jean-Jacques Chifflet, Paolo Maria Paciaudi, historical methodology, Masonic symbols</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Nicolai's discussion of how his wide-ranging historical reading—from Renaissance art history to medieval travelogues—allows him to distinguish between actual Freemasonry and superficial similarities found in other organizations.</meta>

...parts of the inner workings of **Freemasonry** became better known to me, I came upon the trail of a very peculiar explanation for a certain matter with the help of this book. That I remained constantly attentive to these subjects even when reading works of a completely different kind is evident from the fact that in **Vasari's** *Lives of the Artists* <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), author of the foundational text on Renaissance art history</note> I found the humorous **Society of the Trowel** <note>original: "Gesellschaft der Mauerkelle"; in Italian, *Compagnia della Cazzuola*, a social dining club of Florentine artists known for their elaborate pranks and pageantry</note> (see Part I, p. 214 of my *Essay*). This society originated in Florence at the beginning of the 16th century and bore a superficial resemblance to the **Masonic Society**, yet it was in no way **Freemasonry**. I could cite many more such examples.

On this occasion, I must rebuke a small oversight in an otherwise estimable scholarly journal, as it could have some influence on the history of **Freemasonry**. In the *Memoirs of the National Institute of Sciences and Arts, Moral and Political Sciences, Volume I, Year XII, p. 422 ff.*, the travel account of a Frenchman, **Bertrandon de la Brocquière**, to and from Jerusalem in the years 1432 and 1433, was published from the manuscript with notes by the well-regarded **Legrand d’Aussy**. On page 632, the traveler recounts that on his return journey he traveled with two German noblemen, **Monseigneur de Vasse** and **Messire Jacques Troussel**, who showed him great friendship. When he came from **Melk** <note>A town in Lower Austria, famous for its Benedictine abbey</note> to **Valle**, they met a nobleman from Bavaria whom **Messire Jacques Troussel** wanted to have hanged from a tree in the garden, though he managed to escape. The traveler adds: “The reason for this anger was that Messire Jacques, as well as most of the people he had with him, belonged to the secret company, and that the nobleman, who was also a member, had misused his position” <note>original French: "La raison de cette colère est, que Messire Jacques... était de la secrette compagnie, et que le gentilhomme... avait mésusé."</note>. **Legrand d’Aussy** adds the note: “Probably this refers to **Freemasonry**” <note>original French: "Probablement il s'agit ici de Franc-maçonnerie"</note>; a note which the reviewer of this writing in the *Göttingen Scholarly Announcements* <note>original: "Göttingschen Gelehrten Anzeigen"; one of the oldest and most influential academic journals in the German-speaking world</note>...

<summary>Nicolai demonstrates his extensive research by identifying historical societies that resemble Freemasonry, such as the Florentine Society of the Trowel, while cautioning against misidentifying any "secret company" in medieval texts as Masonic.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Giorgio Vasari, Society of the Trowel, Bertrandon de la Brocquière, Legrand d’Aussy, secret societies, historical methodology, Florence, Melk</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicolai's critique of a French travelogue by Bertrandon de la Brocquière, specifically addressing a scholarly misinterpretation that might confuse the medieval "Vehmic courts" with early Freemasonry.</meta>

from the year 1805, No. 184, cites directly without refutation. Thus, the delusion could very easily be revived that **Freemasonry** had existed as early as the 14th century, even in Germany. It is surprising that it did not immediately occur to a German scholar that the *secret company* <note>original French: "secrette compagnie"</note> was nothing other than the Society of the **Free Jurors** <term>Freyschöpfen: lay judges or initiated members of the Vehmic courts</term> of the secret **Vehmic Court** <term>Fehmgericht: a medieval system of vigilante tribunals in Westphalia and later across Germany, known for their secrecy, unique signs of recognition, and summary executions</term>, which was so powerful, especially in the 14th century. These secret Vehmic courts also spread into **Swabia** from the year 1427 (see Datt, *On Public Peace* <note>original Latin: "Datt de pace publica," a legal history by Johann Philipp Datt published in 1698</note>, p. 733). It is well known that the Free Jurors hanged anyone condemned by their court—and especially a traitorous Free Juror (as was the case with the **Bavarian** mentioned here, as indicated by the word *misconduct* <note>original French: "mésuser," meaning to act wrongly or betray a trust</note>)—as soon as they could get hold of him, from a tree. They used a tree rather than a gallows as a sign of their "free" judicial office, which was not bound to any established manorial court site. 

Furthermore, the place *Valse* <note>original spelling in the French travelogue</note> mentioned in this old travel account is the castle of **Nieder-Wallsee** in Lower Austria on the **Enns**, located between **Melk** and **Enns**. The Lord of Wallsee <note>original French: "Seigneur de Valse"</note> was very likely **Reinprecht von Wallsee**, who had served as the **High Steward** <term>Oberhofmeister: the highest-ranking official in a princely household</term> to the Duke of Austria (later Emperor **Albrecht**), and at that time, according to the traveler's report, resided in **Linz** as the High Captain of Upper Austria <note>original: "Oestreich ob der Enns"</note>. The so-called *Sir Jacques Trousset* <note>original French: "Sire Jacques Trousset"</note>, on the other hand, was clearly **Jakob Truchses von Waldburg**, the progenitor of the still-thriving family line of the Counts Truchses, who died in the year 1460. One finds later in the cited travel account on p. 634 that he received the French traveler "at **Waldburg** in Swabia, one of his castles" <note>original French: "à Walpourch en Souabe, l’un de ses châteaux"</note> and hosted him in a friendly manner.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the Order of the Blue Cross, a Catholic military order founded in 1619. The author explores the connection between this group and the Rosicrucian movement, suggesting that the "Blue Knights" served as a Counter-Reformation alternative to Protestant secret societies.</meta>

almost nothing was known about this knightly order outside of Germany, from which it can clearly be understood that the defense of Christians against infidels they had vowed was actually intended only to be directed against the heretical Protestants. This order of the so-called **Blue Knights**, which seems to have completely dissolved toward the end of the **Thirty Years' War** <note>The devastating conflict (1618–1648) that reshaped central Europe</note>—since I have found no further mention of it after 1630—is spoken of here and there in the writings of that time in a peculiar way, as if it were a secret order. Several individual circumstances make it probable to me that it belonged to the Catholic branch of the **Rosicrucian Society**. I will only cite one trace of this, as I cannot go into many others here.

A small pamphlet printed in quarto in the year 1620 (apparently by a Protestant Austrian) under the title: “Chaos to several *those principally interested* <note>original Latin: *Principaliter Interessirte*</note> and *informed parties* <note>original: *impartirte*</note> in the current *confused universal crisis* <note>original Latin/German hybrid: *confundirten universal paroxysmo*</note> of the laudable House of Austria, and its hitherto subordinate provincial estates, councilors, and deceivers,” contains such hints. Of the Rosicrucian Brothers, it says there: “These *hidden wonders of the world* <note>original Latin: *Occulta miraculi mundi*</note> are said to be no less *interested* <note>original: *interessiret*</note> in this war than the **Jesuits**.” Of the new *Knights* <note>original: *Cavallieri*</note> of the Blue Cross, it is said: “What these *Knights* <note>original: *Cavallieri*</note> have yet *achieved* <note>original: *praetiret*</note> in this war will require little space in the chronicles; only that they wished to *persuade* <note>original: *persuadiren*</note> one among them to break his wartime oath and vow, which he did not accept, but rather with a steadfast spirit *explained* <note>original: *expliciret*</note> the rule of the order better to them than they understood it themselves. — This order is founded upon pride, and therefore it stands like a half-broken tripod, unless it be that this were the *fraternity* <note>original: *fraternitet*</note> of the Rosy Cross.” A certain knight, *Theophilus a Cygno* <note>Latin: "Theophilus of the Swan"</note>

<meta>This page continues Friedrich Nicolai’s critical examination of contemporary scholars—specifically targeting Mr. B. (likely Johann Gottlieb Buhle)—who dismiss the complex history of secret societies. It then transitions into Nicolai’s own bibliographical research regarding the origins of the term "Freemason" and the symbolic shift toward the Temple of Solomon in the late 17th century.</meta>

...[Rosicrucia]nism and Freemasonry! He actually says on page 24 of his book, with his usual flippancy:

> "Who would expect a man who knows the value of time and has a more important calling to examine everything that has ever been dreamed or babbled about Rosicrucianism and Freemasonry?"

I certainly know the value of time as well as anyone, and I have all due respect for the "more important calling" of the Mr. Court Councilor <note>German: *Hofrath*, a title for high-ranking officials or scholars</note> to teach young people the fundamentals of the sciences. However, it was entirely up to him whether he wanted to stoop to the "less important business" of conducting a historical investigation. But, as soon as he found it appropriate to do so, it had to be the most important thing for him, and it was absolutely to be expected—indeed, it was his duty—to examine everything. How can he distinguish what is daydreaming and babbling from what is not without an examination, especially in the case of subjects so deliberately obscured? It is, of course, much harder to leave nothing unexamined than to say in a decisive tone without any examination (as Mr. B. does almost everywhere in his book): "Undisputedly, this is so, and that is so!"

<meta>This page continues Nicolai's linguistic and historical investigation into the origins of Freemasonry. Having previously discussed the dating of the symbolic shift to the Temple of Solomon, he now focuses on the specific English terminology and recounts a personal anecdote regarding the plagiarism and distortion of his research by an acquaintance in Berlin.</meta>

The word **Mason** can mean a common stonemason in **England**, and also a Freemason; but **Freemason** never means anything other than a Freemason in the current sense. The guild-affiliated carpenters are indeed called ***Freemen of the Carpentry***, because they are *free*, that is, they belong to the guild <note>German: *Zunft*</note>; but an Englishman will never say: ***Free-carpenter, Free-grocer, Free-fishmonger, Free-barber***, etc., when he speaks of people who are ***Freemen***, that is, actual masters in the guild of carpenters, grocers, fishmongers, barbers, and so forth. Evidently, then, the designation **Freemason** already had a special significance within the Masons' guild by 1677. **Johnson** <note>Referring to Samuel Johnson, whose famous *Dictionary of the English Language* (1755) omitted the term "Freemason," likely viewing it as a specific technical or secret society term rather than general vocabulary.</note> did not include the word **Freemason** in his dictionary at all.

(39) Specifically, a stranger—who resided in **Berlin** for a year and certainly had no small amount of experience in **Freemasonry**—was very taken with this essay. Having given an express promise never to make use of it, he took some notes from it and presumably kept even more in his memory, albeit in a flawed and vague manner. After a few years, he had parts of it printed abroad, but so mutilated, so far removed from the necessary context, and with such perverse additions, that it became entirely unserviceable. Upon reading it, I could indeed recognize my main idea, but otherwise could hardly recognize my own work at all. When I saw this traveler again a few years later, I told him straight to his face that it was he who had behaved so impolitely toward me. He tried to excuse himself by citing his good intentions. But a purported good intention can never excuse someone for breaking their word. Moreover, no good intention could ever be achieved through the silly and distorted way in which my ideas were used and presented out of their proper context. Still

If this should come to his sight, let him be ashamed. This is not the only case where I have been rewarded with ingratitude for my good will.

(40) In **Paris**, there had formerly and for a long time been a **Brotherhood of the Holy Sepulchre**, as such brotherhoods are known to be very common in Catholic countries, and are established and favored by all monastic orders, but especially by the **Jesuits**, in order to attract people of all ranks. The **Brotherhood of the Holy Sepulchre** in **Paris** had always consisted only of a few common craftsmen. They were mockingly called *the Brotherhood of the Sirloin* <note>original: *la Confrèrie de l'Aloyau*</note>, that is, literally, the brothers of the loin of a beef roast; but *Sirloin* <note>*l'Aloyau*</note> is an <term>anagram</term> <note>A word or phrase formed by rearranging the letters of another; here, the mockery lies in the phonetic and literal similarity between the cut of meat and the Jesuit founder.</note> of *Loyola* <note>Ignatius of Loyola (1491–1556), the founder of the Society of Jesus (Jesuits).</note>, and at that time, when the **Jesuits** were banished from **France** but knew how to nestle back in at every corner, the mockers labeled Jesuit institutions with this name. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Brotherhood of the Holy Sepulchre" in Paris, describing how it was reorganized to attract high-ranking nobility and its relationship to Masonic systems like the Knights Templar.</meta>

the brotherhood established by the **Jesuits** was very clumsily managed, to renew it, and even to place the **Count of Artois** <note>Charles-Philippe (1757–1836), the youngest brother of Louis XVI and the future King Charles X of France.</note> at its head. This order was to consist of three classes: 1) knights, 2) travelers, 3) pious brothers. They had already devised an order ribbon and a ceremonial dress, along with commanderies <note>Administrative districts or manors belonging to a military order.</note> and honorary positions; likewise, they had seen to it that the admissions cost a fair amount of money, all just like with the highly praiseworthy **Knights Templar**. The Court forbade them from wearing the order's cross, and after a long lawsuit, this plan failed. This is also one of the manifold forms which so-called **Higher Freemasonry** <note>Refers to "High Degree" Masonry, which added complex layers of ritual and hierarchy beyond the standard three degrees.</note> in **France** had to adopt. This newly formed **Order of the Holy Sepulchre** clearly has much in common with the **Freemasonic Templar Order**, which had existed in **France** long before it was brought to **Germany**. The intention of having an order cross of this chimeric <note>Imaginary or fanciful; based on a "chimera."</note> order worn publicly is quite similar to the plan later proposed in another country—though not executed—to make the **Freemasonic Templar Order** public. The intention in both cases was to draw noble people into this association, and thereby provide the **Unknown Superiors** <note>A term used in the "Strict Observance" rite of Freemasonry referring to secret, high-ranking leaders whose identities were hidden from the lower members.</note> with even greater influence, along with a substantial income in money; for the High Superiors by no means despise money.

These *Memoirs*, which are not generally known enough (of which **Petit de Bachaumont** edited the first four volumes), are a kind of interesting newspaper, covering not only literary matters but also many other events that occurred in **Paris**. (41) The otherwise so worthy and learned **Dr. Semler** <note>Johann Salomo Semler (1725–1791), a famous German theologian often called the "father of German rationalism."</note> was, as is well known, alas! afflicted with a belief in **alchemy**, and even wanted to produce **brilliant gold** <note>original: "Lustgold"; likely referring to pinchbeck or a decorative brass alloy resembling gold.</note>, **stinking gold** <note>original: "Stinkgold"; likely referring to an alchemical preparation involving sulfur.</note>, and so on. Therefore, when looking at the **Rosicrucians**, he saw nothing but alchemy—aside from the **theosophy** <note>A system of philosophy or religion that proposes to establish direct, personal contact with the divine.</note> that was vividly present in his mind from his studies of **church history**. In the process, he confused everything so thoroughly, and skipped so strangely from one subject to another in his collections, that it will be very difficult to separate the few noteworthy items within them from the vast amount of useless material. Furthermore, the honest man used to—as he himself recounts so as not to lose any time—read alchemical and consequently **Rosicrucian** writings, along with other low-quality works, daily while he lingered on the **privy** <note>original: "Abtritte"; a polite historical term for a toilet.</note> (see his own autobiography, Part I, page 329). Thus, at a time when talk of the Rosicrucians was so prevalent, he likely did not want to have spent these **leisure hours** <note>original: "horae subsecivae"; Latin for "extra hours" or "odd moments."</note> in vain.

(42) **Bacon** <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), an English philosopher and statesman.</note> speaks in his ***Atlantis*** <note>Referring to "New Atlantis," Bacon’s unfinished utopian novel.</note> explicitly of an **Order** or **Society**; **Solomon’s House** is a purely figurative expression. He says: *You shall understand, that amongst the excellent acts of that King* (namely the King on the fictional island of **Bensalem**) *one above all hath the preeminence. It was the Erection and Institution of an order or society, which we call **Salomon’s house**; — It is dedicated to the study of the Works and creatures of God. — The end of our foundation is the Knowledge of causes and secret motions of things and the enlarging of the bounds of human empire, to the effecting of all things possible.* See **Bacon’s** *Works*, Mallet’s Edition Vol. III.

(48) It is therefore said in the history of **Freemasonry** <note>original: "F. M."</note> in the *Freemason’s Calendar* of the year 1778: At that time, the lodges were very poorly attended. (49) This Masonic symbol is translated very clumsily in most German Masonic writings, perhaps through a slavish reproduction of the equally clumsy French translation: *the serrated tassel* <note>original: "la houpe dentelée"</note>. In English, this image is called *the indented tassel* in *Masonry Dissected*, one of the oldest printed books on **Freemasonry**. *Indented*, of course, also means notched or toothed, and *tassel* means a decorative tuft. However, *indented* also means intertwined or knotted, and *tassel* can refer to a thread or a cord—something one can hold onto. (In French, *tasseau* <note>original: "tasseau"</note> refers to a bracket or cleat that holds two boards together.) *Indenture* <term>indenture: a legal contract or agreement</term> means a contract or a connection in English, because in the Middle Ages it was customary to make identical notches in two copies of a document after placing them on top of one another to attest to their authenticity, much like our **tally sticks** <note>original: "Kerbhölzer"; wooden sticks used to record debts or agreements by making matching notches</note>. (See *Du Cange’s Glossary of Medieval Latin* <note>original: "Dufresne Glossarium lat. med. aev."; referring to Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange</note>, under the entries for *Indenture* <note>original: "Indentura"</note> and *Indented Charters* <note>original: "Indentatae Chartae"</note>.) Therefore, *the indented tassel* must be translated as the **intertwined cord**. The image is, in fact, an intertwined cord with tassels at both ends. In **Thomas Treter’s** *Symbolic Delineation of the Christian Life* <note>original: "Thomae Treteri symbolica vitae christianae delineatio"</note> (**Braunsberg**, 1612, quarto), this same image is found on page 139 as a symbol for **Obedience** <note>original: "Obedientia"</note>. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the symbolic origins of Freemasonry, specifically analyzing the visual representation of the two pillars (Jachin and Boaz) and their transition from Rosicrucian mysticism into Masonic ritual. *Masonry both Ancient and Modern* <note>original: "masonry both ancient and modern"</note> (London 1800, large octavo) depicts a celestial and a terrestrial globe on the columns J and B <note>Referring to Jachin and Boaz, the two bronze pillars of Solomon's Temple</note> (as is also common in a certain foreign system). Regarding these, it says specifically: *The Terrestrial and Celestial Globes, representing the Works of Creation* (see above, the 42nd note, page 83). These globes meant the same thing to the **Rosicrucians**; thus, in the illustrations of the *Mysteries of the High Degrees* <note>original French: "mystères des Hauts Grades"</note> and in *Jachin and Boaz*, one finds allegorical signs of their shared descent and their differences. Much of it may well be taken from the Talmud, where all sorts of miraculous stories are told about Solomon’s Temple. The old Rosicrucians, as well as the more recent Rosicrucian Freemasons, imagined they could find great secrets in Hebrew and Rabbinic tales. Michaelis <note>Johann David Michaelis (1717–1791), a prominent German biblical scholar</note> says (regarding 1 Kings 7:26) that the Jews even assumed there were globes within the columns.

Of course, the first *patriotic* lodge does not have to be the first lodge *overall*! But if Mr. B. had looked up Semler, he would have found that Semler held or expressed no opinion on the matter at all, but merely had the letter from an anonymous person printed; thus, he could be neither wrong nor right.

<meta>This page continues the author’s line-by-line rebuttal of Johann Gottlieb Buhle’s history of Freemasonry. ...well know that the police in **London** were informed of a **Freemason lodge** at that time? Mr. B. <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821), a German philosopher whose works on the origin of Freemasons were highly controversial for their lack of primary evidence</note> issues such arbitrary fabrications as truths with a boldness matched only by his statement on page 283:

(54) I must provide an example here to show that Mr. von Murr—whom Mr. Buhle so often boldly copies—is not only far from historically accurate, but that he also knowingly (provided he understands Latin) seeks to throw dust in the eyes of uninformed readers. He relates on page 75 of his book:

And then he adds for his own part: "This is entirely **Rosicrucian**." The proof he provides is as follows:

> "On the title page of **Maier's** *Philosophical Week* <note>original: "Majers Septimana philosophica," published in 1620 by Michael Maier, a famous alchemist and defender of the Rosicrucians</note>, **King Solomon** is seated, and, as a gallant gentleman, he has the **Queen of Sheba** sitting to his right and **Hiram** to his left. Beside and behind them sit **Rosicrucians** at their writing desks, and in the preface, **Maier** says: **Solomon shared riddles with the Queen of Sheba and Hiram, the Prince of Tyre.** <note>original: "Salomonem aenigmata cum Regina Sabae ac Hiramo Tyrio Principe communicaffe."</note>" 

Thus, riddles are being exchanged there between **Solomon**, the **Queen of Sheba**, and the King of **Tyre**, **Hiram**. It truly seems that the name **Hiram** is destined to confuse all minds and all history! For King **Hiram** is certainly not the same person as...

Furthermore: when a king and a queen of the ancient world <note>Likely referring to King Solomon and the Queen of Sheba, who in biblical and apocryphal tradition exchanged riddles to test each other's wisdom</note> pose riddles to a third party—whose wisdom they admired—regarding physics and chemistry, there is not even a mention of the construction of Solomon's Temple. Such groundless assertions are only too deceptive. The majority of readers do not have the true nature of the matter in mind; they do not know, or do not pay attention to the fact, that King Hiram <note>Hiram I of Tyre, the monarch who sent materials and men to Solomon</note> and Hiram the Coppersmith <note>Hiram Abiff, the master artisan described in 1 Kings 7, who became the central allegorical figure in the Masonic Master Mason degree</note> are as distinct as Alexander the Great and Alexander the Smith. The bold exclamation <note>original: "Epiphonem"; a rhetorical term for a striking concluding remark</note>: "that is entirely Rosicrucian," sticks in the memory and is repeated, because one does not believe someone would assert something directly contrary to the truth. Writers such as Mr. von Murr and Mr. Buhle, and those like them, rely on this.

The latter also had all his writings printed in Frankfurt and Oppenheim. What other Englishman at that time would have had his books published on poor German printing paper, as is the case with all of Fludd's writings? — Furthermore, Michael Maier himself says that he only became acquainted with the Rosicrucian Society through the *Fama* <note>Referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614), the first anonymous Rosicrucian manifesto</note>, although he claims this happened first in England—which, however, is not very likely due to various circumstances.

> “...you published the Brothers of the Rosy Cross <note>original Latin: "fratres Rosaceae Crucis edidisti." > “Now for the first time translated from the Italian (in which it was only written and kept hidden by a few) into our German language.”
> <note>original: „Nun erstlich aus dem Italienischen (darin es vnd zwar allein geschrieben, bey etlich Wenigen verborgen geweßt) in Vnser teutsche Sprach übersezt.“</note>

There was certainly no printed edition of the Latin translation before 1640. This same claim is made in the preface to the fourth edition (Frankfurt an der Oder, 1686) regarding all three previous ones. From this, it can be inferred that this treatise must have circulated in manuscript form for a long time, which gave rise to the many errors.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the publication history of Tommaso Campanella’s works, specifically focusing on how his manuscripts were circulated and edited by scholars like Caspar Schoppe before reaching formal printing houses like the Elzevirs. “...you published the brothers of the **Rose Cross**, and by all Germans, who published it for that reason in their own vernacular language, although it reached almost everyone's hands in Italian and Latin from the library of **Gaspar Schoppe** <note>original: Gasparis Schioppii. Without doubt, he and Schoppe received **Campanella’s** <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639) was an Italian Dominican friar, philosopher, and poet best known for his utopian work *The City of the Sun*. He spent much of his life imprisoned by the Spanish Inquisition.</note> book in manuscript and shared it with others in the same way; for the greatest bibliophiles have never seen a printed Italian edition of this book and doubt that one exists. It also states on the title page of the German translation of Campanella's *Spanish Monarchy*: “Now first translated from the Italian (in which it was only written and kept hidden among a few) into our German language.” 

Certainly no edition of the Latin translation was printed before 1640. **Ludwig Elzevir** <note>Also known as Lodewijk Elzevir, a member of the famous Dutch printing family known for high-quality scholarship and portable book formats.</note> says in the preface to his aforementioned first edition of 1640: “which treatise we now publish with this aim, so that it may be read by many.” <note>original Latin: „quem tractatum hoc fine nunc publicamus, ut a pluribus legi possit.“</note> 

In the preliminary report to his second edition of 1641 (actually the third, for the second was likewise printed at Harderwijk in 1640), this same printer says that in this edition of his, almost no line remained unchanged because the first two were so full of errors. This very same thing is said of all three previous editions in the preface to the fourth edition (Frankfurt an der Oder, 1686). From this, it can be inferred that this treatise must have circulated in manuscript form for a long time, which gave rise to the many errors.
