<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dissemination of Tommaso Campanella’s works, specifically focusing on the critical reception and misunderstandings by Gabriel Naudé regarding the Rosicrucian movement. It then transitions to the political climate of the Austrian Protestants during the early 17th century.</meta>

...cited passage against the **Rosicrucians** <note>The Rosicrucians (Rosenkreuzer) were a mysterious spiritual and cultural movement that emerged in early 17th-century Germany, claiming to possess secret wisdom.</note> (as he says quaintly enough: "this passage from a German book that Campanella composed" <note>original: „ce passage d'un livre allemand que Campanel-la a composé“</note>) printed in French. But it is also obvious that **Naudé** <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653) was a French librarian and scholar who wrote a famous defense of historical figures accused of magic.</note> did not know the book at all, since he did not understand German, and that this passage was therefore intentionally sent to him by someone else immediately after the appearance of the German appendix. In general, it can be seen from Naudé’s rambling book that he knew nothing more about the Rosicrucians than through an extract that someone must have made for him from the *Fama* and *Confessio* <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis* and *Confessio Fraternitatis* were the primary manifestos of the Rosicrucian order, published in 1614 and 1615.</note>; for he nowhere mentions that a French translation existed. Furthermore, he knows no other Rosicrucian writings. Because he considers the Rosicrucians to be merely **gold-makers** <term>Alchemists (Goldmacher)</term> and magical fraudsters, he warns against them with the best of intentions. It does not occur to him at all, however, that there could be Rosicrucians who hid other intentions under the visionary exterior. If only the good man had thought of the second chapter of his fine book, *Apology for all the Great Personages who have been falsely suspected of Magic* <note>original: Apologie des grands hommes soupçonnés de Magie</note>, in which he demonstrates: that many great figures have been considered Magicians, who were only Politicians! <note>original: que beaucoup de grands personnages ont été estimez Magiciens, qui n'étoient que Politiques!</note> Then he might have found another side to some of the Rosicrucians.

(65) The Austrian Protestants were at that time in actual revolt due to religious oppression, which they called the **Work of Defense** <term>Defensionswerk</term>. The **Estates above the Enns** <note>The "Stände ob der Ens" refers to the regional assembly of Upper Austria.</note> refused to pay homage to **Emperor Ferdinand II** until he had granted their demands, which mainly concerned the freedom to persist in the **Evangelical confession** <note>The Protestant/Lutheran faith.</note>, and they had established an interim government. In the years 1619 and 1620, several small pamphlets related to this were published: for example, *Resolution on whether and to what extent the Estates of an Empire and Kingdom are permitted by law to defend themselves and their subjects in religious matters...*

...oppressions against their highest authority [to be] <term>defended by force of arms</term> <note>original: armata manu to defendiren</note> 1620. 4. <meta>This page continues the author's critique of the uncritical acceptance of Masonic myths. He specifically targets a 1790 publication and begins a detailed historical explanation of why medieval stonemasons used secret signs—arguing these were practical trade tools rather than evidence of a mystical secret society.</meta>

...published a book in the year 1790 under the title: *The Raised Curtain of Freemasonry, by Means of the Only True History of the Same* <note>original: "Der aufgezogene Vorhang der Freymaurerey." This was one of many "exposé" style books common in the late 18th century that claimed to reveal the secret history of the craft.</note>, (behind which raised curtain, however, little more than another, not very transparent curtain is to be seen), also accepts this alleged document <note>This refers to the purported Henry VI manuscript mentioned on the previous page.</note> as definitively correct, as do almost all the legends in **Freemason** books. I shall provide a new example of the gullibility of both writers in a subsequent note.

(71) It should be noted that in England, the **guild** <term>Innung: A professional association or guild of craftsmen that regulated their trade.</term> of masons and the trade of **stonecutters** constitute only a single guild. A stonecutter is called both *Stonecutter* and *Mason* in English. The workshop where they work is called a *Lodge* in English. In Germany, in places where the **stonecutters** are separate from the masons, it is their custom to have a foreign **journeyman** <note>A "Geselle" (journeyman) was a craftsman who had completed his apprenticeship but was not yet a master; they frequently traveled to find work.</note> who arrives from elsewhere perform the customary professional greeting in the "Hütte" <note>Hütte: The German term for a mason's lodge or workshop.</note>—where they partly work and partly keep their tools. During this, a couple of journeymen guard the door from the outside to receive the credentials and the signs by which they recognize the traveling journeymen of their trade. No reasonable person, however, would likely take this as proof that they were **Freemasons** (no more so than other guilds that also have such things or something similar). It is understandable that in the Middle Ages, when the **guilds** were established, masonry and stonecutting work occurred in only a few places, and thus the journeymen often had to travel far before they found work. Naturally, it was important that whoever was employed on a building project had learned the craft properly, so that the mistakes of unskilled workers would not harm the whole structure as the building rose higher. At a time when few people were literate, one recognized each other primarily through physical signs and by

These Greeting-masons currently stand under three German Chief Lodges and claim for themselves a kinship with the **Freemasons**." In Section 9 of the aforementioned Imperial Constitution, the distinction in the mason's trade "between Greeters and Letter-bearers is completely abolished, suppressed, and forbidden." Thus, this distinction can no longer legally exist, and least of all can anything regarding Freemasonry be inferred from it. The matter is as follows, as I know from very reliable inquiries. <meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the distinctions between different branches of the masonry trade. It clarifies the technical and social boundaries between "stonemasons" (Steinmetzen) and "masons" (Maurer), while debunking claims about their connection to speculative Freemasonry.</meta>

...[The distinction between] **stonemasons** and **masons** has always consisted in the fact that stonemasons work only with mallet and chisel, while masons are permitted to work with both hammer and trowel (i.e., by setting stones in lime mortar) as well as with mallet and chisel. Specifically, masons also dress the stones which they need to be square, whereas the cutting-plan <term>Schnittriß: A technical drawing or template used in stereotomy to determine the exact geometry for cutting stones.</term> (the *stone-cutting* <note>original French: "Coupe de pierres"</note>) for vaults and so on remains the domain of the **stonemasons**. Therefore, especially in those lands where buildings are constructed only from rubble stone, the masons also call themselves "stone-hewers" <note>original: "Steinhauer"</note>, a designation which is still common today in Franconia, Swabia, and Switzerland.

In some places, these stone-hewers consider themselves somewhat superior to the other masons, despite the fact that they constitute the very same trade. Thus, in some locations, the stone-hewers align themselves with the stonemasons. Consequently, there was formerly a common distinction in the manner in which journeymen traveled and identified themselves <note>This refers to the "wandering years" of a journeyman and the secret signs used to prove their status to distant lodges.</note>, a distinction which has completely disappeared since the year 1731 <note>The Imperial Trades Decree of 1731 attempted to standardize guild practices and suppress secret "brotherhood" rituals across the Holy Roman Empire.</note>; for in all places, the arriving journeyman masons—whether they are stone-hewers or not—bring certificates <term>Kundschaften: Formal written testimonials or identity papers issued by a guild to a traveling journeyman.</term> from the places where they have worked.

Old craftsmen often harbor pretensions about their own trade that have long since ceased to be true, and they maintain misguided notions, or perhaps even wish to mislead anyone who inquires about their craft. This was the case with the old master mason who told a tall tale to the author of the *Letters on Freemasonry*: "the Freemasons were certainly the wealthiest among the masons, but they nonetheless strove for the 'word' of the Greeting Masons, just as the Greeting Masons strove for the 'word' of the Freemasons." Yet the majority of **Freemasons** do not even know if "Greeting Masons" <term>Grußmaurer: A term for operative masons who identified themselves through traditional trade greetings and signs rather than speculative rituals.</term> ever existed in the world, let alone that they should be covetous of their secret words.

(74) Both the author of the *Letters* [concerning] **Freemasonry**...

<meta>This page continues the author’s critical examination of Freemasonry's historical claims. ...strikingly, as the author of *The Unveiled Curtain of Freemasonry* <note>original: "aufgezogenen Vorhangs der Freymaurerey," an anonymous work published around 1789 that aimed to reveal the secrets of the order.</note>, many busy themselves with the letter mentioned in the text, which Dr. Knipe is said to have communicated to the author of the *Biographia Britannica* <note>A multi-volume biographical dictionary of notable British figures, first published in 1747.</note> (see there, page 740). I have always doubted that this letter was authentic. Nevertheless, in my *Attempt* <note>Referring to the author’s own earlier work, *Attempt at a History of Freemasonry* (Versuch einer Geschichte der Freymaurerei).</note> Part I, page 196 and Part II, page 196, I still spoke of it in an undecided manner, and contented myself with saying that what was cited therein—as having supposedly been said by Ashmole <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a famous antiquary and one of the earliest recorded "speculative" (non-working) Freemasons initiated in England.</note>—was merely a legend of the mason’s trade and did not concern Freemasonry. This was sufficient for my purpose at that time. But since then, I have examined the matter more closely, and clearly realized that what is attributed to Ashmole's account in this letter does not originate from him at all, but is a forgery <note>original: "untergeschoben," meaning surreptitiously substituted or falsely attributed.</note>. This letter is said to have been written in the year 1687 and taken from a book that is not named, just as the author and the recipient of the letter are not named. It is, however, obviously a hollow invention intended to propagate legends of Freemasonry; as is already shown by the fabricated report cited in the text on page 110 and following, concerning a [Papal] Bull which the Pope is said to have given to Italian builders in the Middle Ages to travel through all of Europe and build chapels <note>This refers to a popular myth in early Masonic histories claiming that medieval popes granted "Comacine Masters" or other guilds universal jurisdiction to build churches.</note>. The unnamed and unknown letter-writer pretends that he has this report “from manuscripts by Ashmole, in which highly valuable collections were located concerning the history of the Freemasons.” This, however, is certainly a mere empty pretense. In Ashmole’s diary, there is not the slightest trace that he had “made collections concerning the history of the Freemasons.” Dr. Plot <note>Dr. Robert Plot (1640–1696), an English naturalist and the first keeper of the Ashmolean Museum at Oxford.</note>, keeper of the Ashmolean Museum at Oxford around the year 1680, would certainly have mentioned something of these manuscripts if they existed; for if they were not present in the Ashmolean Museum...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of secret societies, specifically transitioning from the mystical influence of figures like Robert Fludd to the historical evidence of Freemasonry's origins in 17th-century England. The previous page ended with the word "mixed."</meta>

...ed, we also know well enough that <term>Fludd</term> <note>Robert Fludd (1574–1637) was an English physician and mystical philosopher who defended the Rosicrucians and blended science with occultism.</note>, who wrote so much mystical material, nevertheless also made known in his writings several physical and anatomical truths that were not entirely unimportant, especially for that time. Such was the spirit of those times. In general, people of very different ways of thinking can very well unite for a single purpose if it is important to them; indeed, it is a very fortunate coincidence when people who are otherwise burdened with gross prejudices come into contact with open-minded people. Sound reason develops only gradually, but one is on the best path toward it when one begins to combine reflection with experience through social effort and the exchange of thoughts.

2) I first proved the oldest public historical trace of <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>The author uses the archaic spelling "Freymaurerey."</note>, namely, that the well-known antiquarian <term>Elias Ashmole</term> <note>Ashmole (1617–1692) was a famous English politician and student of alchemy whose diary provides the earliest record of a non-stonemason being initiated into a lodge.</note>, who was also a zealous <term>Rosicrucian</term>, was initiated as a Freemason in the year 1646, specifically in Warrington in Lancashire (*4). Later, in London, the lodge was held in the Masons' guild hall. (See my *Essay*, Part I, pp. 191, 193, and Part II, p. 192.) This was a sign that the members of this society were now joining the guild of the Masons; for it is well known in England that anyone who wishes to have the rights of citizenship in London must join one of the <term>guilds</term> <note>original: "Zünften"; referring to the London Livery Companies, which controlled trade and city governance.</note>, without it being necessary to actually practice the business of that guild. Hence the name **Freemason** arose, for in the English language, **Free** refers to one who is a member of a guild <note>In this context, a "freeman" is someone who has been granted the "freedom of the city," allowing them to trade and participate in a guild.</note>. Another proof is that the Grand Lodge of Freemasons in London has the very same coat of arms as the Masons' guild there, and that every operative mason, when he is admitted as a Freemason, pays only half of the

<summary>The author traces the historical transition of Freemasonry from mystical alchemy to a formal society, citing Elias Ashmole's 1646 initiation as a key turning point. He explains the etymology of "Freemason" as deriving from the legal status of "freemen" within London's guild system.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the philosophical definitions of justice and social organization, transitioning from classical commentary into a historical survey of the expansion of Freemasonry and the origins of social "clubs" in Germany.</meta>

...received the name of justice; and because it is evident from all that has been said above how much it relies on the fullness of both its parts and its own separate existence, it is rightly called "full." <note>original: "justitiae nomen accepit; et quia ex supradictis omnibus apparet, quanta et partium suarum et seorsum sua plenitudine nitatur, jure plenus vocatur." This quote emphasizes the completeness of a just system.</note> See *Macrobius’s* <note>Macrobius was a 5th-century Roman provincial who wrote a famous commentary on Cicero’s Dream of Scipio.</note> *Commentary on the Dream of Scipio*, Book 1, Chapter 5. Edited by Zeunius (Leipzig, 1774), p. 27.

(78) See *Macrobius*, as cited above, Book 1, Chapter 6, p. 36 following.

(79) See *Correspondence of Sam. Richardson* in six volumes. London 1804, octavo. In the *Life* of the same, p. XLIII.

(80) Shortly before this, <term>Freemasonry</term> began to spread outside of England. In the year 1725, the Grand Lodge in England established the first lodge in Paris. In the year 1727, it sent a delegation to Madrid to establish a lodge there.

(81) Regarding the fact that, according to the 2nd Book of Samuel, Chapter 13, Verse 27, Absalom invited his brother Amnon to a feast, <term>Geiler von Kaisersberg</term> <note>Johann Geiler von Kaisersberg (1445–1510) was a renowned priest and one of the most influential preachers of the late Middle Ages in Strasbourg.</note> says in his *Spiritual Spinner*, p. 7:

> "Once Absalom organized a *Kolben* <term>Kolben</term> <note>Literally "mace" or "club"; historically used to denote a social drinking society or an organized gathering.</note> or a
> tavern gathering and invited his brother Amnon to it."

See *Scherz’s Germanic Glossary of the Middle Ages*, edited by Oberlin, under the entry *Kolben*. Frisch also mentions in his dictionary a society of noble persons from the 14th century who were called "at the Club" (*zu dem Kolben*). It is very likely that this was a society that met at certain times in a specific place, just like a modern club.

(82) The Council of Strasbourg issued a police edict in the year 1556 against the wastefulness of citizens in taverns; it states:

> "that henceforth no host or inn-
> keeper, nor any guild-hall master in this city
> and its jurisdiction, nor in Ruprechtsau, Schiltigheim,
> Illkirch, or at St. Oswald, shall accept or maintain
> any newly started 'Kingdom' <term>Kingdom</term> <note>In this context, *Kuinigreich* (Kingdoms) refers to "Kingdoms of Fools" or mock-societies, which were festive associations often banned for their rowdy behavior.</note>
> or *Kolben*, or similar established societies in
> their houses or guild rooms, nor shall they assist those
> who carry out such kingdoms, clubs, or societies."

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the historical terminology of social organizations, specifically focusing on the origins of the "Kolben" (clubs) and "Kingdoms" in Strasbourg, following the mention of Geiler von Kaisersberg's use of the term.</meta>

> "...wanting to be present or with them, they should neither eat nor drink—
> nor prepare or provide [food or drink]."

(Mr. Oberlin cites this in his *Scherzian Glossary* <note>Jeremias Jakob Oberlin (1735–1806) was a scholar from Strasbourg who edited the *Glossarium Germanicum Medii Aevi*, a dictionary of medieval German compiled by Johann Georg Scherz.</note> under the entry **Kolben**.) One sees here that the general concept of the **Kingdom** or **Kolben** <term>Kolben</term> <note>Literally meaning "mace" or "club," this term was used in the Middle Ages to refer to private social societies or drinking clubs.</note> was that they were established societies—that is, those which always consisted of the same members and had agreed to meet at specific times and in certain places. Innkeepers were not supposed to serve food or drink to such closed societies; for one could not, after all, forbid innkeepers from serving food and drink to any other group that happened to gather by chance. To explain why such a closed society was called an "established **Kingdom**," one must remember that in former times, the word *Anfänger* <note>In modern German, this means "beginner," but the author notes its archaic meaning of "originator" or "leader."</note> also meant a leader, as found in the works of Frisch and Henisch <note>Johann Leonhard Frisch and Georg Henisch were prominent 16th and 17th-century German lexicographers.</note>. In England, every club elects a president who regulates all the club's affairs. The closed societies of Strasbourg likely also elected such a leader among themselves, whom they jokingly called "King" (as the Greeks and Romans already did at drinking parties), from which the society itself received the name **Kingdom**.

(83) Frisch cites the following regarding the "Society of the Kolben": *Serarius, History of Mainz, Volume II, p. 706.* <note>original: "Serar. Rer. Mogunt. Tom. II, p. 706." Nicolaus Serarius was a Jesuit historian.</note> In this citation, there is a printing error, and it is unclear. Serarius's book does not have a Volume II, but rather consists of five books. The first edition of it dates from 1604. However, it was reprinted in *Joannis's History of Mainz, Volume 1 (Frankfurt, 1722, folio edition)*. <note>original: "Joannis Rerum Moguntiacarum Vol. 1."</note> In this second edition, the cited passage appears on page 706. However, it is not found there within Serarius’s own text, but in a note to Section II of the *Life of Archbishop Conrad II*, in Serarius’s fifth book.

<summary>This page explores the linguistic and historical origins of "Kingdoms" and "Kolben" (clubs) in Strasbourg, explaining that these were exclusive social societies led by a mock "King." The author also corrects a scholarly citation error regarding the history of Mainz.</summary>

<keywords>Strasbourg, Kolben, Kingdom, social clubs, Oberlin, Serarius, Mainz, etymology, leadership, drinking societies</keywords>

(84) Wood, already in the first edition of his *Athenae Oxonienses* <note>original: "Athenae Oxonienses" (Athenians of Oxford). (85) One should see the book by the Spanish Jesuit Francisco Ribera: *On the Temple of Jerusalem and those things which pertain to the Temple*. <note>original: "De templo hierosolymitano et iis quae ad templum pertinent."</note> In the second edition of 1623, it says on page 58:

The first edition of this book was printed in Salamanca in 1590. One should further see the book by the Jesuit Juan de Pineda, *Solomon the Precursor, or on the affairs of Solomon*, <note>original: "Salomon praevius s. de rebus Salomonis."</note> Mainz, 1613, in folio. There, one finds several testimonies from the Church Fathers and other writers who represent the Solomonic Temple as the symbol of the Church; indeed, even verses from the Bible are drawn toward this meaning.

(86) So that one may see that Magister Gregorius Albrecht is second neither to Robert Fludd nor to Mr. Hofrath Buhle <note>Johann Gottlieb Buhle (1763–1821) was a German philosopher who argued that Freemasonry originated from the Rosicrucianism of Robert Fludd.</note> in his clever schematic applications, I wish to share the following quite fine passage from him (p. 106) with my readers:
> “People build common houses and dwellings for men. In doing so, a man should remember the condition of his own body, for it contains itself quite neatly, just like a house. Only pay attention to it, and you will marvel at how you always carry your house around with you on your body, like a snail. The highest peak of the house is the human head, which he holds aloft. The windows are the eyes. The ears are roof-openings, which should be open so that the wind—the Holy Spirit—may blow in, and they may hear what the Spirit says to the...”

> “wanting to be part of it or with them, neither eating nor drinking —

(Mr. Oberlin <note>Johann Jakob Oberlin (1735–1806), an Alsatian philologist and archaeologist.</note> includes this in his *Glossary of Scherz* <note>Referring to the *Glossarium Germanicum Medii Aevi*, a dictionary of medieval German compiled by Johann Jakob Scherz and edited by Oberlin.</note> under the entry for **Kolben** <note>The "Society of the Mace" (*Gesellschaft zum Kolben*) was an elite social organization in Strasbourg.</note>.) One sees here that the general concept of the <term>Königreich</term> <note>Literally "Kingdom," but here used to denote a private social club or organized society.</note> or **Kolben** was that they were established societies—that is, those which always consisted of the same members and had agreed to meet at specific times and in certain places. To explain why such a closed society was called an "established Kingdom," one must remember that "Beginner" <note>original: "Anfänger"</note> formerly also signified a "Leader," as can be found in the works of Frisch and Henisch <note>Johann Leonhard Frisch and Georg Henisch were influential 17th and 18th-century German lexicographers.</note>. In England, every club chooses a President who regulates all the club's affairs. It is probable that the closed societies of Strasbourg also chose such a leader from among themselves, whom they jokingly called **King** (just as the Greeks and Romans did at drinking parties), from which the society itself received the name **Kingdom**.
